The classification of simple complex Lie superalgebras of polynomial vector fields and their deformations
Dimitry Leites a,∗ , Irina Shchepochkina b

Abstract
. We overview classifications of simple infinite-dimensional complex ℤ \mathbb{Z} -graded Lie (super)algebras of polynomial growth, and their deformations. A subset of such Lie (super)algebras consist of vectorial Lie (super)algebras whose elements are vector fields with polynomial, or formal power series, or divided power coefficients. A given vectorial Lie (super)algebra with a (Weisfeiler) filtration corresponding to a maximal subalgebra of finite codimension is called W-filtered; the associated graded algebra is called W-graded. Here, we correct several published results: (1) prove our old claim “the superization of É. Cartan’s problem (classify primitive Lie algebras) is wild”, (2) solve a tame problem: classify simple W-graded and W-filtered vectorial Lie superalgebras, (3) describe the supervariety of deformation parameters for the serial W-graded simple vectorial superalgebras, (4) conjecture that the exceptional simple vectorial superalgebras are rigid. We conjecture usefulness of our method in classification of simple infinite-dimensional vectorial Lie (super)algebras over fields of positive characteristic.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 17B20, 17B66; Secondary 58A30

In memory of A. N. Tyurin.

§1.7.1

1.
Setting of the problem. Steps of our classification. Our results

Several times at least one of the classification problems we consider here was claimed to be solved, starting with
[
K2
]
.
We analyse these
claims, correcting them when needed, and complete the results and claims announced in

[
LSh2
,
LSh2′
]
and preprinted in
[
LSh1
,
LSh5
]
with a description of
deformations, in particular, with odd parameters, see also
[
Ld
]
. Observe that to shy away from some of odd parameters is contrary to the very
spirit of supersymmetry.
1
1

1

An interesting question: why all people
can easily accept extensions with odd center, such as the extension of
{\mathfrak{le}}(n)
to
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)
, but some can not accept
odd parameters of deformations or representations?…
When the deformation parameters are even, we describe
deforms
— the

results
of deformations.
2
2

2

A term coined by M. Gerstenhaber, in the same manner as
transform
is the result of
a transformation, or,
e.g.
, history is an ideological
construct
(as becomes more and more evident). We like these terms and suggest the term
prolong
for the result of Cartan prolongation.
Hereafter, we abbreviate “Lie algebra or Lie superalgebra” to “Lie (super)algebra”.

In this paper, the ground field is
{\mathbb{C}}
unless otherwise specified, the term
“
vectorial
Lie (super)algebra” is short for “Lie
(super)algebra of vector fields with polynomial coefficients”, or with formal-power-series coefficients if a topological completeness is needed.
The adjective “vectorial” reflects the
fact that such Lie superalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}
is realized by
vector fields on the
linear supermanifold
,
i.e.
, a ringed space, corresponding (as described in formula (
29
); for details, see
[
KLLS
,
Ld
]
) to the

superspace
,
i.e.
, a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded space,
({\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0})^{*}
, where
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{L}}
is
a maximal Lie subsuperalgebra of finite codimension. Let
\dim{\mathcal{L}}=\infty
unless otherwise stated.

1.1.
Weisfeiler filtrations and gradings

Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
be a maximal subalgebra of finite codimension, let
{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
be a minimal
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
-invariant subspace strictly
containing
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
; for
i\geq 1
, set:

(1)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathcal{L}}_{-i-1}=[{\mathcal{L}}_{-1},{\mathcal{L}}_{-i}]+{\mathcal{L}}_{-i},\\
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}=\{D\in{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}\mid[D,{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}]\subset{\mathcal{L}}_{i-1}\}.\end{array}

We thus get a filtration, called a
Weisfeiler filtration
, see
[
W
]
:

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{-d}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{-d+1}\supset\dots\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\supset\cdots

The number
d
in (
2
) is called the
depth
of
{\mathcal{L}}
, as well as
of the associated
Weisfeiler-graded
Lie superalgebra

(3)

L=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{-d\leq i}L_{i}
, where

L_{i}={\mathcal{L}}_{i}/{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}
.

Any Lie superalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}

together with a Weisfeiler
filtration
will be briefly called
W-filtered
; the graded algebra
L

associated with
{\mathcal{L}}
will be called
W-graded
.

For vectorial Lie
(super)algebras, the notion invariant with respect to automorphisms is that of filtration, not grading, but graded (super)algebras are easier to work with.

1.2.
A wider picture. Main Problem

The

growth
of a

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra
A=\oplus A_{i}
is defined, if
\dim A_{i}<\infty
for all
i
, to be

\text{gth}(A)=\mathop{\overline{\lim}}\limits_{n\longrightarrow\infty}\;\frac{\ln\ \dim\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{|i|\leq n}A_{i}}{\ln n}.

A
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra
A
is said to be of
polynomial growth
if

\text{gth}(A)<\infty
,
i.e.
,

(4)

\dim\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{|i|\leq n}A_{i}\sim n^{\text{gth}(A)}
as
n\longrightarrow\infty
.

A
filtered
Lie (super)algebra
\cdots\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{i}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}\supset\cdots
is said to be of
polynomial growth
if the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie (super)algebra
L=\oplus L_{i}
, where
L_{i}:={\mathcal{L}}_{i}/{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}
, associated with
{\mathcal{L}}
is of polynomial growth. Vectorial Lie (super)algebras constitute a subset in the set of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded (or filtered) Lie (super)algebras of polynomial growth (GLAPG for short), see eq. (
4
), so to place vectorial Lie (super)algebras in a natural wider picture we also review, briefly, the results and
Open problems
in the classification of simple Lie
(super)algebras of GLAPG type such as the following
partly classified
types 1)–4) and their “
relatives
”, see Subsection
1.2.1
:

1) Lie (super)algebras of vector fields with Laurent polynomials (or series when a topological completeness is needed) as coefficients (a.k.a.
stringy
Lie (super)algebras), their central extensions, see
[
GLS1
,
KvdL
]
, and algebras of their derivations, see
[
Po2
, Th.4.1]
;

2)
loop
(super)algebras of Laurent polynomials (or series) with values in a simple Lie (super)algebra, their “twisted” versions, see
[
LSS
,
LS1
,
ShS
]
, and their non-simple “relatives” —
double extensions
, see
[
BLS
]
, especially the last two sections in it, and
[
CCLL
,
HS
]
;

3) Lie (super)algebras of “matrices of complex size” type, see
[
Lq
]
;

4) Lie queerifications of the simple associative (super)algebras, see
[
Lq
]
.

In the
proof
of a tame problem of classification of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}
,
we consider maximal subsuperalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
of only
finite

codimension, and related
Weisfeiler filtrations
, see formulas
(
1
). However, filtrations with

\dim({\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0})=\infty
are also useful, see Subsection

2.17
.

We survey the current state of the classification of simple Lie
(super)algebras of polynomial growth, not only vectorial ones, and, briefly,
several related topics (continuous irreducible representations
of vectorial Lie (super)algebras (
[
GLS3
]
), a successful attempt to see the exceptional finite-dimensional Lie algebras in a series on Vogel’s plane, see
[
AM
]
, etc.).

Properties of Lie
super
algebras (even over fields of characteristic 0) resemble those of Lie algebras over fields
{\mathbb{K}}
of positive characteristic, a.k.a.
modular
Lie algebras. In the 1960s, Kostrikin and Shafarevich
suggested a method for constructing simple finite-dimensional Lie
algebras over algebraically closed fields
{\mathbb{K}}
, and conjectured that their method produced all simple finite-dimensional Lie algebras for

\mathop{\text{\rm char}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}:=p>7
, provided they are
restricted
(for details and update, see
[
BLLS1
,
BGLLS
]
).

This KSh-conjecture is now proved, in a
generalized form,
i.e.
, for any, not only restricted, simple Lie algebras, for
p>3
, see
[
S
,
BGP
,
Sk
]
. The result was partly implicit until recently: the explicit formulas of the brackets of the deformed Hamiltonian Lie algebras can be found at the moment only in the Ph.D. thesis of S. Kirillov in Russian, see
[
Kir
]
based on
[
Sk
]
. Here we provide with notions and correct formulas helping one to formulate conjectural lists of all
simple finite-dimensional Lie algebras over algebraically closed
fields of characteristic
p=2
and
3
, and Lie superalgebras for
p>5
,
see
[
BLLS1
,
BGLLS
]
and references therein.

1.2.1.
A broader goal.

Starting with a simple Lie
algebra of GLAPG type, list the results of iterated combinations
of the following operations and various gradings of these results:

a)
{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{e}}({\mathfrak{g}})
, the nontrivial central
extension of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Examples: central extensions of loop algebras; the
Poisson algebra.

b)
{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{der}}~({\mathfrak{g}})
, the derivation algebra of

{\mathfrak{g}}
, or a double extension of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Examples: affine Kac–Moody
algebras, see
[
BLS
]
.

c) Deformations of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. (Although they sometimes lead out of the GLAPG type, such deformed Lie algebras are often no less important in
applications than the original algebras. Important examples: the result of the
deformation of the algebra of Hamiltonian vector fields induced by
the
quantization
of the Poisson algebra. An example due to P. Golod (Holod in modern
Ukrainian)
[
GoHo1
,
GoHo2
]
evolved into the Krichever-Novikov theory of certain deformations
of loop algebras; for a review, see
[
Shei
]
and Open problem in Subsection
1.9
.)

d) All filtered completions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
introducing topology to the
originally algebraic problem.

e) Forms of the Lie algebras of the above types a)– d) over
non-closed fields.

We will call the algebras obtained from a given simple Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
by the above procedures a)–e), most frequently, by just a) – d),
relatives
of each
other, and of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

1.3.
Problems we solve here over
{\mathbb{C}}

Our results (
[
ALSh
]
, the key ingredients and ideas, see
[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
, and
[
LSh3
,
LSh5
]
announced in
[
LSh1
,
LSh2
,
LSh2′
]
), and
later papers
[
Kga
,
Kbj
,
FaKa
,
CK
,
CK1
,
CK1a
,
CK2
,
CCK
,
CaKa1
,
CaKa2
,
CaKa3
]
are devoted to exposition of
solutions of the following problems (cf.
[
Klc
,
K2
,
K3
]
):

(5)

1) Classify simple W-graded vectorial Lie
superalgebras.

2) Classify deformations of simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras.

There still remained points to be
clarified and corrected,
e.g.
,
see Section
11
. In particular, we recall the definition of Lie
superalgebras which shows how to interpret odd
parameters of deformation, see Subsection
2.2
and
[
Ld
]
.

Pivotal ideas of our classification of simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}:=\oplus_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
and their deformations
:

(6)

.
1) If
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}=\infty
, then among the operators in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
acting in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, there is a rank-
1
operator with an even covector, except for the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings of
{\mathfrak{g}}

compatible
3
3

3

Starting with
[
K1.5
]
, instead of “compatible” an inappropriate term “consistent” is sometimes used.
with the
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-grading by parity,
i.e.
, when
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}=\oplus\,{\mathfrak{g}}_{2i}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}=\oplus\,{\mathfrak{g}}_{2i+1}
. (The exceptional cases yield a
conjectural
part of our classification of W-gradings.)

.
2) Every simple vectorial Lie superalgebra has finitely many W-gradings.

.
3) Deformations of any Lie superalgebra are parameterized by a supervariety (degenerated to a point if the algebra is rigid).

Conjecture
. Our main method to obtain examples of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras —
search for irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
modules
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
with a rank-1 operator
— will be useful for obtaining new examples of simple W-graded
infinite-dimensional
vectorial Lie (super)algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
in characteristics
p=2
and
3
; we conjecture that this classification will be easier to obtain than the classification of
finite-dimensional
simple Lie (super)algebras, cf.
[
BGLLS
,
CSS
]
.

1.3.1.
Classification of simple vectorial Lie algebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
. Their deformations.

The well-known answer is as follows

(7)

There are 4 series of simple W-graded vectorial Lie
algebras
4
4

4

This part is due to É. Cartan, for an exposition, see

[
GQS
]
.
; they are rigid, see Section
9
, except for the Hamiltonian Lie
algebra
5
5

5

This part was a kind of folklore, known since
1940s or 1950s; for details, see
[
LSh3
,
KT
]
.

{\mathfrak{h}}(2n)
to which one can restrict the single non-trivial deformation
6
6

6

The physicists call this
{\mathcal{Q}}

quantization
, it leads both
{\mathfrak{po}}(2n)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}(2n)
out of the class of W-graded or W-filtered Lie algebras: the deform
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{po}}(2n))

.

{\mathcal{Q}}
of its central extension — the Poisson Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{po}}(2n)
.

1.4.
On setting of the classification problems

Selection of Lie
(super)algebras with reasonably nice properties is a matter of taste and is
influenced by the governing “meta”-problem. One of the usual
choices is classification of
simple
Lie (super)algebras: they have
a richer structure than other types of Lie (super)algebras, and therefore
are easier to study; they also illuminate important symmetries. Even
representatives of the “complementary” type — solvable Lie (super)algebras
— are often of significant interest only when considered as subalgebras of simple Lie (super)algebras.

\bullet
The
finite-dimensional
simple Lie algebras are naturally the first
to study. Note that this, ostensibly natural, choice is, however, unnatural in positive characteristic.
Conjecture
: For
p>0
, to classify
infinite-dimensional
simple vectorial Lie (super)algebras will be easier than to classify finite-dimensional Lie (super)algebras and our pivotal idea 1) in (
6
), will help.

Over
{\mathbb{C}}
, there are three “classical” series
(
{\mathfrak{sl}}
,
{\mathfrak{o}}
and
{\mathfrak{sp}}
) and five exceptional (ostensibly not belonging to series) algebras; all these
simple Lie algebras are neatly encoded by (normalized) Cartan
matrices which are symbolically represented by very simple graphs — Dynkin
diagrams, see
[
Bbk
,
OV
]
.

Killing and É. Cartan, who discovered all 5 exceptional simple Lie algebras, described them as preserving a
non-integrable distribution, see
[
Cart
,
Co
]
. (Actually, Killing and É. Cartan considered serial simple Lie algebras in these terms as well. Later, with the discovery of the powerful method of studying the structure of simple Lie algebras and their representations in terms of roots and weights, the realization of simple Lie algebras as algebras preserving a non-integrable distribution was abandoned and forgotten, although this approach can be fruitful in modern times as well, see
[
GL5
,
BGLLS
,
KLS
]
.)

For an interesting interpretation of the exceptional Lie algebras, see

[
El3
,
BE
]
, where the picture is made clearer by superizing it and
passing to fields of positive characteristic
(Elduque’s Super Magic Super Square). On breathtaking attempts, initiated by Vogel, to consider exceptional simple finite-dimensional complex Lie algebras as members of a series, see a review
[
AM
]
.

\bullet
Next on the agenda are simple infinite-dimensional

{\mathbb{Z}}
-
graded Lie algebras of polynomial growth
; briefly:
of GLAPG type
. Recall that a

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra
A=\oplus A_{i}
is said to be
simple
graded
algebra if it has no

graded
ideals,
i.e.
, no ideals
I=\oplus I_{i}
such that

I_{i}=I\cap A_{i}
. Note that a simple
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra can be nonsimple as an
abstract algebra:
e.g.
, loop algebras with values in a simple Lie
algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
are simple graded but not simple: indeed, the
collection of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued functions (loops)
that vanish at any non-empty set of points is an ideal.

In
[
K
]
, Kac classified infinite-dimensional

(8)

simple
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie algebras of GLAPG type

provided they are generated by elements of
degree
\pm 1
.

Among the simple graded Lie algebras of polynomial growth, only
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1):={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[x]
and
{\mathfrak{witt}}
or
{\mathfrak{vect}}^{L}(1):={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[x^{-1},x]
are not generated by elements of
degree
\pm
1 with respect to any grading. Kac conjectured that
these two examples, plus his list of Lie algebras (
8
),
exhaust all the simple Lie algebras of GLAPG type (we recall this list in Subsection

1.4.2
).

Twenty years after
[
K
]
has been published, O. Mathieu proved this conjecture in a string of
rather complicated papers culminating in
[
M
]
.

1.4.1.
Certain Lie algebras of infinite growth are also useful.

The reader should not think, as was customary circa 50
years ago, that all Lie (super)algebras of infinite growth are useless
or too difficult to study. For example, various versions of

{\mathfrak{gl}}(\infty)
and Lie (super)algebras studied by Borcherds,
Gritsenko and Nikulin, and Penkov with coauthors are, though huge,
of considerable interest; some of these (super)algebras are quite describable, together
with several types of their representations, see an approach in
[
Eg
]
and
[
CCLL
,
GN
,
PS
,
FPS
,
HP
]
.

1.4.2.
Types of simple Lie algebras of polynomial growth.

Let us qualitatively describe the simple Lie algebras of polynomial
growth to better visualize them. They break into the disjoint union
of the following types of which the types 1)–4) are
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded:

i)
Finite-dimensional
algebras (growth 0). Each of them has a symmetrizable Cartan matrix.

ii)
Loop algebras
, perhaps twisted (growth 1); more
important in applications are their nonsimple “relatives” called

affine Kac-Moody
algebras, cf.
[
Kb3
]
and Subsection

1.9
. Recently, these relatives were identified as
double extensions
;
for a review and succinct description of this notion, see
[
BLS
]
.

iii)
Vectorial algebras
,
i.e.
, Lie algebras of vector
fields
7
7

7

These algebras are sometimes called “algebras of Cartan type”, the term introduced in the 1960s when only 4 series of simple Lie algebras of vector fields were known and their deformations (results of quantization) were ignored. There are serial (pertaining to series) simple vectorial modular Lie (super)algebras (in any characteristic) and exceptions (from series) discovered from the 1970 till now, see
[
BGL
,
BGLLS
,
KLS
]
. Note that
{\mathfrak{svect}}_{\lambda}^{L}(1|2)
is the only stringy Lie superalgebra with Cartan matrix, see

[
GLS1
]
: a new super phenomenon.
with polynomial coefficients (growth is equal to the
number of indeterminates, which is
assumed to be finite
) or their completions with formal power series as coefficients.

iv) The
stringy algebra

{\mathfrak{vect}}^{L}(1):={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[t^{-1},t]
(the superscript
L
stands for Laurent). This class consists of one
algebra. This algebra and its completion
\overline{{\mathfrak{vect}}^{L}(1)}:={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[t^{-1}][[t]]
are often called
Witt algebra

and denoted
{\mathfrak{witt}}
in
honor of Witt who considered its analog over fields of prime
characteristic; physicists call it the
centerless Virasoro
algebra
because (the result of) its nontrivial central extension
(discovered by Fuchs and Gelfand) is called the

Virasoro algebra
,
{\mathfrak{vir}}
, in honor of Virasoro, who rediscovered the
corresponding central extension and was the first to demonstrate the importance of

{\mathfrak{vir}}
in physical models.

Remark
.

Strictly speaking, stringy algebras are also vectorial, but we
apply the term
vectorial
only to (super)algebras whose coefficients are

polynomials
or
formal series
or divided powers in the modular setting. The term “stringy algebra” comes from the lingo
of imaginative physicists who now play with the idea that the
elementary particle is not a point, but rather a slinky, a springy tiny
string, see
[
GSW
]
. The
term “stringy algebra” means
“pertaining to string theory”, and also mirrors the structure of
stringy Lie superalgebras as collections (direct sums) of several strings — the
modules over the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{witt}}
every stringy Lie superalgebra
contains. In our papers, starting with
[
FL
]
, “stringy” means either a “simple
infinite-dimensional vectorial superalgebra on a supermanifold whose
underlying manifold is a circle, or a “
relative
” (see
Subsection
1.2
) of such algebra”. In some works, stringy Lie superalgebras (not necessarily simple) are called “superconformal”; this is unjustified and inappropriate when applied to the central
extensions of simple stringy Lie superalgebras, as explained in

[
GLS1
]
.

v)
Filtered Lie (super)algebras of type GLAPG
are not
classified. The graded Lie algebras associated to certain such filtered Lie
algebras are not simple and have a huge radical; these filtered examples are
particular solutions to the Main Problem
1.2
, many of them cannot be
obtained as deformations of simple algebras of GLAPG type. Though there
are considerably many more simple filtered Lie algebras than simple

graded
ones, at the
moment we hope that it is possible to distill tame classification
problems. The examples known to us are:

(vA) The Poisson algebras of functions on the orbits in the coadjoint
representation of any simple finite-dimensional Lie algebra, the
multiparameter deformations of these Poisson algebras, and superizations of these constructions, as well as Lie (super)algebras
of matrices over rings of differential operators, cf.
[
GL2
,
LSe
,
DGS
,
Kon
]
.

(vB) The Lie superalgebras constructed by S. Montgomery (
[
My
]
), Vasiliev and Konstein-Tyutin,
Pinczon and Ushirobira, see
[
GL2
,
KV
,
PU
,
KT1
]
and references
therein; for the queerification of these Lie superalgebras, see
[
Lq
]
.

(vC) The current algebras and vectorial algebras on varieties
M
,
cf.
[
L7
]
; such examples are lately known for compact Riemann
surfaces
M
as
Krichever-Novikov algebras
, see
[
KN
,
Shei
]
.

The set of examples of simple Lie
super
algebras of type v) is much
wider than the set of the non-super examples; there are several super
versions of each of the non-super examples (vA)–(vC), see
[
Lq
]
.

Additional features: Cartan matrix and NIS

Some of the Lie (super)algebras of the above types i) – v) have certain properties which are sometimes overvalued and taken
for the definition of these Lie (super)algebras, as in

[
FSS
]
, complicating the nomenclature.

Each Lie algebra of type i) and each affine Kac-Moody algebra has
a Cartan matrix; at least some of the algebras of type 5) also have
a Cartan matrix albeit in a generalized sense due to Saveliev and
Vershik
[
SV
]
. For algebras of type i) and affine Kac-Moody
algebras, this matrix is always symmetrizable,y integer, and rather sparse so it
can be encoded by a simple graph (Dynkin diagram). Lie
algebras with a symmetrizable Cartan matrix and certain
algebras of type v) carry a non-degenerate invariant symmetric
bilinear form
--- NIS for short — a powerful tool for solving numerous problems (for examples, see the encyclopedia
[
DSB
]
).

It was, perhaps, Vinberg who coined the term
contragredient
for the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}\simeq({\mathfrak{g}}_{-i})^{*}
with respect to the maximal torus in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
;
e.g.
,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vir}}
or
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
. Lately, this term is sometimes (thoughtlessly)
used instead of “Lie (super)algebra with Cartan matrix”.

1.5.
The super version of the Main Problem
1.2
is open at the moment

Open is even its subproblem
:

(9)

\begin{array}[]{l}\textit{Classify simple ${\mathbb{Z}}$-graded Lie superalgebras of
polynomial growth}\\
\textit{and their deformations}.\end{array}

Let us describe what part of problem (
9
) is solved. The
above types i)–iv) of simple Lie algebras, and their properties,
become intermixed under superization as well as under passage to Lie
algebras over fields of positive characteristic. Nevertheless, there exists a very simple stratification: There are only two types of
Lie (super)algebras
— “symmetric” and “lopsided”:

(SY) The
symmetric Lie (super)algebras
are those for which

(10)

there is a root decomposition (related to a maximal diagonalizing
subalgebra, called in what follows a
maximal torus
) such
that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits\;{\mathfrak{g}}_{\alpha}=\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits\;{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\alpha}
for any root

\alpha
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits
is

superdimension
.

(LO) The
lopsided algebras
are those for which the condition (
10
)
does not hold.

\bullet

Finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras
. The simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras are classified in the “naive” setting; for a review together with the first results on the representation theory, see
[
K2
]
. The deformations of simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras, in particular with odd parameters, were recently classified as well, see
[
Ld
]
, thus completing the classification.

The Lie (super)algebras of type
SY
split into the following two subtypes:

SYCM
, the class of symmetric algebras
with Cartan matrix
;

a posteriori
we see that the Cartan matrices of the finite-dimensional simple Lie (super)algebras are all symmetrizable, and
hence each of these Lie (super)algebras has an
even NIS, see
[
BKLS
,
KLLS
]
or
[
KLLS1
]
.

SYoCM
, the class of symmetric algebras
without Cartan matrix

in the conventional sense (but, perhaps, having one in the sense of
Saveliev and Vershik,
[
SV
]
) but with a NIS that is either even or odd.

Some of simple Lie (super)algebras without Cartan matrix have relatives
with a Cartan matrix (
e.g.
, the loop (super)algebras
and related to them affine Kac-Moody (super)algebras, cf.
[
CCLL
,
BLS
]
).

\bullet

(Twisted) loop superalgebras
. Obviously,
any loop (super)algebra with values in any simple Lie (super)algebra is
simple as
graded algebra
(but not as abstract algebras, see Subsection
1.4
).

For any (simple, or a relative of a simple) finite-dimensional Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
, we consider spaces
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ell(1)}:={\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[t^{-1},t]
of “loops”,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued functions on the circle
S
that admit expansions into Laurent polynomials (or their completions, Laurent series); here
t=\exp(i\varphi)
, where
\varphi
is the angle parameter on
S
. We introduced the notation
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ell(1)}
to distinguish from
{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}:=[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}]
and to make the expression
({\mathfrak{h}}^{(1)}(0|n))^{\ell(1)}
, and similar expressions, meaningful, see
[
BLS
]
. In this paper, we consider loops only in this subsection, so we simply write
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}:=[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}]
.

Recall that if
\psi
is an order-
m
automorphism of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{k}}
, where
0\leq\bar{k};=k\pmod{m}\leq m-1
, are eigenspaces of
\psi
with eigenvalue
\exp(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2\pi k\sqrt{-1}}{m}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2\pi k\sqrt{-1}}{m}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2\pi k\sqrt{-1}}{m}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2\pi k\sqrt{-1}}{m}$}})
, then a “twisted” loop (super)algebra is

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi}^{\ell(m)}:=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{0\leq\bar{k}\leq m-1,\ j\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{k}}t^{k+mj}.

Serganova classified non-isomorphic loop superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ell(1)}:={\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[t^{-1},t]
for simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
, and twisted loops
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi}^{\ell(m)}
corresponding to order-
m
automorphisms
\psi
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, see
[
Se2
,
Se3
]
; some “relatives” of these simple algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\psi}^{\ell(m)}
have Cartan matrices classified in
[
HS
]
. Cantarini rediscovered some of these examples, the ones with compatible grading, see
[
Ca1
]
, obviously unaware, together with the referee of her paper, of Serganova’s results (at least, Serganova was not cited).

Like the finite-dimensional superalgebras, loop superalgebras can
also be symmetric or lopsided. For details in the non-super case, where only symmetric loop algebras exist, see
[
Kb3
]
.

For some finite-dimensional Lie
superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
with a Cartan matrix, there are twisted loop
superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ell(k)}_{\psi}
no relative of which has a
Cartan matrix; an opposite phenomenon also occurs: for some simple
Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
no relative of which has a Cartan matrix,
there is a relative of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\ell(k)}_{\psi}
with a Cartan matrix.

The double extension of every simple (twisted) loop algebra has a Cartan matrix corresponding to an extended Dynkin diagram. Unlike the non-super case, some of the loop superalgebras have no relative with Cartan matrix, and some have
a non-symmetrizable Cartan matrix.

An intrinsic characterization of loop superalgebras without appeal
to Cartan matrices
together with an intrinsic characterization for stringy
superalgebras (both borrowed from
[
M
]
) are given in
[
GLS1
]
: both types of (super)algebras are
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded

{\mathfrak{g}}=\oplus_{i=-d}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
of infinite
depth
d=\infty
, but their elements act differently in the adjoint
representation:

(11)

for the loop-type (super)algebras
, every root vector corresponding
to any real
8
8

8

Recall that a root
\alpha
is said to be

real
if
k\alpha
is a root only for finitely many distinct
values of the scalar
k
. Otherwise, the root
\alpha
is called

imaginary
.
root acts locally nilpotently
in the adjoint representation;

for the stringy (super)algebras
, this is
not the case.

Before O. Mathieu gave the above intrinsic definition for the Lie
algebras (
[
M
]
), Leites conjectured that, as for Lie
algebras, simple twisted loop superalgebras correspond to outer
automorphisms
\varphi\in\mathop{\mathrm{{A\hskip-0.86108ptu\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}/\mathop{\mathrm{{I\hskip-1.07639ptn\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{{A\hskip-0.86108ptu\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}

is the group of all automorphisms
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{{I\hskip-1.07639ptn\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
is the
subgroup of the inner automorphisms. Serganova listed these
automorphisms and amended the conjecture: to get non-isomorphic
algebras, one should factor
\mathop{\mathrm{{A\hskip-0.86108ptu\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
by a group larger than
\mathop{\mathrm{{I\hskip-1.07639ptn\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
, namely, by the connected component of
the unit of
\mathop{\mathrm{{A\hskip-0.86108ptu\hskip-0.43057ptt\/}}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
, see
[
Se2
,
FLS
]
.

An
a priori

classification of Lie superalgebras of polynomial growth with
symmetrizable Cartan matrix is due to V. Serganova and J. van de Leur
[
Se2
,
vdL
]
; these
classifications and Serganova’s classification of Lie superalgebras
of polynomial growth with
non-symmetrizable
Cartan matrix
cited in
[
LS1
,
GLS1
]
(for a published proof, see

[
HS
]
) prove the conjectured list of loop-type superalgebras
with indecomposable Cartan matrix given in
[
FLS
]
.

Recall the conjecture in
[
FLS
]
: the only other simple Lie superalgebras
of loop type (see definition (
11
)) are (twisted) loops with values in simple finite-dimensional “lopsided” or “queer” Lie superalgebras. To prove this conjecture remains an
Open problem
, tackled in
[
Ca2
]
.

\bullet

Stringy superalgebras
. These are Lie
superalgebras of vector fields on the super circles, and their
relatives. For the conjectured list of simple ones, see
[
GLS1
]
.
Partly (with several extra conditions) this conjecture is proved in

[
KvdL
]
, where all non-trivial central extensions of simple stringy superlgebras are found; for a proof which, inexplicably, ignores Ramond-type superalgebras, see
[
Kcf
,
FaKa
,
Kcfe
]
. Observe
that some of the stringy superalgebras have Cartan matrices, though
non-symmetrizable ones
[
GLS1
]
, and some of them have nontrivial
deformations, whereas their namesakes
with polynomial coefficients are rigid according to the modern
deformation theory that we will use in what follows, see Subsection
1.9
.

Some of the simple stringy superalgebras (
[
FL
,
GLS1
]
) are

distinguished
: they have a nontrivial central extension.
In
[
GLS1
]
, there are also indicated exceptional stringy
superalgebras and occasional isomorphisms unnoticed in previous
papers and often ignored in later ones.

In this paper, we consider the remaining type of simple
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
Lie superalgebras of polynomial growth:

\bullet

Infinite-dimensional vectorial Lie superalgebras
.
The main example to look at with the mind’s eye is the Lie algebra

L={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[x]
, where
x:=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
, of polynomial vector fields with the grading and
filtration given by
\deg x_{i}=1
for all
i
, and its

(x)
-adic completion, the filtered Lie algebra

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[[x]]
of formal vector fields.

É. Cartan came to the classification problem of simple vectorial
algebras from geometrical problems in which
primitive
, rather
than simple, Lie algebras naturally arise; let us recall their definition. Let a Lie algebra
{\mathcal{L}}
contain
a subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
which contains no non-zero ideal of
{\mathcal{L}}
; then,
{\mathcal{L}}
is called
transitive
.
A transitive Lie algebra
{\mathcal{L}}
is called
primitive
(relative a maximal subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
), see
[
O
]
, if

(12)

the space
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}:=\{X\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\mid[X,{\mathcal{L}}]\in{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\}

is not
\{0\}
.

The set of infinite-dimensional
primitive Lie algebras of polynomial growth (resp., the set of finite-dimensional primitive Lie algebras) coincides with the set of Lie algebras of derivations
of simple vectorial Lie algebras (resp., the set of trivial central extensions of simple finite-dimensional Lie algebras, see
[
O
]
). Since
\dim{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{g}}\leq 1
and is seldom equal to 1, the task of
classifying infinite-dimensional
primitive
Lie algebras is
practically the same as that of classifying the simple vectorial Lie algebras.

By contrast, the classification of primitive Lie

super
algebras is a wild problem, as shown already in
[
ALSh
]
, see also
Subsection
12.6
. Having distinguished a feasible
problem, namely,

(13)

“classify infinite-dimensional
simple

vectorial Lie superalgebras,”

it is also natural to classify all relatives of these
algebras, see Subsection
1.2.1
.

1.6.
Weisfeiler filtrations and gradings

Consider
infinite-dimensional filtered Lie (super)algebras
{\mathcal{G}}
with
decreasing filtration of the form

(14)

{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{G}}_{-f}\supset{\mathcal{G}}_{-f+1}\supset\cdots\supset{\mathcal{G}}_{0}\supset{\mathcal{G}}_{1}\supset\cdots\ ,

where
depth

f
is finite and where

(15)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{a)}&{\mathcal{G}}_{0}~~\text{is a~maximal subalgebra of finite
codimension;}\\
\text{b)}&{\mathcal{G}}_{0}~~\text{does not contain ideals of the whole ${\mathcal{G}}$.}\end{array}

The very term “filtered algebra” implies that
[{\mathcal{G}}_{i},{\mathcal{G}}_{j}]\subset{\mathcal{G}}_{i+j}
. Set
G_{i}:={\mathcal{G}}_{i}/{\mathcal{G}}_{i+1}
. We
assume that

(16)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{a)}&\dim G_{i}<\infty\text{~~for all~ $i$;}\\
\text{b)}&\text{$\dim\oplus_{k\leq n}\,G_{k}$ grows as
a~polynomial in $n$.}\end{array}

We assume that these Lie (super)algebras
{\mathcal{G}}
are complete with
respect to a natural topology whose basis of neighborhoods of zero
is formed by the spaces of finite codimension,
e.g.
, by the
{\mathcal{G}}_{i}
.
(In the absence of odd indeterminates, this topology is the most
natural one: two vector fields are said to be
k
-close
if
their coefficients coincide up to terms of degree
\leq k
.) No
other topology will be encountered below, so all topological terms
(complete, open, closed) refer to this topology.
9
9

9

This
topology is naturally called
projective limit
topology
, but the even clumsier term ‘‘linearly compact
topology” is also used.

Weisfeiler
[
W
]
endowed every above-described filtered Lie
algebra
{\mathcal{G}}
with another, refined, filtration, called now the
Weisfeiler filtration
(here
{\mathcal{G}}={\mathcal{L}}
as
abstract algebras and all inclusions are strict):

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}_{-d}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{-d+1}\supset\cdots\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\supset{\mathcal{L}}_{1}\supset\cdots,

where
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}={\mathcal{G}}_{0}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{-1}
is the minimal

{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
-invariant subspace strictly containing
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
; the other
components are defined by formula (
1
).

1.6.1.
On the importance of W-filtrations.

Each simple infinite-dimensional vectorial Lie
algebra
{\mathcal{L}}
over
{\mathbb{C}}
or
{\mathbb{R}}
has only one (up to an automorphism) maximal
subalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
of finite codimension; this
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}

determines the W-filtration; unlike W-grading, the W-filtration is invariant under automorphisms. The topology generated by the W-filtration
is precisely the topology in which
{\mathcal{L}}
can be considered as the Lie
(super)algebra of
continuous
derivations of the algebra of
functions represented by formal power series, see
[
BL3
]
.

It is often more natural to consider infinite-dimensional algebras,
and modules over them, as
topological
algebras, rather than
as
abstract
ones, and therefore vectorial algebras with

formal
coefficients and
(x)
-adic filtration (topology)
are more natural objects than vectorial algebras with

polynomial
coefficients. These “more natural” algebras are
completions with respect to the
(x)
-adic filtration of the
vectorial algebras with polynomial coefficients,
i.e.
, we complete
polynomials to series adding “tails”,
i.e.
, elements of higher
degree. The complete topological algebras are natural objects, but it
is easier to classify first the associated graded algebras.

The W-filtration of a given vectorial Lie (super)algebras
{\mathcal{G}}
is the filtration
corresponding to the
(x)
-adic topology on the space of functions,
or a modification of this topology that takes into account a
non-integrable distribution preserved by
{\mathcal{G}}
. Moreover, for the
W-filtratered
Lie (super)algebra
{\mathcal{L}}\simeq{\mathcal{G}}
, the
L_{0}
-action on

L_{-1}
(recall definition (
3
)) is irreducible.

For the
simple
filtered Lie (super)algebra
{\mathcal{L}}
, and the graded algebra
L
associated
with it, the
L_{0}
-module
L_{-1}
is faithful.
So, being primarily interested in
simple
Lie
superalgebras,
we assume that the
L_{0}
-module
L_{-1}
is
faithful
.

1.7.
Classification problems: A to C

The following problems arise:

A) Classify simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras
as abstract ones,
i.e.
, disregarding difference in grading.

We immediately see that this problem is rather unnatural: quite
distinct algebras become equivalent. Indeed, as early as in

[
ALSh
]
, we observed that
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|1)
, the Lie algebra of all
vector fields on
(1|1)
-dimensional superspace, is isomorphic as an
abstract algebra to
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)
, the Lie algebra of
contact

vector fields on
(1|2)
-dimensional superspace (and also to

{\mathfrak{m}}(1)
, another, “odd” type of contact Lie superalgebra). Therefore, a more
natural formulation is

A
′
) Classify simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras.

In some applications it suffices to confine ourselves to graded
algebras (
[
L8
]
), but in most applications (
e.g.
, in the study
of representations
[
GLS4
]
), the Lie (super)algebras that are

complete
with respect to a topology are more natural than
the graded ones associated with them. So the following problem might
look like a reasonable goal:

On the road to solution of Problem B
′
the following problem

seems
to be a natural step:

B) Classify simple W-filtered complete vectorial Lie
superalgebras as abstract ones,
i.e.
, disregarding differences in filtrations.

B
′
) Classify simple W-filtered complete vectorial Lie
superalgebras.

Natural (‘‘straightforward’’) examples of complete algebras are provided by the
algebras
\overline{L}
obtained from the list of examples
L
that
answer Problem A or A
′
by taking formal series, rather than
polynomials, as coefficients. In addition to these examples, there
might occur nontrivial
filtered deforms
of
\overline{L}
,

i.e.
, complete algebras
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
such that the graded
algebras associated with
\overline{L}
and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
are
both isomorphic to
L
, whereas
\overline{L}\not\simeq\widetilde{{\mathcal{L}}}
. Examples of such filtered deforms will be given
later.

Observe that whereas the difference between Problems A
′
and B
′

is as big as that between Problems A and B, the difference between
Problems B and B
′
is negligible. Actually, it is impossible to
solve Problem B
′
and not solve Problem B. Indeed, various filtered
deforms are not
a priori
isomorphic as abstract algebras,
so, in order to solve Problem B
′
, we have to know, first, all the
W-filtrations or the associated W-gradings and then describe
filtered deforms for
every
W-grading.

However, even Problem B
′
is not the most natural one: there exist
deforms of algebras from class B
′
that do not lie in class B
′
:
e.g.
,
the result of factorization modulo the center of the quantized Poisson superalgebra
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{po}}(2n|2m))
, cf. (
7
); clearly,
{\mathcal{Q}}({\mathfrak{po}}(2n|2m))
is a familiar Lie superalgebra of differential operators with polynomial coefficients. So the true problem one should solve is

C) Describe the simple W-graded vectorial Lie
superalgebras together with all their deformations (not only the filtered
ones).

1.7.1.
Notation

We will
describe all the Lie superalgebras from the tables in our Main
Theorem in detail in due course, but to simplify grasping the
general picture from the displayed formulas of the following
theorem, let us immediately point out that the prime
example, the
general vectorial algebra

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n;r)
, is the Lie
superalgebra of vector fields whose coefficients are polynomials (or
formal power series, in which case it is more correct to denote it
\overline{{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n;r)}
) in
m
commuting and

n
anticommuting indeterminates with the grading (and filtration)
determined by equating the degrees of
r
, where
0\leq r\leq n
, of
odd indeterminates to 0, the degrees of all (even and odd) of the
remaining indeterminates being equal to 1. The regradings of other
series are determined similarly, but in a more complicated way, described
below, see Subsection
2.9
. Usually, we do not indicate the parameter
r
if
r=0
.

In the sequel, for any
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we write
{\mathfrak{cg}}:={\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{{\mathbb{C}}}\cdot z
or
{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{g}})
to denote the trivial central extension with the
1-dimensional even center generated by
z
.

Let
{\mathfrak{i}}\ltimes{\mathfrak{b}}
or
{\mathfrak{b}}\rtimes{\mathfrak{i}}
denote the semidirect sum
of modules (resp., algebras) in which
{\mathfrak{i}}
is a submodule (resp., an ideal).

Let
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{g}}):={\mathfrak{g}}\ltimes{\mathbb{K}}D
be a
sub
algebra of the Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{der}}~\ {\mathfrak{g}}
of all derivations of the Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, where
D
is usually (in examples below) the grading operator of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In proofs, we use standard notation of roots in the simple finite-dimensional Lie algebras, see
[
OV
, Table 5]
(or the respective tables in
[
Bbk
]
or
[
Hm
]
).

Let
\lambda=\frac{2a}{n(a-b)}\in{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
for some

a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
. Although notation
{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n;r)
introduced in
(
108
) is more accurate than
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;r)
, we
often use the shorter notation for brevity.

The notation
{\mathfrak{svect}}_{a,b}(0|n)
and
{\mathfrak{spe}}_{a,b}(n)
, though
looks similar to
{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n;r)
, means something different: it stands for
{\mathfrak{g}}\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}(az+bD)
, where
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}
stands for
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
or
{\mathfrak{spe}}(n)
, respectively,
D
is
the operator that determines the standard
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
and
z
is a central element generating the trivial center (
i.e.
, a
direct summand).

The
exterior algebra
of the (super)space
V
is denoted by

E(V)
or — if
V=V_{\bar{1}}
— by
\Lambda(V)
or
\Lambda(n)
, where
n=\dim V
, a.k.a.
Grassmann algebra

\Lambda(\xi)
on
n
generators
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}
.

Overview of various properties of vectorial Lie (super)algebras

\bullet

On vectorial superalgebras, there are two analogs of trace
; for details, see Subsection
2.6.1
:

1)
Traces
(or
super
traces, for emphasis) ---
various linear functionals that vanish on the first derived
subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
or its (super)commutant
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}:=[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}]
— are traces in the classical sense, simpleminded analogs of the trace on the matrix Lie algebra.

2)
Divergences
— “Cartan prolongs”, as we will see, of traces on the linear subalgebras — though not, actually, traces, are “morally”, as Manin might have dubbed it, closer to, and better resemble the traces on matrix Lie algebras than (super)traces in the classical sense 1).

Accordingly, the analog of the
special
Lie (super)algebra — the traceless subalgebra of the matrix Lie (super)algebra — is the
divergence-free

subalgebra of a vectorial algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
; this analog is denoted by
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{g}}
,

e.g.
,
{\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m)
,
{\mathfrak{sm}}(n)
, etc. The even (resp., odd) part of the codimension of

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime}
is equal to the number of even (resp., odd) supertraces on
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

\bullet
Lie superalgebras of series
{\mathfrak{k}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
are the straightforward
analogs of the Lie algebras of
contact
and
Hamiltonian
series,
respectively; the
Poisson
Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m)
is the — there is just one (class of) — nontrivial
central extension of
{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)
.

\bullet
Lie superalgebras of series
{\mathfrak{m}}
and
{\mathfrak{le}}
are the
“odd” analogs

of the Lie algebras of contact and Hamiltonian series, respectively.
The bracket in the
Buttin superalgebra

{\mathfrak{b}}
, the central extension of
{\mathfrak{le}}
, is the
classical
Schouten bracket
, currently more popular under the name of

antibracket
. We say “odd” in quotation marks: all these
“odd” analogs have both even and odd parts.

\bullet
The
antibracket can be deformed
, the corresponding deformed Lie
superalgebras inside the GLAPG type are
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}
, where

\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
; the results of other deformations of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}
, some of them with an odd parameter, lie outside of
the GLAPG class.

\bullet
The algebras
{\mathfrak{sm}}
,
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{le}}
, and
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}
are divergence-free
subalgebras in the respective algebras.

\bullet
The simple algebra
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}
is a deformation (deform if the parameter of deformation is even) of a nonsimple
Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}(n|n+1;n)
.

\bullet
We note
an outstanding property of the bracket in

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)
: it can be considered
as a deform of the Poisson bracket as well as a deform of the anti-bracket
.

\bullet
In tables (
22
), (
101
) below: in parentheses after the
“family name” of the algebra there stands the superdimension of the
superspace spanned by the indeterminates and
— after the semicolon — an abbreviated description of the regrading.

The regradings of the series are governed by a vector
\vec{r}
, often abbreviated to a number
r
or a symbol, as
described in Subsection
2.9
. All regradings are given in
(hopefully) suggestive notation,
e.g.
,
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\xi}(1|6;3\eta)

means that, having taken
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\xi}
as the point of reference, we
set
\deg\eta=0
for each of the three
\eta
’s (certainly, this
imposes some conditions on the degrees of the other indeterminates, see Subsection
2.9
). Passing from one regrading to another one, we take the realization with the minimal value of
\dim{\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
) as the
point of reference. For the exceptional Lie superalgebras, another
point of reference — the
compatible
grading is often more convenient; it is marked by
K
.

The exceptional grading
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
is
described in passing in the list of occasional isomorphisms
(
23
); it is described in detail for another incarnation of
this algebra in Subsection
2.12.3
.

\bullet

On “drop-outs”.
Several algebras are ‘‘drop-outs’’ from the
series. Examples: the algebras
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;r)
are “drop-outs”
from the series
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n;r)
since the latter are not simple
for
m=1
, but contain the simple ideal
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;r)
.

Similarly,
{\mathfrak{le}}(n|n;r)
,
{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n|n+1;r)
and

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(n|n+1;r)
are “drop-outs” from the Lie superalgebras

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n|n+1;r)
corresponding to
\lambda=0
, 1 and

\infty
, respectively, having either a simple ideal of codimension
1, or a center the quotient modulo which is simple. For
n=2
, there
are several more of exceptional values of
\lambda
.

The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{sm}}(n|n+1;r)
is a drop-out from the series

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n|n+1;r)
for another reason: it is singled out
by its divergence-free property, hence deserves a separate line.

The finite-dimensional Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|n)
of Hamiltonian
vector fields is not simple: it contains a simple ideal
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)
. The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)
for
m=n
is not simple, and therefore the simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)
for
n>1
is a “drop out” from the
{\mathfrak{sl}}
series.

One should carefully distinguish drop-outs
not only in the case of
characteristic
p
or the super-case. People justly consider the
Lie algebra of Hamiltonian vector fields and its central extension, the
Poisson algebra
as totally distinct algebras for any
p
(or loop algebra and its double extension,
i.e.
, affine Kac-Moody relative having a Cartan matrix). Likewise, the difference between

{\mathfrak{sl}}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}
in characteristic
p
and in the super-case is
enormous:
e.g.
, the irreducible
{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}
-module of least dimension is the adjoint one.

Thus, all drop-outs, without exception, are quite separate items on
the list.

1.8.
The steps of our classification

(From
[
LSh1
, 1988, pp.235–289]
; compare with practically the same steps in

[
Kga
, §3]
.) For Lie algebras, the following facts and ideas
are known, though, perhaps, not as well as they deserve, see

[
J
,
GQS
]
.

Namely, given a Lie superalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}
with a Weisfeiler filtration,
consider the associated graded Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}:=\oplus_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}={\mathcal{L}}_{i}/{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}
. There are two distinct cases: that of
a
compatible

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading and
when the grading is
incompatible
.

To the
incompatible grading
certain arguments used in
classification of simple vectorial Lie algebras (
[
J
]
) are
applicable: in particular, the proof of the facts that for W-graded Lie (super)algebras,

(17)

1)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
generates
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\oplus_{i<0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
;

2)
If
a\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},a]=0
, then
a=0

(transitivity condition);

3) the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is not just transitive, it is
irreducible.

The proof of the fact

(18)

“a root of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is the sum of positive
weights of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
”

is given in Section
3
,
where (
18
) reads as follows:

(19)

the Cartan prolong of the pair
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
is of
infinite dimension if and only if the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
has a rank-
1
operator — the coroot corresponding to the root

(
18
)
— with an
even
covector
.

Having established this, we have to find the pairs
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
for which (
18
) holds. The search for such pairs, and the
proof of the completeness of their list occupy Sections
3
–
7
.

Step 1
. Formulation of the Pivotal Ideas of our proof.

(1) After Weisfeiler, we use the fact that every simple filtered
vectorial Lie superalgebra has a W-filtration such that the
associated graded Lie superalgebra is simple, see
[
W
]
;

(2) We show that any simple
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie superalgebra

\oplus_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, with

in
compatible grading, is infinite-dimensional if and only
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
so that the action has a rank-1
operator with an even covector.

We have listed all simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras of
depth 1 in the union of
[
ALSh
]
,
[
Sh3
]
,
[
ShM
]
,

[
Sh14
]
. This union contains (as
is now established) all the examples of simple vectorial
superalgebras of any depth
d
,
considered as abstract ones, and all
their W-regradings, except for the W-regrading
CK
, see table (
101
).

(3) The cases where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is a purely odd superspace
(compatible
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading) or
d>1
. While we left these cases to be
considered later, Cantarini, Cheng and Kac investigated them
in
[
K3
,
Kga
,
CK2
]
and
[
CCK
]
.

For a proof of the classification, see
[
LSh5
]
, a
detailed version of
[
LSh1
,
LSh3
]
. The case of compatible
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading cannot be considered by the above-described methods. This gap in our proof was filled in in the papers by Kac and his co-workers; no new examples were discovered as we predicted.

Step 2
. Criterion for infinite dimensionality of a given Cartan
prolongation: existence of rank-1 operators with an even covector.

Step 3
. Main reduction: the three cases. For
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and an
irreducible faithful representation
\rho
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}

with a rank-1 operator, the following cases are possible:

I)
\rho
is induced (from a representation of a Lie subsuperalgebra of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
of codimension
0|k
).

II)
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is
almost simple
(for this definition, see Subsection
2.4
) or
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is a central extension of an
almost simple Lie superalgebra;

III)
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=2|2
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is contained in a particular Lie superalgebra.

Step 4
. Description of prolongations of induced
representations (case I of step 3).

Step 5
. Description of prolongations of almost simple Lie
superalgebras and their central extensions (case II of step 3).

Step 6
. Case III of step 3. (The unique simple infinite-dimensional Lie superalgebra arising at this step is a regrading of

{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}_{\infty}(2)
.)

Step 7
. Description of W-gradings of the
simple superalgebras found above.

Step 8
. The case of compatible grading. Proof of our conjecture, see

[
K3
,
Kga
,
CK2
,
CCK
]
.

Theorem

(
[
K3
]
)
.

The only simple vectorial Lie superalgebras with compatible
Weisfeiler filtration are:
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|m)
and the exceptional algebras

{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
,
{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{le}}(4|3;K)
,
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6;K)
, and
{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{b}}(4|5;K)
.

Step 9. Description of deformations and deforms
, see Sections
9
and
9.2
.

1.9.
Warning: results of cohomology calculations might be confusing

There are several types of confusing phenomena:

1) For Lie
(super)algebras, the deformation problem
ostensibly
has an
unequivocal answer: calculate the infinitesimal deformations — the elements of
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
, the tangent space to the
(super)variety of deformation parameters — and that is it: if
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})=0
, the
algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
rigid
,
i.e.
, has no deformations. If not, construct the global deformations
whose tangent vectors correspond to the basis elements of

H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
. To find isomorphisms among the members of
the parametric family of deforms still remains sometimes an
Open problem
, especially in the modular cases.

There are, however, examples of simple
(finite-dimensional) Lie (super)algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})\neq 0
, some or all the cocycles constituting a basis of
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})

can be integrated to global deformations, but the deforms corresponding to these cocycles are isomorphic to the initial Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, see

[
Ri
,
Car
,
BLW
,
BLLS
]
.
We call the cocycles with this property, the
cohomology classes they represent, and the corresponding deformations

semi-trivial
. We call the deforms that are neither trivial nor semi-trivial
true
deforms. Fortunately, the reasons for a deform to be
semi-trivial given in
[
BLLS
]
do not exist over
{\mathbb{C}}
. Still,
for infinite-dimensional Lie (super)algebras, the knowledge of

H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
does not always help to describe the deforms:

2a) For example,
{H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})=0}
in terms of the modern cohomology theory (see

[
FF
,
LR
]
), if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is any of the
simple loop algebras. Despite this, P. Golod (Holod) gave the first example of a deform of a loop
algebra, see
[
GoHo1
,
GoHo2
]
; Krichiver and Novikov initiated a representation theory of such deforms,
for a review, see
[
Shei
]
.

2b) In
[
FiFu
]
, an example is given of several non-isomorphic smooth deforms
with a common tangent vector. In this case, to compute

H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
does not suffice to describe all deformations.

To figure out how to describe deformations that explain the above examples is an
Open problem
.

1.9.1.
On filtered deformations.

For the

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of depth 1 and a decreasing
filtration, it was well known (see,
e.g.
,
[
KfiD
]
; superization is streightforward) that

(20)

the filtered
deformations of
{\mathfrak{g}}
correspond to ‘‘certain elements” of the Spencer
cohomology
10
10

10

For examples of calculation and to compare the techniques and interpretations of the results, compare
[
CK1
,
CK1a
]
with
[
Po3
]
(by “hand”) and
[
GL4
,
GLS2
,
BGLS
,
L8
]
.

H^{2,j}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1};{\mathfrak{g}}):=\{c\in H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1};{\mathfrak{g}})\mid\deg c=j\}
.

Let us describe which are the “certain elements” alluded to in
(
20
), and how to treat the algebras of depth
d>0
.

The latter question was answered in
[
GL4
]
, for more details,
see
[
BGLS
]
: one has to pass from the Spencer cohomology to the Lie
(super)algebra cohomology:
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1};{\mathfrak{g}})=\oplus_{j}H^{2,j}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1};{\mathfrak{g}})
. In this
formulation, it is obvious how to generalize the above-described passage to the algebras of depth
d>0
: let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\oplus_{-d\leq j<0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
, then deformations correspond to
“certain elements” of
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}})
.
These “certain elements” are the elements of
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
that are homogeneous of positive
degree. This
description allows the filtration of
{\mathfrak{g}}
to begin with the component of degree

\geq 0
, and hence answers the question Kontsevich posed to us in
2002:

“How to describe filtered deformations of
{\mathcal{L}}
if its depth is
equal to 0?”

However, for
d>0
, computing the cocycles of the
space
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
is a much tougher task than computing even
the whole space
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}})
.

In the paper
[
CK1
]
by Cheng and Kac, the “certain elements”
mentioned above are explicitly described (for the first time, as far as
we know): it is shown that filtered deformations of a given graded Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of non-zero depth and with a decreasing filtration
correspond to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-invariants of the superspace
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}})
.
(Note that Cheng and Kac did not believe in odd parameters
of deformations, and hence considered only even invariants, see
[
CK1
,
CK1a
]
.)

A question arises: given two non-isomorphic
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
of the
same abstract algebra, when does an isomorphism

(21)

(H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}}))^{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}\simeq(H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}}_{-};{\mathfrak{h}}))^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}}

take place? The answer in the cases where all, not only filtered,
deformations are known clearly shows that the isomorphism (
21
)
takes place only if

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{the cocycles $c_{1}\in(H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}}))^{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}$
and $c_{2}\in(H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}}_{-};{\mathfrak{h}}))^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}}$}\\
\text{are compatible with both gradings.}\end{array}

For example, the unique cocycle in cases (
26
) is only
compatible with the W-gradings for which the product of an even number of odd
indeterminates is of degree 0.
A posteriori
we know that for other
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings, there are
no filtered deformations.

Therefore,
[
CK1
]
does not contain a complete list of filtered
deformations of all simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras

{\mathcal{L}}
, as claimed. In fact,
[
CK1
]
considered only
\frac{1}{m}
th of all possibilities for
each
n|m
-dimensional superspace
({\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0})^{*}
. Actually, the claim of
[
CK1
]
is more vague and subject to interpretations: “we determine all simple
filtered deformations of certain
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebras
classified in
[
K3
]
”, but since in
[
K3
]
the algebras are
classified only as abstract ones, it is impossible to say what
exactly is claimed in
[
CK1
]
.

1.10.
Our results

For the list of problems A to C, see Subsection
1.7
.

1)
Simple vs primitive
. In
[
ALSh
]
we have distilled a tame subproblem —

classify simple vectorial Lie superalgebras
— from its
straightforward, but
wild in super setting
, see Subsection
12.6
, problem

classify primitive Lie superalgebras
, see
[
K2
,
K3
,
Kga
]
.

2)
Solution of Problem A
′
. The union of our
examples (
[
ALSh
]
,
[
LSh1
]
,
[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
) was our conjectural
list solving Problem A
′
, as announced in
[
LSh2′
]
. The list we
announced is, indeed, complete. For compatible gradings, see
[
K3
,
CK1
,
CK1a
,
CK2
,
CCK
]
, which prove our conjecture.

3)
Solution of Problem B
′

with the results on filtered deformations, see
[
LSh3
,
GLS2
]
, we
obtain solution of Problem B
′
.

4)
Classification of W-gradings
. See Subsections
8.1.4
,
2.9
,
2.15
.

Theorem

(Solution to Problems A and A
′
, B and B
′
)
.

The simple W-graded vectorial Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
can be thriftily collected into
14
families as abstract algebras (
22
), further divided into
34
W-graded series, see Subsection
2.15
, and
15

W-graded exceptional algebras forming
5
abstract algebras, see Subsection
2.14.1.  Theorem
. They are pair-wise non-isomorphic,
as graded and filtered superalgebras, except for occasional isomorphisms
(
23
).

(22)

\tiny\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{tabular}[]{|l|l|}\hline\cr$N$&algebra and conditions for its simplicity\cr\hline\cr 1&${\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n;r)$ for $m\geq 1$ and $0\leq r\leq n$\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr FD&${\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n;r)$ for $n>1$ and $0\leq r\leq n$\cr\hline\cr 2&${\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n;r)$ for $m>1$, $0\leq r\leq n$\cr\hline\cr FD&${\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n;r)$ for $n>2$ and $0\leq r\leq n$\cr\hline\cr 3&${\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;r)$ for $n>1$, $0\leq r\leq n$\cr\hline\cr FD&$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|n)$ for $n>2$\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr 4&${\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n;r)$ for $0\leq r\leq[\frac{n}{2}]$,
$(m|n)\neq(0|2k)$\cr\hline\cr&${\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;r)$ for $0\leq r\leq k$ except for
$r=k-1$\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr 5&${\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n;r)$ for $m>0$ and $0\leq r\leq[\frac{n}{2}]$\cr\hline\cr FD&${\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)$ for $n>3$\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}&\begin{tabular}[]{|l|l|}\hline\cr$N$&algebra and conditions for its simplicity\cr\hline\cr 6&${\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2;r)$ for
$r=0$, $1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{h}}}$\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr 7&${\mathfrak{m}}(n|n+1;r)$ for $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr 8&${\mathfrak{sm}}(n|n+1;r)$ for $n>1$ but $n\neq 3$ and\cr&$0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr 9&${\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n|n+1;r)$ for $n>1$, where $\lambda\neq 0,1,\infty$\cr&and $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$;\cr&${\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2|3;r)$, for
$r=0$, $1$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}}$\cr\hline\cr 10&${\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n|n+1;r)$ for $n>1$, $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr 11&${\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(n|n+1;r)$ for $n>1$, $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr 12&${\mathfrak{le}}(n|n;r)$ for $n>1$, $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr 13&${\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n|n;r)$ for $n>2$, $0\leq r\leq n$, $r\neq n-1$\cr\hline\cr 14&$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})$ for $\mu\neq 0$ and
$n>2$ even\cr&$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)$ for $\mu\neq 0$ and
$n>1$ odd\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{array}

Hereafter we abbreviate the algebras
{\mathfrak{m}}(n|n+1;r)
,
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n|n+1;r)
,
{\mathfrak{le}}(n|n;r)
and so on by
{\mathfrak{m}}(n;r)
,
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;r)
,
{\mathfrak{le}}(n;r)
,
and so on, respectively.

Proof

of this theorem occupies the main body of this article. ∎

Observe that real forms (classified in an interesting paper
[
CaKa1
]
) do not always survive regrading.

1.10.2.
W-gradings of serial simple vectorial Lie superalgebras.

Though
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;r)

and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2;r)
are isomorphic, we consider them
separately because they are deforms of very distinct structures: the superalgebras with the
odd and the even bracket, respectively. In Subsection
2.17
we
show wherefrom the mysterious at this point isomorphism

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1-\lambda}(2|2)
comes.

The family
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)

should be considered as an exceptional one.
The following table lists all W-gradings of serial simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, see Theorem
2.15.1.  Theorem
.

Occasional isomorphisms

One can directly verify that

(23)

\footnotesize\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|1)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|1;1);\\
{\mathfrak{svect}}(2|1)\simeq{\mathfrak{le}}(2;2);\;{\mathfrak{svect}}(2|1;1)\simeq{\mathfrak{le}}(2)\\
{\mathfrak{sm}}(n)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{2/(n-1)}(n);\\
\text{in particular,
${\mathfrak{sm}}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{2}(2)$, and ${\mathfrak{sm}}(3)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}(3)$; hence, ${\mathfrak{sm}}(3)$ is not
simple};\\
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{le}}^{\prime}(3)\simeq{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{le}}^{\prime}(3;3);\\
{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2;2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{1/2}(2|2)={\mathfrak{h}}(2|2);\hskip 8.50012pt{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2);\\
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2;1)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2);\\
\text{${\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(2;{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{le}}(2)$,}\\
\text{${\mathfrak{le}}(2;{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(2)$},\\
\text{${\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(2;{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(2)$};\\
{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(2;\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{-b,-a}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{b,a}(2),\ \ \text{see eq.~\eqref{reg4}};\\
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{-1-\lambda}(2)\;\text{
and
}\;{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1-\lambda}(2|2),\ \ \text{see eq.~\eqref{unmyst}; hence,}\\
\text{the fundamental domain is either }\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\mathop{\text{\rm Re}}\nolimits\lambda\geq-\frac{1}{2}\}\text{ or
$\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}\mid\mathop{\text{\rm Re}}\nolimits\lambda\leq-\frac{1}{2}\}$};\\
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})\simeq\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\nu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})\text{ for $n>2$ even and $\mu\nu\neq 0$},\\
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)\simeq\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\nu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)\text{ for $n>1$ odd}.\end{array}

Warning
. Isomorphic abstract Lie superalgebras may be
quite distinct as filtered or graded:
e.g.
, regradings provide us
with isomorphisms (
[
ALSh
]
):

(24)

{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2;1)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|1)\simeq{\mathfrak{m}}(1;1).

Observe that only one of the above three non-isomorphic graded
algebras is W-graded over
{\mathbb{C}}
; the situation becomes more complicated over
{\mathbb{R}}
, cf.
[
CaKa1
]
.

The standard grading of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)
, though non-Weisfeiler, is often considered in physics
papers.
Observe that some real forms of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k-1)
are W-graded.

Comments

For completeness, our table (
22
) includes occasional finite-dimensional
versions labeled by “FD” instead of the number.

All the Lie superalgebras listed in Theorem
1.10.1.  Theorem
are the results of

Cartan prolongation
, see Subsection
2.8
(or the derived algebras thereof),
and therefore are determined by the terms
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}

with
i\leq 0
(or
i\leq 1
in some cases), see Subsection
2.15
. The “shape” of these terms might drastically vary with
n
and
r
, though they can be united in the
“families” (
22
) and (
101
).

The Lie superalgebras
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|n)
and

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)
depend on an odd
parameter for
n
odd.

The exceptional regrading
{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2)

and the isomorphism
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)

determine an exceptional grading
{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
of

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
. These regradings (
{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
and
{\rm Reg}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
, see eq. (
23
)
and Subsection
2.12.3
) are irrelevant as far as the
classification of simple Lie superalgebras is concerned, thanks to the isomorphism. On the
other hand, these regradings contribute to the group of
automorphisms of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)
.

5)
Solution of Problem C
is given by Theorem
2.12.1.  Theorem
, Theorem
9.4.  Theorem
on deformations of serial simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, and Conjecture
9.5.  Conjecture
on rigidity of the exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras. The following propositions classify the filtered deformations.

Proposition

(No filtered deformations of exceptional vectorial Lie
superalgebras)
.

For the
W-graded exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, the isomorphism (
21
) does not take place. The spaces
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-};{\mathfrak{g}})
and

H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}}_{-};{\mathfrak{h}})
are always distinct in these cases: they consist
of different
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
- and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-modules.

Proof

is given in
[
GLS2
]
. ∎

Proposition

(Filtered deformations of simple serial vectorial Lie
superalgebras)
.

There are only
4
series

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
of filtered deformations of simple serial vectorial Lie
superalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}
corresponding to the following W-gradings:

(25)

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n),\quad{\mathfrak{sle}}(n),\quad{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}(n),\quad{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}(n;n).

The first three of these deformations
can be described as follows.
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)
be a subalgebra with
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=n|m
,
where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\oplus_{i<0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
; let
\Theta
be
the product of all
m
odd indeterminates and let
t
be a parameter,

p(t)=m\pmod{2}
. Then,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{t}=(1+t\Theta){\mathcal{L}}
and

(26)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{t}\simeq\begin{cases}{\mathcal{L}}&\text{for $t$
even},\\
{\mathcal{L}}\otimes\Lambda(t)&\text{for $t$ odd},\end{cases}
is a subsuperalgebra of
\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)&\text{for $t$
even},\\
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)\otimes\Lambda(t)&\text{for $t$ odd}.\end{cases}

Moreover,
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{t_{1}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{t_{2}}
whenever
t_{1}t_{2}\neq 0
for
t_{1},t_{2}
even, and for
t_{1}=at_{2}
with
a\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
a\neq 0
for
t_{1},t_{2}
odd.

Proof

follows from the classification of deformations of the serial Lie superalgebras, see Section
9
. ∎

2.
Background and basic examples

2.1.
Linear algebra in superspaces. Generalities

A

superspace
is a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded space

V=V_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus V_{{\bar{1}}}
, where
{\bar{0}},{\bar{1}}\in{\mathbb{Z}}/2
. A

supalgebra
is a superspace with multiplication.

By
\Pi(V)
we denote another copy of
the same superspace
V
, but with the inverted parity,
i.e.
,
(\Pi(V))_{\bar{i}}=V_{\bar{i}+{\bar{1}}}
. We call non-zero elements of
V_{{\bar{0}}}

even
(resp., those of
V_{{\bar{1}}}

odd
); we denote the parity function by
p
and write
p(v)=i
if
v\in V_{i}
and
v\neq 0
.

Let
a=\dim V_{{\bar{0}}}
,
b=\dim V_{{\bar{1}}}
. As is clear from Milnor’s
K_{0}
-functor point of view, the
superdimension
of
V
is
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V:=a+b\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon^{2}=1
. (Usually, the superdimension is written as a pair
(a,b)
or
a|b
;
this notation obscures the fact that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits(V\otimes W)=\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits W
.) For any superspace
V
, let
\mathop{\mathrm{{P\hskip-1.29167ptt\hskip-0.21529pty\/}}}\nolimits
be the parity operator,

i.e.
,
\mathop{\mathrm{{P\hskip-1.29167ptt\hskip-0.21529pty\/}}}\nolimits^{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
and the eigenspaces of
\mathop{\mathrm{{P\hskip-1.29167ptt\hskip-0.21529pty\/}}}\nolimits
corresponding to
the eigenvalues 1 and
-1
are
V_{{\bar{0}}}
and
V_{{\bar{1}}}
,
respectively.

A superspace structure in
V
induces a superspace structure in
the space
\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits(V)
. A
basis of a superspace
is always
a basis consisting of vectors
homogeneous
with respect to parity; let
\text{Size}:=(p_{1},\dots,p_{\dim V})
be an ordered collection of the parities of these vectors. We
call
Size
the
format
of (the basis of)
V
. A square

supermatrix
of format (size)
Size
is an
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V\times\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V
matrix whose
i
th row and
i
th column are of the same
parity
p_{i}\in\text{Size}
. We set
|\text{Size}|=\dim V
.

One usually considers one of the simplest formats

Size
,
e.g.
, the format
\text{Size}_{st}
of the form
({\bar{0}},\dots,{\bar{0}};{\bar{1}},\dots,{\bar{1}})
is called
standard
. In this paper we
can do without nonstandard formats. But they are vital in the
classification of systems of simple roots of simple finite-dimensional
Lie superalgebras, see
[
Se4
]
. In particular, the reader
might be interested in them in connection with applications to

q
-quantization or integrable systems. Systems of simple roots
corresponding to distinct nonstandard formats are related by
so-called odd reflections — analogs of our nonstandard regradings, see
[
CCLL
,
Leb
]
.

The matrix unit
E_{ij}
is by default of parity
p_{i}+p_{j}
.

The formulas of Linear Algebra are defined via
the
Sign Rule
:

(27)

if something of parity
\alpha
moves past something of parity

\beta
, the sign
(-1)^{\alpha\beta}
accrues; the formulas defined only on
homogeneous elements are extended to arbitrary elements via linearity
.

Examples of application of the Sign Rule: by setting
[X,Y]:=XY-(-1)^{p(X)p(Y)}YX
we get the notion of the
super commutator

and the ensuing notions of supercommutative and super anticommutative
superalgebras; a
Lie superalgebra
is a superalgebra
A
that satisfies
super anticommutativity and the super Jacobi identities:

\begin{array}[]{l}{}[x,y]=-(-1)^{p(x)p(y)}[y,x];\\
{}[x,[y,z]]=[[x,y],z]+(-1)^{p(x)p(y)}[y,[x,z]]~~\text{for any $x,y,z\in A$}.\end{array}

A
super derivation

of a superalgebra
A
is a linear mapping
D:A\longrightarrow A
that satisfies
the super Leibniz rule

D(ab)=D(a)b+(-1)^{p(D)p(a)}aD(b)~~\text{for any $a,b\in A$}.

In particular, let
A={\mathbb{C}}[x]
, where
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, be the free supercommutative
polynomial superalgebra in
x
; let the
superstructure in
A
be determined by the parities of the indeterminates:

p(x_{i})=p_{i}
.
Partial derivatives
are defined (with the help of
the Sign and Leibniz Rules) by the formulas

{\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{j}}}}(x_{i})=\delta_{ij}
. Clearly, the collection
{\mathfrak{der}}~\ {\mathbb{C}}[x]
of all superderivations of
A
is a Lie superalgebra whose
elements are of the form
\sum f_{i}(x){\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}
with
f_{i}(x)\in{\mathbb{C}}[x]
for all
i
.

Note that applying the Sign Rule sometimes requires some dexterity.
For example, in non-super setting
skew
- and
anti-commutativity
are synonyms. We have to distinguish between (anti-)commutativity of the superalgebra
A
, and
skew
- or
anti-skew-commutativity
commutativities of
\Pi(A)
; the Sign Rule applied for any
a,b\in A
yields the four cases:

\begin{array}[]{ll}ba=(-1)^{p(b)p(a)}ab&(\text{super commutativity})\\
ba=-(-1)^{p(b)p(a)}ab&(\text{super anti-commutativity})\\
ba=(-1)^{(p(b)+1)(p(a)+1)}ab&(\text{super skew-commutativity})\\
ba=-(-1)^{(p(b)+1)(p(a)+1)}ab&(\text{super antiskew-commutativity})\end{array}

Thus, the

skew
formulas are those that can be “straightened” by the
reversal of parity of the space on which the multiplilcation is considered,
whereas the prefix
anti
requires an overall minus sign regardless of
parity.

Convention
. In what follows, the supercommutative
superalgebra is always supposed to be associative with unit 1; morphisms of superalgebras are the even homomorphisms;
morphisms of supercommutative superalgebras send 1 into 1.

Let the indeterminates
x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n+m})
, so that
n
of them are
even and
m
of them odd, generate the supercommutative superalgebra

{\mathcal{F}}
of “functions” (polynomials or series, etc.). Define the
supercommutative superalgebra
\Omega
of differential forms as
the
polynomial
algebra over
{\mathcal{F}}
in the
dx_{i}
, where
p(d)={\bar{1}}
.

Since

dx_{i}
is even for
x_{i}
odd, we can go beyond polynomials
in
dx_{i}
: smooth or analytic functions in the
dx_{i}
are called

pseudodifferential forms
on the supermanifold with
coordinates
x_{i}
, see
[
BL2
]
. We will need such pseudo-forms to interpret

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
. The exterior differential is defined on the
space of (pseudo) differential forms by the formulas (mind the Super
and Leibniz rules):

d(f):=\sum dx_{i}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial x_{i}}$}}\text{~~and~~}d^{2}=0.

The Lie derivative is defined (minding the same rules) by the formula

L_{D}(df)=(-1)^{p(D)}d(D(f)).

In particular,

L_{D}\left((df)^{\lambda}\right)=\lambda(-1)^{p(D)}d(D(f))(df)^{\lambda-1}\text{
for any $\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}$ and $f$ odd}.

2.2.
What the Lie superalgebra is
