When dealing with
superalgebras it sometimes becomes useful to know their definition.
Lie superalgebras were distinguished in topology in the 1940s, so an offer of a “better than usual” definition of
a notion which seemed to have been known since then might
look strange, to say the least. Nevertheless, the answer to the
question “what is a Lie superalgebra?” is still not commonly
known.

So far, we operated naively: via the Sign Rule. However, the naive
definition suggested above (“apply the Sign Rule to the definition
of the Lie algebra”) is manifestly inadequate for considering
supervarieties of deformations and for applications of
representation theory to mathematical physics, for example, in the
study of the coadjoint representation of a given Lie supergroup which
can act on a supervariety, but never on a vector superspace (an
object from another category). So, to deform Lie superalgebras and
apply group-theoretical methods in the “super” setting, we must be
able to recover a supervariety (a ringed space) from a superspace (an object of the Linear Algebra), and vice versa.

A supervariety isomorphic to the ringed space whose base is a variety
M
and whose structure sheaf is the sheaf of sections of the Grassmann algebra
\Lambda^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(E)
of a vector bundle
E
over
M
, is called
split
. Observe that every object in the category of smooth supervarieties (= supermanifolds) is split (for a short proof of this fact, first established in
[
Ga
,
Bat
]
, see
[
MaG
, Subsection 4.1.3]
), and therefore there is a one–to–one correspondence between the set of objects in the category of vector bundles over manifolds
M
and the set of objects in the category of smooth supermanifolds. The latter category has, however, many more morphisms than the former, see
[
Ld
, formulas (5) in Subsection 3.1]
.

A(n affine)
superscheme

\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
— a purely algebraic version of the above over any field (or any commutative ring) of arbitrary characteristic — is defined in
[
L0
]
exactly as the affine scheme (see
[
MaAG
]
): its points are prime ideals defined literally as in the commutative case,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{p}}\subsetneq C
is
prime
11
11

11

K. Coulembier pointed out to us that the so far conventional definition in the non-commutative case is at variance with the common sense: at the moment, if condition (
28
) holds,
{\mathfrak{p}}
is called (say, in Wikipedia)
completely prime
while it would be natural to retain the term
prime
, as is done in
[
L0
]
, since the definition is the same despite the fact that supercommutative rings are not commutative, whereas the term
prime
refers (so far) to any ideal
P\subsetneq R
in a non-commutative ring
R
with the property that
for any two ideals
A
and
B
in
R
the following version of condition (
28
) holds:

\text{if $AB\subset P$, then either $A\subset P$, or $B\subset P$}.

(28)

\text{if $a,b\in C$ and $ab\in{\mathfrak{p}}$, then either $a\in{\mathfrak{p}}$, or $b\in{\mathfrak{p}}$}.

The spectrum of the supercommutative superalgebra is a ringed space, where
the left localization with respect to the multiplicative system
C\setminus{\mathfrak{p}}
is equivalent
12
12

12

No proof of this fact was ever published to this day, so some authors try to evade discussing this fact and describe localizations only with respect to purely even multiplicative systems, thus giving a wrong definition of the superscheme.
to the right localization,
i.e.
,
cd^{-1}=d^{-1}c
for any
c\in C
and
d\in C\setminus{\mathfrak{p}}
.

To every linear (a.k.a. vector) superspace
V
there corresponds the
linear supervariety
or supermanifold (in the
C^{\infty}
-category) which is a ringed space

(29)

{\mathcal{V}}=(V_{\bar{0}},\ \ {\mathcal{O}}_{V_{\bar{0}}}\otimes\Lambda^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(V_{\bar{1}}^{*})).

The
supervariety of linear homomorphisms

V\longrightarrow W
is the linear supervariety corresponding to the superspace
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)
. The
morphisms
of linear superspaces constitute the space
\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits}(V,W):=(\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W))_{\bar{0}}
.

A proper definition of Lie superalgebras is as follows
. A

Lie superalgebra
is a
Lie superalgebra in the category of supervarieties
. Instead of expressing the boldfaced requirement in terms of commutative diagrams (and how to check their commutativity?!) one uses the
functor of points
, see
[
Ld
]
.
Namely, any Lie superalgebra
L
in the sense of the naive definition represents a functor
\mathop{\mathrm{{\sf ScommSalgs}}}\nolimits\leadsto{\sf LieSAlgs}
from the category of supercommutative superalgebras to the category of Lie superalgebras (over the same ground field),

\text{$C\longmapsto L(C):=L\otimes_{{\mathbb{K}}}C$ for any $C\in\mathop{\mathrm{{\sf ScommSalgs}}}\nolimits$},

such that

(30)

to any morphism of supercommutative superalgebras
C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}
, there corresponds a morphism of Lie superalgebras
L\otimes C\longrightarrow L\otimes C^{\prime}
so that a composition of morphisms
C\longrightarrow C^{\prime}\longrightarrow C^{\prime\prime}
goes into the composition of morphisms
L\otimes C\longrightarrow L\otimes C^{\prime}\longrightarrow L\otimes C^{\prime\prime}
; the identity map goes into the identity map.

In these terms, a
homomorphism of Lie superalgebras

\rho:L_{1}\longrightarrow L_{2}
is a functor morphism,
i.e.
, a collection of Lie algebra
homomorphisms
\rho_{C}:L_{1}(C)\longrightarrow L_{2}(C)
such that any
homomorphism of supercommutative superalgebras
\varphi:C\longrightarrow C_{1}

induces a Lie algebra homomorphism
{\varphi:L(C)\longrightarrow L(C_{1})}
, and
products of such homomorphisms are naturally compatible. In
particular, a
representation
of a Lie superalgebra
L
in
a superspace
V
is a Lie superalgebra homomorphism
\rho:L\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
,
i.e.
,
a collection of Lie superalgebra homomorphisms
\rho_{C}:L(C)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes C
.

Observe that if the characteristic of the ground field is not equal to 3 or 2, one can use another functor of points, called “Even rules” in
[
Del
, Ch.1]
, cf.
[
KLLS
]
.

2.2.1.
Deformations with odd parameters. Deforms.

Which of the infinitesimal deformations can be extended to
a global one is a separate question, much tougher than the search of infinitesimal deformations, and it is usually solved

ad hoc
. For examples over fields of characteristics
3
and

2
, see
[
BLW
,
BLLS
,
BGL1
]
and references therein. Deformations with odd parameters are always integrable.

1)
Deformations of representations
. Consider a representation
\rho:{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
.
The tangent space of the moduli superspace of deformations of
\rho

is isomorphic to
H^{1}({\mathfrak{g}};V\otimes V^{*})
. For example, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
the
0|n
-dimensional (
i.e.
, purely odd) Lie superalgebra (with the
only bracket possible: identically equal to zero), its only
irreducible representations are the 1-dimensional trivial one,

\mathbbmss{1}
, and
\Pi(\mathbbmss{1})
. Clearly,

\mathbbmss{1}\otimes\mathbbmss{1}^{*}\simeq\Pi(\mathbbmss{1})\otimes\Pi(\mathbbmss{1})^{*}\simeq\mathbbmss{1},

and, because the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is commutative, the
differential in the cochain complex is zero. Therefore,

H^{1}({\mathfrak{g}};\mathbbmss{1})\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}^{*},

so there are
\dim\,{\mathfrak{g}}
of different odd parameters of deformations of the
trivial representation. If we consider
{\mathfrak{g}}
“naively”, we miss and lose all
these deformations.

2)
Deformations of the brackets
.
Let
C
be a supercommutative superalgebra, let
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
be the affine superscheme defined literally as the affine scheme (see
[
MaAG
]
) of any commutative ring, see
[
L0
]
later expounded in
[
LSoS
,
MaG
]
.

Recall, see
[
Ru
]
, where the non-super case is considered, that a
deformation
of
a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over
\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}\nolimits C
, is a
C
-algebra
{\mathfrak{G}}
such that
{\mathfrak{G}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes C
, as spaces. The deformation is
trivial
if
{\mathfrak{G}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes C
, as Lie superalgebras, not just as spaces, and
non-trivial
otherwise.

In particular, consider a deformation with an odd parameter
\tau
. This is a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{G}}
isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
as a
super space
; if, moreover,
{\mathfrak{G}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
as a
Lie superalgebra
,
i.e.
,

[a\otimes f,b\otimes g]=(-1)^{p(f)p(b)}[a,b]\otimes fg\text{~~for all~ $a,b\in{\mathfrak{g}}$ and $f,g\in{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]$},

then the deformation is considered
trivial
(and
non-trivial
otherwise). Note that
{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\tau
is not an ideal of
{\mathfrak{G}}
: the ideal should be a free
{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
-module.

Comment
. In a sense, the people who ignore odd parameters of deformations have a point: they consider classification of simple Lie superalgebras over the ground field
{\mathbb{K}}
, right? This can be considered correct, but it is not right: the deformations parameterized by
(H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}}))_{\bar{1}}
are no less natural than the odd part of the deformed Lie superalgebra itself. To take these parameters into account, we have to consider everything not over
{\mathbb{K}}
, but over
{\mathbb{K}}[\tau]
. We do the same, actually, when
\tau
is even and we consider formal deformations over
{\mathbb{K}}[[\tau]]
. If
\tau
is even and the formal series converges in a domain
D
, then we can evaluate the series and consider copies
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\tau}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
for every
\tau\in D
.

When the parameter is formal (the series diverges) or odd, the only evaluation possible is
\tau=0
.

Examples that lucidly illustrate why one should always remember that
a Lie superalgebra is not a mere linear superspace, but a linear
supervariety, are,
e.g.
, deformations
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|2n+1)
and

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{2n}-1|2^{2n})
with odd parameters. These deformations are simple Lie superalgebras in
the category of supervarieties.

2.3.
Examples of Lie superalgebras

2.3.1.
Algebras of operators.

The
general linear
Lie
superalgebra of all supermatrices of size
Size
is denoted by

{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
, where
\text{Size}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{|\text{Size}|})
is the ordered
collection of parities of the rows identical with the ordered
collection of parities of the columns;
usually, for the
standard
(simplest in a sense) format,
{\mathfrak{gl}}({\bar{0}},\dots,{\bar{0}},{\bar{1}},\dots,{\bar{1}})
is abbreviated to
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\dim V_{\bar{0}}|\dim V_{\bar{1}})
. Any non-zero supermatrix from
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
can
be uniquely expressed as the sum of its even and odd parts; in the
standard format, this is the following block expression with
p
defined for
A,B,C,D
non-zero:

\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}$}=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{pmatrix}$}+\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}$},\quad p\left(\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\
0&D\end{pmatrix}$}\right)={\bar{0}},\ \ p\left(\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&B\\
C&0\end{pmatrix}$}\right)={\bar{1}}.

The
supertrace
is the mapping

{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
,
A=(A_{ij})\longmapsto\sum(-1)^{p_{i}(p(A)+1)}A_{ii}
,

where
\text{Size}=(p_{1},\dots,p_{|\text{Size}|})
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits[x,y]=0
, the subsuperspace of supertraceless
matrices constitutes the
special linear
Lie subsuperalgebra

{\mathfrak{sl}}(\text{Size})
.

There are, however, at least two super versions of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(n)
, not
one. Another version is the
queer

Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(n)
defined as the Lie superalgebra that preserves the
complex structure given by an
odd
operator
J
in the
n|n
-dimensional superspace
V
,
i.e.
,

{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(n)
is the centralizer
C(J)
of
J
in
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)={\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)
:

{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(n)=C(J)=\{X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)\mid[X,J]=0\},\text{ where $p(J)={\bar{1}}$ and $J^{2}=-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits$}.

By a change of basis one can reduce
J
to the following normal shape
in the standard
format:
J=J_{2n}:=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}0&1_{n}\\
-1_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}$}
, and then
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(n)
takes the form

(31)

{\mathfrak{q}}(n)=\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}$}\mid A,B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n)\right\}.

The nonstandard formats of
{\mathfrak{q}}(\text{Size})
for
|\text{Size}|=2n
are of the
form (
31
) with
A,B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
.

On
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
, the
queertrace
is defined by the formula

\mathop{\mathrm{qtr}}\nolimits\colon\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}$}\longmapsto\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits B,

first introduced in
[
BL5
]
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{sq}}(n)
the Lie superalgebra
of
queertraceless
matrices.

Note that the tautological representations of
{\mathfrak{q}}(V)
and

{\mathfrak{sq}}(V)
in
V
, though irreducible in super sense (no invariant
sub
super
spaces), are not irreducible in the nongraded sense. Indeed, take
homogeneous (with respect to parity) and linearly independent
vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
from
V
; then,
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(v_{1}+J(v_{1}),\dots,v_{n}+J(v_{n}))
is an invariant subspace of
V
which is not a
subsuperspace.

The representation of a superalgebra is said to be
irreducible

of

general type
, or just of
G
-type
, if there is no
invariant subspaces; the representation is called
irreducible of

Q
-type
if it has no invariant sub
super
spaces, but

has
an invariant subspace.

On normal shapes of supermatrices
J
preserved by
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(V)

Over an algebraically non-closed field
{\mathbb{K}}
, there are several types of queer Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(V)\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
, defined as centralizers of an odd operator
J
such that
J^{2}=\lambda\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
for a
\lambda\in{\mathbb{K}}^{\times}
. Over algebraically closed fields, all these superalgebras
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(V)
are isomorphic as is easy to see. Over
{\mathbb{C}}
, we can select

\lambda=-1
and interpret
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(V)
as the Lie subsuperalgebra of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
preserving the complex structure given by an odd operator
J
. For real forms of
{\mathfrak{q}}_{J}(V)
, see
[
Se1
]
with details of the proof in
[
B3
, Ch. D5]
. Over algebraically closed fields of characteristic
2
, there is also one class of normal shapes, we take
\lambda=1
, see
[
BGLLS
]
.

2.3.2.
Supermatrices of bilinear forms

To a given linear mapping
F:V\longrightarrow W
of superspaces there corresponds the
dual mapping
F^{*}:W^{*}\longrightarrow V^{*}
between the dual superspaces. Let a basis of the superspace
V
have format
Size
and
consist of vectors
v_{i}
; then, the formula

F(v_{j})=\sum_{i}v_{i}X_{ij}
assigns to the endomorphism
F
the
supermatrix
X
. In the dual basis, the
supertransposed

matrix
X^{st}
corresponds to the dual operator
F^{*}
:

(X^{st})_{ij}=(-1)^{(p_{i}+p_{j})(p_{i}+p(X))}X_{ji}.

Consider
X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}_{\mathcal{C}}(\text{Size})
over a supercommutative superalgebra
{\mathcal{C}}
, so the entries of the matrices
A,B,C,D
belong to
{\mathcal{C}}
for any parity of
X
. In the standard format, this means that

X=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}$}\longmapsto X^{st}:=\begin{cases}\begin{pmatrix}A^{t}&C^{t}\\
-B^{t}&D^{t}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if $p(X)={\bar{0}}$},\\
\begin{pmatrix}A^{t}&-C^{t}\\
B^{t}&D^{t}\end{pmatrix},&\text{if $p(X)={\bar{1}}$}.\\
\end{cases}

The supermatrices
X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})
such that

X^{st}B+(-1)^{p(X)p(B)}BX=0\quad\text{for a~ homogeneous matrix
$B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(\text{Size})$}

constitute the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{aut}}(B)
that preserves the
bilinear form
{\mathcal{B}}
on
V
whose Gram matrix
B=(B_{ij})
is given by the formula, see
[
LSoS
, Ch.1]
,

(32)

B_{ij}:=(-1)^{p(B)p(v_{i})}{\mathcal{B}}(v_{i},v_{j})\text{~~~~for the basis vectors $v_{i}\in V$}.

The formula (
32
) makes it possible to identify the bilinear form
{\mathcal{B}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{Bil}}\nolimits(V,W)
with an element of
\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W^{*})
.

Consider the
upsetting
of bilinear forms

u\colon\mathop{\mathrm{Bil}}\nolimits(V,W)\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{Bil}}\nolimits(W,V)
, see
[
LSoS
, Ch.1]
, given by the formula

(33)

u({\mathcal{B}})(w,v):=(-1)^{p(v)p(w)}{\mathcal{B}}(v,w)\text{~~for any $v\in V$ and $w\in W$.}

In terms of the Gram matrix
B
of
{\mathcal{B}}
, the form

{\mathcal{B}}
is
symmetric
if and only if

(34)

u(B)=B,\;\text{ where $u(B)=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}R^{t}&(-1)^{p(B)}T^{t}\\
(-1)^{p(B)}S^{t}&-U^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}$ for $B=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}R&S\\
T&U\end{pmatrix}$}$.}

Similarly,
anti-symmetry
of

{\mathcal{B}}
means that
u(B)=-B
.

Thus, we see that the
upsetting
of bilinear forms corresponds to a new operation on supermatrices,
not
to the supertransposition.

Observe
that
the passage from
V
to
\Pi(V)
turns every symmetric (resp., anti-symmetric)
form
{\mathcal{B}}
on
V
into an anti-symmetric (resp., symmetric) form
{\mathcal{B}}^{\Pi}
on
\Pi(V)
by setting

\text{${\mathcal{B}}^{\Pi}(\Pi(x),\Pi(y)):=(-1)^{p(B)+p(x)+p(x)p(y)}{\mathcal{B}}(x,y)$ for any $x,y\in V$}.

Most popular normal shapes of the even non-degenerate symmetric
form are the following ones in the standard format:

(35)

\begin{array}[]{l}B^{\prime}_{ev}(m|2n)=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}1_{m}&0\\
0&J_{2n}\end{pmatrix}$},\quad\text{where
$J_{2n}=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&1_{n}\\
-1_{n}&0\end{pmatrix}$}$}\\
B_{ev}(2k|2n)=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{2k}&0\\
0&J_{2n}\end{pmatrix}$},\quad\text{where
$\Pi_{2k}=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}0&1_{k}\\
1_{k}&0\end{pmatrix}$}$},\\
B_{ev}(2k+1|2n)=\text{\small$\begin{pmatrix}\Pi_{2k+1}&0\\
0&J_{2n}\end{pmatrix}$},\quad\text{where
$\Pi_{2k+1}=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}0&0&1_{k}\\
0&1&0\\
1_{k}&0&0\end{pmatrix}$}$}.\end{array}

The usual notation for the algebra
{\mathfrak{aut}}(B_{ev}(m|2n))
preserving a non-degenerate symmetric even form is
{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)
;
sometimes we write more precisely:
{\mathfrak{osp}}^{sy}(m|2n)
.

The anti-symmetric non-degenerate even bilinear form is preserved by the
“symplectic-orthogonal” Lie superalgebra,
{\mathfrak{sp}}{\mathfrak{o}}(2n|m)
or
{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)
, which is isomorphic to

{\mathfrak{osp}}^{sy}(m|2n)
, but has a different matrix realization.
Let
{\mathfrak{o}}(m):={\mathfrak{aut}}(\Pi_{m})
and
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n):={\mathfrak{aut}}(J_{2n})
. In the standard format, we have:

(36)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)=\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}$}\mid A\in{\mathfrak{o}}(m),\ \ D\in{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n),\ \ C=J_{2n}B^{t}\Pi_{m}\ \text{for any}\ B\right\}.

The normal shapes of the odd non-degenerate bilinear
forms in the standard format (NB: the signs are correct) are

(37)

B_{odd}(n|n)=\begin{cases}J_{2n},&\text{if the form is symmetric},\\
\Pi_{2n},&\text{if the form is anti-symmetric}.\\
\end{cases}

The present day usual notation for

{\mathfrak{aut}}(B_{odd}(\text{Size}))
is
{\mathfrak{pe}}(\text{Size})
.
The passage from
V
to
\Pi(V)
establishes an isomorphism

{\mathfrak{pe}}^{sy}(\text{Size})\simeq{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(\text{Size})
. These isomorphic Lie
superalgebras are called, as A. Weil suggested,
13
13

13

“Don’t you
know the ancient Greek?!” he asked.

periplectic
. In the
standard format, the matrix realizations of these superalgebras is:

(38)

\begin{split}{\mathfrak{pe}}^{sy}\ (n)&=\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}\mid A\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n),\,B=-B^{t},\;C=C^{t}\right\};\\
{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)&=\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}\mid A\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n),\,B=B^{t},\;C=-C^{t}\right\}.\end{split}

Note that, although
{\mathfrak{osp}}^{sy}(m|2n)\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)
and
{\mathfrak{pe}}^{sy}(n)\simeq{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)
, certain properties of these incarnations are sometimes
very different, see Subsection
2.8
,
and

[
Sh5
]
.

The
special periplectic
superalgebra is

{\mathfrak{spe}}(n):=\{X\in{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)\mid\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits X=0\}.

Of particular interest to us will be the Lie superalgebras

(39)

{\mathfrak{spe}}_{a,b}(n):={\mathfrak{spe}}(n)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}(aD+bz),\text{~~~
where $z=1_{2n}$, $D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\nolimits(1_{n},-1_{n})$}

and the nontrivial central extension
{\mathfrak{as}}
of
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)
that
will be described after some preparation, see Subsection
2.11
.

2.3.3.
More notation

The tautological representation
of a matrix Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
in
V
, and sometimes
the module
V
itself
, are denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
or, for clarity,

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
. The context prevents confusion of this notation with
that of the identity (scalar) operator
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}
on the space
V
,
as in the next paragraph:

For
{\mathfrak{g}}=\oplus_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, the trivial
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is denoted by
{\mathbb{C}}
(if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is
simple) whereas
{\mathbb{C}}[k]
denotes the representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
that is
trivial on its semi-simple part and such that
k
is the value with which the
central element
z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, where
z
is chosen so that

z|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}}=i\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}}
, acts on
{\mathbb{C}}[k]
.

Projectivization

If
{\mathfrak{c}}
is a Lie algebra of scalar matrices, and
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)
is a Lie subsuperalgebra containing
{\mathfrak{c}}
, then the
projective

Lie superalgebra of type
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
{\mathfrak{pg}}={\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{c}}
. The Lie
superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\bigodot{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}
described in Subsection
4.1.1
are also
projective.

Projectivization sometimes leads to new Lie superalgebras, for
example:
{\mathfrak{pq}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{psq}}(n)
;
{\mathfrak{pgl}}(n|n)
,
{\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)
;
note that
{\mathfrak{pgl}}(p|q)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(p|q)
if
p\neq q
.

2.4.
Simple, almost simple and semi-simple Lie
superalgebras

Recall that any Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
without proper ideals and of dimension
>1
is said to be

simple
. The list (
40
) contains all simple
finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
, for various
subcases of the classification, see
[
K1
,
Kapp
,
K1C
,
SNR
,
Dj1
,
Dj2
,
Dj3
]
; for a
summary ignoring odd parameters of deformations, see
[
K2
,
Sch
]
; for a summary and classification of all deformations, see
[
Ld
]
:

(40)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{${\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)$ for $m>n\geq 1$, ${\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)$ for
$n>1$, ${\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)$ for $mn\neq 0$};\\
\text{${\mathfrak{psq}}(n)$ and ${\mathfrak{spe}}(n)$
for $n>2$;}\\
\text{a 1-parameter family of deformations ${\mathfrak{osp}}_{a}(4|2)$ for $a\neq 0,-1$};\\
\text{exceptional Lie superalgebras ${\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{g}}(2)$ and
${\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}}(3)$;}\\
\text{finite-dimensional vectorial Lie superalgebras, namely}\\
\text{${\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)$ for $n>1$, ${\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)$ and
$\widetilde{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|2n)$ for $n>2$, ${\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)$ for $n>3$;}\\
\widetilde{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|2n+1)\text{~~ for $n\geq 1$; the only (mind \eqref{ocIso}) deformation with an odd parameter.}\\
\end{array}

For descriptions of these algebras, especially those with Cartan
matrices, see
[
BGL
,
CCLL
,
GL1
,
GL3
]
, and
[
K2
,
vdL
]
; for descriptions of the exceptional algebras, see
[
DWN
,
Sud
,
El3
,
BE
]
.

Occasional isomorphisms of finite-dimensional simple Lie superalgebras
:

(41)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(n|m),\ \ {\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(1|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2),\\
{\mathfrak{spe}}(3)\simeq{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|3),\ \ {\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|4).\\
\end{array}

The isomorphisms
{\mathfrak{osp}}_{\alpha}(4|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}_{\alpha^{\prime}}(4|2)
are generated by the
transformations (see
[
BGL
]
):

(42)

\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\prime}:=-1-\alpha\ ,\qquad\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\prime}:=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\alpha}$}}\ ,

so the other isomorphisms are

(43)

\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\prime}:=-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1+\alpha}{\alpha}$}}\ ,\quad\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\prime}:=-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\alpha+1}$}}\ ,\quad\alpha\longmapsto\alpha^{\prime}:=-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\alpha}{\alpha+1}$}}.

We say that a Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
almost simple
if it can be sandwiched
(non-strictly) between a simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{s}}
and the Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{s}}
of derivations of
{\mathfrak{s}}
,
i.e.
,

{\mathfrak{s}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{s}}.

By definition,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
semi-simple
if its
radical
—
the maximal solvable ideal — is
\{0\}
.

Copying R. Block’s description of semi-simple Lie algebras over fields of prime
characteristic we describe
semi-simple Lie superalgebras as follows, cf.
[
K2
]
.

Let
{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},\dots,{\mathfrak{s}}_{k}
be simple Lie superalgebras, let
n_{1},\dots,n_{k}
be
pairs
of nonnegative integers

n_{j}=(n_{j}^{\bar{0}},n_{j}^{\bar{1}})
, let
{\mathcal{F}}(n_{j})
be the supercommutative
superalgebra of polynomials in
n_{j}^{\bar{0}}
even and
n_{j}^{\bar{1}}
odd
indeterminates, and let
{\mathfrak{s}}=\oplus_{j}\left({\mathfrak{s}}_{j}\otimes{\mathcal{F}}(n_{j})\right)
. Then,

(44)

{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{s}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{j}\left(({\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{s}}_{j})\otimes{\mathcal{F}}(n_{j})\ltimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}}\otimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(n_{j})\right).

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{s}}
containing
{\mathfrak{s}}
. If

(45)

the projection of
{\mathfrak{g}}
to

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}}\otimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(n_{j})_{-1}
is
onto
for each
j
,

then
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semi-simple
.

2.4.1.
Conjecture.

If the
(super)algebras
{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}
are finite-dimensional or of polynomial growth, and
the (super)algebras of functions
{\mathcal{F}}(n)
depend on a finite number of indeterminates, then
all semi-simple Lie superalgebras arise in the manner indicated,

i.e.
, as sums of subalgebras of the summands of (
44
)
satisfying condition (
45
), cf.
[
Che
]
.

2.5.
The irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules of least
dimension
\neq 1
over
{\mathfrak{g}}
simple finite-dimensional

Up to the reversal of parity, such modules are: the adjoint one for

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{psq}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)
for
n>2
,
{\mathfrak{ag}}(2)
,
{\mathfrak{ab}}(3)
and
{\mathfrak{osp}}_{a}(4|2)
for
a
generic (
i.e.
,
a\neq 1,2,3
or obtained from such values of
a
under the
S_{3}
-action, see formulas (
43
)).

For
a=1,2,3
, the irreducible modules of least dimension are, up to the reversal of parity
\Pi
, of superdimension

4|2
,
6|4
and
8|6
, respectively, see
[
GL3
]
.
This fact was
first claimed in
[
Kpr
]
without proof. The above claims are now deducible from results of Penkov and Serganova, and of Brundan.

2.6.
Remarks on Lie (super)algebras

1) Not all filtered Lie superalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}
or associated with them
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebras
L:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i>-d}L_{i}
, where
L_{i}:={\mathcal{L}}_{i}/{\mathcal{L}}_{i+1}
,
of finite depth are
vectorial
,
i.e.
, realizable by vector
fields on a supermanifold of the same superdimension as that of

{\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
; only those with faithful
L_{0}
-action on

L_{-}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i<0}\ L_{i}
are.

Unlike Lie algebras, simple infinite-dimensional vectorial
super
algebras can have

several
non-isomorphic maximal subalgebras of finite
codimension
.

2) We denote the vectorial Lie (super)algebras as is customary in differential geometry:
{\mathfrak{vect}}(x)
or
{\mathfrak{vect}}(V)
if
V=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(x)
for
x=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m})
, where the
u_{i}
are even
indeterminates and the
\theta_{j}
are odd ones. We use a similar
notation for the subalgebras of
{\mathfrak{vect}}
introduced below. Some
algebraists sometimes abbreviate
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n):={\mathfrak{vect}}(n|0)
by

W(n)
in honor of Witt
14
14

14

Witt considered a version of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n)
over fields of characteristic
p>0
.
, and its subalgebra
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{vect}}(n)
of divergence-free fields
to
S(n)
, where
S
stands
for “special”. Following Bourbaki, we mostly use Gothic font to name Lie (super)algebras.

3) Considering the subspaces
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
as the basis of a topology,
we can complete the graded or filtered Lie superalgebras
L
or

{\mathcal{L}}
; the elements of the completion are the vector fields with
formal power series as coefficients. Though the structure of the
graded algebras is easier to describe, in applications one usually needs the completed
vectorial Lie superalgebras.

2.6.1.
Traces and divergences on vectorial Lie
superalgebras.

Recall Subsection
1.7.1
.

On any Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
over any field

{\mathbb{K}}
, a
trace
is any linear mapping
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits:{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{K}}
such
that

(46)

\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}^{\prime})=0.

Let
{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{C}}
. Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be the superalgebra of “functions”. Let
{\mathfrak{g}}:={\mathfrak{der}}~\,{\mathcal{F}}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie algebra with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i<0}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
generated by

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, and let
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits
be a trace on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.
Conjecturally
, any

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of a given vectorial Lie algebra is given by the degrees
of the indeterminates with a proof similar to that given in
[
Sk91
,
Sk95
]
.

The
divergence

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits:{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}
is a degree-preserving

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}
-invariant prolongation of the trace on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
satisfying the following conditions,
so
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\in Z^{1}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathcal{F}})
,
i.e.
, is a cocycle:

\begin{array}[]{l}X_{i}(\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D)=\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits([X_{i},D])\text{~~ for all elements
$X_{i}$ that span ${\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}$};\\
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits;\\
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}}=0.\end{array}

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits:={\mathcal{F}}^{*}
be the
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
-module of
volume forms
dual to
{\mathcal{F}}
. As an
{\mathcal{F}}
-module,
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits
is generated by the
volume element

\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}=1^{*}
for
fixed indeterminates (“coordinates”)
x
which we often do not
indicate. On the rank-1
{\mathcal{F}}
-module of
weighted

\lambda
-densities
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(m|n)
with generator

\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}^{\lambda}
over
{\mathcal{F}}
, the
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
-action is given for any
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
and
D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
by the

Lie derivative

(47)

L_{D}(f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}^{\lambda})=\left(D(f)+(-1)^{p(f)p(D)}\lambda f\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits(D)\right)\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}^{\lambda}.

Often by a
volume form
we mean only elements
f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}
, where
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
, such that
f(0)\neq 0
.

2.7.
Examples of vectorial Lie superalgebras

1) General vectorial Lie superalgebras
. Let
x=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n},\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m})
. The
the general vectorial
Lie superalgebra
is defined to be
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m):={\mathfrak{der}}~\;{\mathbb{C}}[x]
.

2) Special Lie superalgebras
. The

divergence
of the field

D=\mathop{\sum}_{i}f_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{u_{i}}}}+\mathop{\sum}_{j}g_{j}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}
is the function

(48)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}{\frac{\partial{f_{i}}}{\partial{u_{i}}}}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j}(-1)^{p(g_{j})}{\frac{\partial{g_{i}}}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}.

\bullet
The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m):=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)\mid\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D=0\}
is called the
special
(or

divergence-free
)
vectorial superalgebra
.

It is clear that one can also describe
{\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m)
as

\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)\mid L_{D}\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}=0\}
.

\bullet
The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n):=[{\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n),{\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n)]
is called the
traceless special vectorial
superalgebra
.

\bullet
The deformation of
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
is the Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{svect}}_{\lambda}(0|m)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)\mid\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits(1+\lambda\theta_{1}\cdot\ldots\cdot\theta_{m})D=0\},

where
p(\lambda)\equiv m\pmod{2}
and if
p(\lambda)={\bar{1}}
, then
{\mathfrak{svect}}_{\lambda}(0|m)
is considered over
{\mathbb{C}}[\lambda]
. It is called the
deformed
special
(or
divergence-free
)
vectorial
superalgebra
. Clearly,
{\mathfrak{svect}}_{\lambda}(0|m)\simeq{\mathfrak{svect}}_{\mu}(0|m)
if
\lambda\neq 0
and
\mu\neq 0
. Accordingly, we briefly denote
these deformations by
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|m)
, see Subsection
1.10.4.  Proposition
.

3) Lie superalgebras that preserve Pfaff equations or, equivalently, non-integrable distributions, contact and
symplectic forms
.

\bullet
Recall that a
symplectic form
is a non-degenerate closed differential 2-form
\omega
. In agreement with the normal shapes of the bilinear forms (
35
) and (
37
), the descriptions of normal shapes of the symplectic forms (Darboux’s theorem, when on manifolds) were formulated in
[
L1
]
; this claim was proved in
[
Shan
]
. The obstruction to reducing the non-degenerate differential 2-form
\omega
to the normal shape is
d\omega
, see
[
BGLS
]
. If
\omega
is odd, it is called, as A. Weil advised,
periplectic
.

\bullet
The
contact form
(resp.,
pericontact form
) is a nowhere vanishing differential 1-form
\alpha
on a (super)manifold
M
that singles out in the tangent bundle
TM
a
maximally
non-integrable distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
of codimension 1 (resp.,
\varepsilon=0|1
),
i.e.
, at every point
m\in M
, there are given

(49)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{a~subspace ${\mathcal{D}}_{m}$ of codimension 1 (resp., $\varepsilon=0|1$) in the tangent space $T_{m}M$, and}\\
\text{a~differential 1-form $\alpha$ such that $d\alpha|_{{\mathcal{D}}_{m}}$ is non-degenerate.}\end{array}

Note that in Differential Geometry on odd-dimensional manifolds (over the ground field
{\mathbb{K}}
), the contact form is often defined as
a nowhere vanishing differential 1-form
\alpha
such that

(50)

\alpha\wedge(d\alpha)^{\wedge n}=\lambda\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}
for some
\lambda\in{\mathbb{K}}
.

Obviously, this definition cannot be superized since on supermanifolds there are no differential forms of highest degree. Condition (
50
) is a
property
of the contact form on a manifold, whereas (
49
) is a reasonable definition.

\bullet
Let
u=(t,p_{1},\dots,p_{n},q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
be even indeterminates and
\Theta=(\theta_{1},\dots,\theta_{m})
be odd indeterminates. The form

(51)

\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}=dt+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}(p_{i}dq_{i}-q_{i}dp_{i})\ +\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq j\leq m}\theta_{j}d\theta_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{\omega}_{0}:=d\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}

is called
contact
, and the form

\widetilde{\omega}_{0}
is called
symplectic
. Sometimes
it is more convenient to redenote the
\theta_{j}
and set

\Theta=\begin{cases}(\xi,\eta),&\text{ if }\ m=2k\\
(\xi,\eta,\theta),&\text{ if }\ m=2k+1,\end{cases}
where

(52)

\xi_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta_{j}-\sqrt{-1}\theta_{k+j}),\quad\eta_{j}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\theta_{j}+\sqrt{-1}\theta_{k+j})\;\text{~
for~}\;j\leq k=\lfloor\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}\rfloor,\;\;\theta=\theta_{2k+1},

and in place of
\widetilde{\omega}_{0}
or
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
take

\alpha_{1}
and
\omega_{0}=d\alpha_{1}
, respectively, where
(we mostly consider the cases where
\ell=0
or
1
)

(53)

\alpha_{1}=dt+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}(p_{i}dq_{i}-q_{i}dp_{i})+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq j\leq k}(\xi_{j}d\eta_{j}+\eta_{j}d\xi_{j})+\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }\ m=2k\\
\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq s\leq\ell}\theta_{s}d\theta_{s},&\text{ if }\ m=2k+\ell.\end{cases}

The Lie superalgebra that preserves the distribution given by the
Pfaff equation

(54)

\tilde{\alpha}_{1}(X)=0
for
X\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(2n+1|m)

(or by an equivalent equation
\alpha_{1}(X)=0
),

i.e.
, the superalgebra

(55)

{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m):=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(2n+1|m)\mid L_{D}\alpha_{1}=f_{D}\alpha_{1}\text{ for some }f_{D}\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\theta]\},

is called the
contact superalgebra
. The divergence-free subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
is the Lie superalgebra

(56)

{\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)\mid L_{D}\alpha_{1}=0\}.

It is called the
Poisson
superalgebra. (The Poisson superalgebra can be interpreted as
the Lie superalgebra that, over a symplectic supermanifold with the
symplectic form
d\alpha_{1}
, preserves the connection with form

\alpha_{1}
and curvature proportional to
d\alpha_{1}
in a line bundle.)

The above “symmetric” expression of
\alpha_{1}
is popular among
algebraists. However,
in analytical mechanics and differential geometry, the following
expression of the form
\alpha_{1}
(without odd coordinates, of
course) naturally appears:

(57)

\alpha_{1(2)}=dt-\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq n}p_{i}dq_{i}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq j\leq k}\xi_{j}d\eta_{j}+\begin{cases}0,&\text{ if }\ m=2k\\
\theta d\theta,&\text{ if }\ m=2k+1.\end{cases}

The form
\alpha_{1(2)}
is the only reasonable expression of the
contact form over fields of characteristic 2, whereas the
symmetric expression of the contact forms is inapplicable:

d\alpha_{1}=2\omega=0
.

\bullet
Similarly, let
u=q=(q_{1},\dots,q_{n})
and
\theta=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n};\tau)
be even
and odd indeterminates, respectively. Set

(58)

\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{0}=d\tau+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}(\xi_{i}dq_{i}+q_{i}d\xi_{i}),\qquad\qquad\omega_{1}:=d\alpha_{0}\end{array}

and call the form
\omega_{1}
, as A. Weil advised,
periplectic
; accordingly, we call
\alpha_{0}

pericontact
.

The Lie superalgebra that preserves the distribution given by the Pfaff equation

(59)

\alpha_{0}(X)=0
for
X\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|n+1)
,

i.e.
, the superalgebra

(60)

{\mathfrak{m}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|n+1)\mid L_{D}\alpha_{0}=f_{D}\cdot\alpha_{0}\text{ for some }\;f_{D}\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi,\tau]\},

is called the
pericontact superalgebra
.
15
15

15

To call
{\mathfrak{m}}
“odd contact
superalgebra” is misleading, since the even part of
{\mathfrak{m}}
is non-zero.
To say “the “odd” contact superalgebra” is OK, though a bit too long, and the pericontact form is even.

2.7.1.
On normal shapes of contact and symplectic/periplectic forms.

The
restriction of
\omega_{0}
to
\ker\alpha_{1}
is the orthosymplectic
form
B_{ev}(m|2n)
; the restriction of
\widetilde{\omega}_{0}
to

\ker\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
is
B^{\prime}_{ev}(m|2n)
. Similarly, the
restriction of
\omega_{1}
to
\ker\alpha_{0}
is
B_{odd}(n|n)
. Clearly,
\ker\alpha_{1}=\ker\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
.

There is just one class of contact forms (both even and odd), whereas the obstruction to reducibility of the non-degenerate bilinear form
\omega
(even or odd)
to the normal shape is
d\omega
, see
[
BGLS
]
.

2.7.2.
Generating functions: contact series and their subalgebras

A laconic way to describe
{\mathfrak{k}}
,

{\mathfrak{m}}
and their subalgebras is via generating functions. We express the contact fields of series
{\mathfrak{k}}
(resp.,
{\mathfrak{m}}
) in natural bases

K_{f}
(resp.,
M_{f}
).

\bullet

Odd form
\alpha_{1}
. For any
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\theta]
, set

(61)

K_{f}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}}-H_{f}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}E,

where
E=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}y_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{y_{i}}}}
(here the
y_{i}

are all the coordinates except
t
) is the
Euler operator
,
and
H_{f}
is the Hamiltonian field with Hamiltonian
f
that
preserves
d\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
:

(62)

H_{f}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\right)-(-1)^{p(f)}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq m}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}\right).

The choice of the form
\alpha_{1}
instead of
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}

affects only the shape of
H_{f}
which we give for
m=2k+\ell
:

(63)

H_{f}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\right)-(-1)^{p(f)}\left(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq k}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\right)+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq\ell}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}\right).

The expression of the contact field corresponding to the form

\alpha_{1}
or
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
is

(64)

K_{f}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial}{\partial{t}}}-H_{f}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}E,

where
E=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}p_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{p_{i}}}}+\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j}\xi_{j}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}
, and
H_{f}

is the Hamiltonian field with Hamiltonian
f
that preserves

d\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
, see (
62
).

\bullet

Even form
\alpha_{0}
. For any
f\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi,\tau]
, set

(65)

M_{f}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial}{\partial{\tau}}}-Le_{f}-(-1)^{p(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}E,

where
E=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i}y_{i}{\frac{\partial}{\partial{y_{i}}}}
(here the
y_{i}
are
all the coordinates except
\tau
), and

(66)

Le_{f}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\right).

Since

(67)

\begin{array}[]{l}L_{K_{f}}(\alpha_{1})=2{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}\alpha_{1}=K_{1}(f)\alpha_{1},\\
L_{M_{f}}(\alpha_{0})=-(-1)^{p(f)}2{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\tau}}}\alpha_{0}=-(-1)^{p(f)}M_{1}(f)\alpha_{0},\end{array}

it follows that
K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
and
M_{f}\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
.
Note that

p(Le_{f})=p(M_{f})=p(f)+{\bar{1}}.

\bullet
To the (super)commutators
[K_{f},K_{g}]
and
[M_{f},M_{g}]

there correspond
contact brackets
of the generating functions:

\begin{array}[]{l}{}[K_{f},K_{g}]=K_{\{f,\;g\}_{k.b.}}\text{~~and~~}{}[M_{f},M_{g}]=M_{\{f,\;g\}_{m.b.}}.\end{array}

To give the explicit formulas for the contact brackets, we first define the brackets on functions that do not depend on
t

(resp.,
\tau
).

The
Poisson bracket

\{-,-\}_{P.b.}
in the
realization with the form
\widetilde{\omega}_{0}
is given by the
formula

(68)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{f,g\}_{P.b.}&=\mathop{\sum}_{i\leq n}\ \bigg({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}{\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\bigg)-\\
&(-1)^{p(f)}\mathop{\sum}_{j\leq m}\ {\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\theta_{j}}}}\text{ for any }f,g\in{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\theta]\end{array}

and in the realization with the form
\omega_{0}
for
m=2k+1
it is
given by the formula

(69)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\{f,g\}_{P.b.}&=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\ \bigg({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}-\ {\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{p_{i}}}}\bigg)-\\
&(-1)^{p(f)}\bigg(\mathop{\sum}\limits_{j\leq m}\left({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\eta_{j}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\right)+{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\theta}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\theta}}}\bigg)\text{ for any }f,g\in{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi,\eta,\theta].\end{array}

The
Buttin bracket

\{-,-\}_{B.b.}
is given by the formula

(70)

\{f,g\}_{B.b.}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\ \bigg({\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}\ {\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}\ {\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{q_{i}}}}\bigg)\text{ for any }f,g\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi].

In terms of the Poisson and Buttin brackets,
respectively, the (peri)contact brackets are

(71)

\{f,g\}_{k.b.}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{t}}}-{\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{t}}}(2-E)(g)-\{f,g\}_{P.b.}

and

(72)

\{f,g\}_{m.b.}=(2-E)(f){\frac{\partial{g}}{\partial{\tau}}}+(-1)^{p(f)}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}(2-E)(g)-\{f,g\}_{B.b.}.

2.7.3.
Divergence-free subalgebras

Since, as is easy to
calculate,

(73)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits K_{f}=(2n+2-m)K_{1}(f),

it follows that the divergence-free subalgebra of the contact Lie
superalgebra either coincides with the whole algebra (for
m=2n+2
)
or is the
Poisson
superalgebra., cf. (
56
)

(74)

{\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m):=\{K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)\mid K_{1}(f)=0\}.

Since for
(n|m)\neq(0|2)
the codimension of
({\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)_{0})^{\prime}
in
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)_{0}
is equal to 1, there is exactly one (up to a non-zero factor) trace on
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)_{0}
. Hence, there should be exactly one (up to a non-zero factor) intrinsic divergence on
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
, see
[
BGLLS
]
.
Setting
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits(K_{t})=1
, the divergence is given by
\partial_{t}
for any
n
and
m
. For
m\neq 2n+2
, this intrinsic divergence coincides with the restriction to
{\mathfrak{k}}
of the divergence on
{\mathfrak{vect}}
.

The case of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)

Although
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)
is not W-graded, let us consider it for completeness. This case is exceptional: the algebra
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)_{0}
of degree-0 elements is commutative and
\dim{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)_{0}=2
, so there are 2 traces on
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)_{0}
, and hence 2 divergences on
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)
.
Let
\alpha_{1}:=dt+\xi d\eta+\eta d\xi
. To the operators

\tilde{K}_{\xi}(f)=(-1)^{p(f)}(\partial_{\eta}-\xi\partial_{t})(f),\ \tilde{K}_{\eta}(f)=(-1)^{p(f)}(\partial_{\xi}-\eta\partial_{t})(f)\text{~~and~~}\tilde{K}_{1}=\partial_{t}

there corresponds the second divergence

(75)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits_{2}:=\tilde{K}_{\eta}\tilde{K}_{\xi}-\tilde{K}_{1}.

Let
\beta
be the symbol of the class of a pseudo-differential form
16
16

16

If the reader wishes to “visualize” this form, one can consider
\beta:=[d\xi(d\eta)^{-1}]
. Actually, the expression of
\beta
in terms of
d\xi
and
d\eta
is irrelevant, we need to know only how the Lie derivative acts on it.
on which the Lie derivative acts as follows

(76)

\begin{array}[]{l}L_{K_{f}}(\alpha_{1}^{a}\beta^{b})=(2a\partial_{t}(f)+(-1)^{p(f)}b\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits_{2}(K_{f}))(\alpha_{1}^{a}\beta^{b})\\
=((2a-(-1)^{p(f)}b)\partial_{t}(f)+(-1)^{p(f)}b\tilde{K}_{\eta}\tilde{K}_{\xi}(f))(\alpha_{1}^{a}\beta^{b}).\end{array}

Let the space
{\mathcal{F}}_{a,b}
of weighted densities over
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2)
be defined as the rank-1
{\mathcal{F}}
-module generated by
\alpha^{a/2}_{1}\beta^{b}
. (For the modified formula (
76
) when
\mathop{\text{\rm char}}\nolimits{\mathbb{K}}=2
, see
[
BGLLS
]
.)

The pericontact series

Here, the
situation is more interesting: the divergence-free subalgebra is
simple.

Since the restriction of the divergence from
{\mathfrak{vect}}
to
{\mathfrak{m}}
is equal to

(77)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits M_{f}=(-1)^{p(f)}2\left((1-E)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}-\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}\right),

it follows that the divergence-free subalgebra of the pericontact
superalgebra is

(78)

{\mathfrak{sm}}(n)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\left(M_{f}\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\mid(1-E)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}\right).

In particular,

(79)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits Le_{f}=(-1)^{p(f)}2\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}.

The odd analog of the Laplacian, namely, the operator

(80)

\Delta:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}

on a periplectic supermanifold appeared in physics under the name of

BRST operator
, cf.
[
GPS
]
, or
Batalin-Vilkovysky
operator
. Observe that
\Delta
is the Fourier transform (with
respect to the “ghost indeterminates”
\check{x}
(the odd ones, if
considered on manifolds) of the exterior differential
d
.

The divergence-free vector fields from
{\mathfrak{sle}}(n)
are generated by

harmonic
functions,
i.e.
, such that
\Delta(f)=0
.

The Lie superalgebra

(81)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathfrak{b}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\mid L_{D}\alpha_{0}=0\}\end{array}

is called the
Buttin
superalgebra in honor of C. Buttin, see Section
2.7.4
. (A geometric interpretation: over a
periplectic
supermanifold,
i.e.
, over
a supermanifold with the periplectic form
d\alpha_{0}
, the Buttin
superalgebra is the Lie superalgebra that preserves the
connection with form
\alpha_{0}
and curvature proportional to
d\alpha_{0}
in a line bundle of superrank
\varepsilon=(0|1)
.)

The Lie superalgebras

(82)

\begin{array}[]{c}{\mathfrak{sm}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\mid\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\ D=0\},\\
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}(n)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{b}}(n)\mid\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\ D=0\}\end{array}

are called the
divergence-free
(or
special
)

pericontact
and
special Buttin
superalgebras,
respectively.

Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{sle}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n)

have traceless ideals
{\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)
of codimension 1 (
i.e.
,
\varepsilon^{n}
,
\varepsilon^{n+1}
and
n
, respectively) defined from the exact sequences

(83)

\begin{array}[]{c}0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sle}}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}}\longrightarrow 0,\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot M_{\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}}\longrightarrow 0,\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}\partial_{t}\longrightarrow 0.\end{array}

The Lie superalgebra of
Hamiltonian
fields
(also called
Hamiltonian
superalgebra
) and its traceless subalgebra (defined only if
n=0
)
are

(84)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m):=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(2n|m)\mid L_{D}\omega_{0}=0),\\
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|m):=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(H_{f}\in{\mathfrak{h}}(0|m)\mid\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\theta}=0).\end{array}

The “odd” analogues of the Lie superalgebra of Hamiltonian
fields are the Lie superalgebra of vector fields
\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{f}

introduced in
[
L1
]
, and its special (divergence-free) subalgebra:

(85)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{le}}(n):=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|n)\mid L_{D}\omega_{1}=0),\\
{\mathfrak{sle}}(n):=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(D\in{\mathfrak{le}}(n)\mid\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D=0).\end{array}

It is not difficult to prove the following isomorphisms (as superspaces):

(86)

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)&\simeq&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(K_{f}\mid f\in{\mathbb{C}}[t,p,q,\xi]),\\
{\mathfrak{le}}(n)&\simeq&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(Le_{f}\mid f\in{\mathbb{C}}[q,\xi]),\\
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)&\simeq&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(M_{f}\mid f\in{\mathbb{C}}[\tau,q,\xi]),\\
{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)&\simeq&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(H_{f}\mid f\in{\mathbb{C}}[p,q,\xi]).\end{array}

We see that

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{po}}(0|m)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{k}}(1|m)\mid K_{1}(f)=0),\\
{\mathfrak{po}}^{\prime}(0|m)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(K_{f}\in{\mathfrak{po}}(0|m)\mid\int f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\theta}=0),\\
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|m)\simeq{\mathfrak{po}}^{\prime}(0|m)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot K_{1}.\end{array}

2.7.4.
On the history of the antibracket.

What we call here
the “Buttin bracket” was discovered in pre-super era by Schouten;
Buttin was the first to prove that this bracket satisfies the super anti-commutativity and Jacobi identities, see
[
Bu
]
. The interpretations of the Buttin
superalgebra similar to that of the Poisson algebra and of the
elements of
{\mathfrak{le}}
as analogs of Hamiltonian vector fields was given
in
[
L1
]
. The Buttin bracket and “odd mechanics” introduced in

[
L1
]
were rediscovered by Batalin with Vilkovisky (and, even
earlier, by Zinn-Justin, but his papers went even more unnoticed, see

[
FLSf
]
, than
[
L1
]
). The Schouten bracket gained a great deal of currency under the name

antibracket
, cf.
[
GPS
]
. The
Schouten bracket
=
Antibracket
=
Buttin bracket

was originally defined on the superspace of multi-vector fields on
a manifold,
i.e.
, on the superspace of sections of the exterior
algebra (over the algebra
{\mathcal{F}}
of functions) of the tangent bundle,

\Gamma(\Lambda^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(T(M)))\simeq\Lambda^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}_{\mathcal{F}}(Vect(M))
.
The explicit formula for the Schouten bracket (hereafter, the hatted
slot should be ignored, as usual) is (mind the Sign Rule!)

(87)

\begin{array}[]{c}[X_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\dots\wedge X_{k},Y_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge Y_{l}]=\\
\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i,j}(-1)^{k-j+i-1}[X_{i},Y_{j}]\wedge X_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\widehat{X}_{i}\wedge\dots\wedge X_{k}\wedge Y_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge\widehat{Y}_{j}\wedge\dots\wedge Y_{l}.\end{array}

With the help of the Sign Rule we easily superize formula
(
87
),
i.e.
, replace any manifold
M
by a supermanifold
{\mathcal{M}}
. Let
x
and

\xi
be the even and odd coordinates on
{\mathcal{M}}
. Setting

(88)

\theta_{i}=\Pi\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}\right)=\check{x}_{i},\quad q_{j}=\Pi\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{j}}}}\right)=\check{\xi}_{j}

we get an identification of the Schouten bracket of multi-vector
fields on
{\mathcal{M}}
with the Buttin bracket of functions on the
supermanifold
\check{\mathcal{M}}
with coordinates
x,\xi
and
\check{x}
,

\check{\xi}
, and the transformation rule of the checked variables
induced by that of unchecked ones via (
88
). Grozman discovered a deformation of the Schouten bracket, see
[
G
]
, but his result was not understood at that time.

2.7.5.
The explicit form of the
pericontact bracket

Set
F_{x}:={\frac{\partial}{\partial{x}}}F
. Let
M_{F}\alpha
be a short for
L_{M_{F}}\alpha
.
Since
M_{F}\alpha_{0}=(-1)^{p(F)+1}2F_{\tau}\alpha_{0}
, it follows
that

(89)

\begin{array}[]{l}(M_{F}M_{G}-(-1)^{(p(F)+1)(p(G)+1)}M_{G}M_{F})\alpha_{0}=\\
\{(-1)^{p(G)+1}2[(\xi F_{\tau}+F_{q})G_{\xi\tau}+(-1)^{p(F)}(F_{\xi}-qF_{\tau})G_{q\tau}+(-1)^{p(G)(p(F)+1)}2G_{\tau}F_{\tau}]\\
-(-1)^{(p(F)+1)p(G)}2[(\xi G_{\tau}+G_{q})F_{\xi\tau}+(-1)^{p(G)}(G_{\xi}-qG_{\tau})F_{q\tau}+(-1)^{p(F)(p(G)+1)}2F_{\tau}G_{\tau}]\}\alpha_{0}.\end{array}

On the other hand, we have

(90)

((2-E)(F)G_{\tau})_{\tau}=2F_{\tau}G_{\tau}+\xi F_{\tau\xi}G_{\tau}-qF_{\tau q}G_{\tau};\

(91)

(F_{\tau}(2-E)(G))_{\tau}=(-1)^{p(F)+1}(2F_{\tau}G_{\tau}+F_{\tau}\xi G_{\tau\xi}-F_{\tau}qG_{\tau q});\

(92)

(F_{q}G_{\xi}+(-1)^{p(F)}F_{\xi}G_{q})_{\tau}=F_{\tau q}G_{q}+(-1)^{p(F)}F_{q}G_{\tau\xi}-F_{\xi}G_{\tau q}.

Clearly,

(-1)^{p(F)p(G)}~\eqref{2.3.11}+(-1)^{p(G)}~\eqref{2.3.12}+(-1)^{p(F)+p(G)}~\eqref{2.3.13}=\{\cdots\},

where
\{\cdots\}
is the coefficient of
\alpha_{0}
in the right-hand
side of (
89
). Therefore,

\{F,G\}_{m.b.}=(-1)^{p(F)+p(G)}\left((2-E)(F)G_{\tau}+(-1)^{p(F)}F_{\tau}(2-E)(G)+\{F,G\}_{\text{B.b.}}\right).

The arguments for the contact bracket are similar (with
\alpha_{0}

replaced with
\alpha_{1}
or
\widetilde{\alpha}_{1}
, and the field
M_{f}

with
K_{f}
, respectively).

2.8.
Cartan prolongations

Recall that the graded Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{b}}=\oplus_{k\geqslant-d}\ {\mathfrak{b}}_{k}

is said to be
transitive
if for all
k\geq 0
we have

\{x\in{\mathfrak{b}}_{k}\mid[x,{\mathfrak{b}}_{-}]=0\}=0,\text{~~where ${\mathfrak{b}}_{-}:=\oplus_{k<0}\,{\mathfrak{b}}_{k}$}.

Let

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-d\leq i\leq-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be a nilpotent

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie (super)algebra and let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
be a Lie sub(super)algebra of the Lie
(super)algebra
\mathfrak{der}_{0}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-})
of degree-0 derivations of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
.
The maximal transitive
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}=\oplus_{k\geqslant-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
whose non-positive part is the given
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is called the
Cartan prolong
— the result of Cartan prolongation — of the pair
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
and is denoted by
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
.

We can realize
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
by elements of negative degree of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
by degree-0 elements in
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})
in a non-standard (see Subsection
2.9
) grading
\vec{r}
of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)
, where
n|m={\rm sdim}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
. Then, the degree-
k
components of the Cartan prolongation
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{k\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{k}

of the pair
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
are obtained for any
k>0
by setting

{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}:=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\vec{r})_{k}\mid[D,{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}]\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{k+i}\text{~~for any $i<0$}\}.

In what follows
{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}
in superscript denotes, as is now
customary, the collection of all degrees, while
*
is reserved for
dualization; in the subscripts we retain the old-fashioned
*
instead
of
{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.72218pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}
to avoid confusion with the punctuation
marks.

Suppose that the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is
faithful
.
Then, clearly,

(93)

\begin{array}[]{ll}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}&\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)={\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},\theta_{1},\dots\theta_{m}],\;\text{ where }\;n|m=\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits~{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\;\text{ and }\\
&{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)\mid\deg D=i,[D,X]\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{i-1}\text{ for any
}X\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\}.\end{array}

It is subject to an easy verification that the Lie algebra structure
on
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)
induces a Lie algebra structure on
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
; actually,
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
has a natural Lie superalgebra
structure even if the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is not faithful.

2.8.1.
Partial Cartan prolongation: involving
positive components

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
be
a proper
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-submodule such that
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}]={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. If such

{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
exists (usually,
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}]\subsetneq{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
), define
the 2nd prolongation of
(\mathop{\oplus}_{i\leq 0}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i})\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
to be

{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}:=\{D\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{2}\mid[D,{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}]\subset{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\}.

The terms
{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
, where
i>2
, are similarly defined by induction. Set

{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}:={\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
for
i\leq 0
and call
{\mathfrak{h}}_{*}:=\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
the
partial Cartan prolong involving positive components
.

The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|n;n)
is a subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)
. The former is obtained as the Cartan prolong of the same
nonpositive part as
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)
and a submodule of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)_{1}
. The simple exceptional superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
introduced in

[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
, see also line
N=12
in Table (
131
), is
another example.

2.8.2.
Lie superalgebras of vector fields as Cartan
prolongs

The superization of the constructions from
Subsection
2.8
are straightforward, via the Sign Rule. We thus get Lie superalgebras

(94)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n))_{*},\\
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))_{*},\\
{\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n))_{*},\\
{\mathfrak{le}}(n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n))_{*},\\
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{le}}(n)=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{spe}}^{a}(n))_{*}.\\
\end{array}

For an algorithm for constructing prolongs, see
[
Shch
]
; it is implemented in the code package
SuperLie
, see
[
Gr
]
.

Note that
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{osp}}^{sy}(m|2n))_{*}
and
(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{pe}}^{sy}(n))_{*}
are finite-dimensional, unlike

(\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits),{\mathfrak{osp}}^{sy}(m|2n))_{*}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n))_{*}
and
(\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits),{\mathfrak{pe}}^{sy}(n))_{*}=(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n))_{*}
.

There are
two
superizations of the contact
series:
{\mathfrak{k}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
. Let us describe them again.

\bullet
Let the space of the Heisenberg Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
be the direct
sum of a
(2n,m)
-dimensional superspace
W
endowed with
a non-degenerate anti-symmetric
even
bilinear form
B
and a
(1,0)
-dimensional space spanned by
z
. Define the bracket in
{\mathfrak{hei}}
by the formula

(95)

z
is in the center and
[v,w]=B(v,w)\cdot z
for any
v,w\in W
.

Clearly,

(96)

{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)=({\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m),{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)))_{*}\,.

More generally, given
{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
and a subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of

{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n))
, we call
({\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m),{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}
the

k
-prolong
of
(W,{\mathfrak{g}})
, where
W
is the tautological

{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)={\mathfrak{osp}}(W)
-module.

\bullet
The “odd” analog of
{\mathfrak{k}}
is associated with the
following “odd” analog of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(2n|m)
. Denote by
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
the

antibracket
Lie superalgebra (
{\mathfrak{ba}}
stands for Anti-Bracket read
backwards) whose space is
W\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot z
, where
W
is an

n|n
-dimensional superspace endowed with a non-degenerate
anti-symmetric
odd
bilinear form
B
and
z
is odd; the bracket in
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
is
given by the relations

(97)

z
lies in the center and
[v,w]=B(v,w)\cdot z
for any
v,w\in W
.

Clearly,

(98)

{\mathfrak{m}}(n)=({\mathfrak{ba}}(n),{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)))_{*}\,.

More generally, given
{\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
and a subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of
{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)
, we
call
({\mathfrak{ba}}(n),{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}
the
m
-prolong
of
(W,{\mathfrak{g}})
,
where
W
is the tautological
{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)={\mathfrak{pe}}(W)
-module.

Generally, given a non-degenerate
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant form
B
on a superspace
W
and a
Lie subsuperalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{aut}}(B)
, we call any of the above
generalized prolongations (
96
) and (
98
) the
mk
-prolongation
of the pair
(W,{\mathfrak{g}})
and denote it by

(W,{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}^{mk}
.

On the history of Cartan prolongations

The above-described procedure is sometimes called a
generalized
prolongation because the initial Cartan prolongation was defined for
d=1
only. Or rather it was customary to write so without reading works by Cartan and Killing who did consider the above-described generalized Cartan prolongation, see
[
Cart
,
Ki
,
Co
]
.

In the non-super setting, the “generalized” Cartan prolongation was introduced by Cartan (see
[
Cart
]
) or, perhaps, Killing (see
[
Ki
]
). The Cartan prolongation was rediscovered in two steps by Tanaka
[
T
]
, see
[
Y
]
.
The emphasis of these
papers is different from ours, this delayed recognition of the similarity of constructions. The
partial prolongation seems to be first described in
[
ALSh
,
Sh14
]
. It also appears in the classification of simple Lie (super)algebras in characteristic
p>0
, see
[
BGLLS
]
, where the correct definition of Cartan prolongation in characteristic
p>0
was first given, after several approximations to the truth in earlier papers.

2.9.
Nonstandard realizations

The Step 7 of our proof (see Subsection
1.8
) implies that the nonstandard
gradings listed in table (

) exhaust all the W-gradings of all the simple vectorial

{\mathfrak{vect}}

{\mathfrak{svect}}

{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}

{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{b}}
