2.10.
The modules of tensor fields

To advance
further, we have to recall the definition of the modules of tensor
fields over
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
and its subalgebras, see
[
BL4
,
L2
,
GLS3
]
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
. For any other
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded vectorial Lie superalgebra
for whose component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
the notion of lowest weight can be
defined, the construction is identical.

Let
V
be the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module with the
lowest
weight

\lambda=\mathop{\mathrm{lwt}}\nolimits(V)
and
even
lowest weight vector. Make
V
into a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq}
-module by setting

{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}\cdot V=0
for
{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i>0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
. Let us realize
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}
by vector fields on the linear
supermanifold
{\mathcal{C}}^{m|n}
corresponding to the superspace
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i<0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
with coordinates
x=(u,\xi)
. The
superspace
T(V):=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{U({\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq})}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),V)
is isomorphic, due
to the Poincaré–Birkhoff–Witt theorem, to
{{\mathbb{C}}}[[x]]\otimes V
. Its elements admit a natural interpretation as formal

tensor fields of type

V
. When
\lambda=(a,\dots,a)
we
will simply write
T(\vec{a})
instead of
T(\lambda)
. We will
usually need
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules coinduced from
irreducible

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-modules.

2.10.1.
Examples.

As
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
- and
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
-modules,

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)=T(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
.

T(\vec{0})
is the superspace of functions;

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(m|n):=T(\underbrace{1,\dots,1}_{m};\underbrace{-1,\dots,-1}_{n})
, where the semicolon separates
the first
m
(“even”) coordinates of the weight with respect to
the matrix units
E_{ii}
of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)
, is the superspace of

volume forms
, see
[
LSoS
, Ch.4]
or
[
Del
, Ch.1, §1.11]
.

We denote the generator of
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(m|n)
, considered as a
{\mathbb{C}}[[x]]
- or
{\mathbb{C}}[x]
-module,
corresponding to the ordered set of coordinates
x
by
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(x)
or
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}
.
The space of
\lambda
-densities is

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(m|n):=T(\underbrace{\lambda,\dots,\lambda}_{m};\underbrace{-\lambda,\dots,-\lambda}_{n}).

In particular,

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(m|0)=T(\vec{\lambda})
, whereas

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(0|n)=T(\overrightarrow{-\lambda})
. In dimension
(0|n)
, we set

(102)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n):=\{v\in\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n)\mid\int v=0\},\\
T_{0}(\vec{0}):=\Lambda(n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1.\end{array}

As
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
-modules,
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits\simeq T(\vec{0})
. So,
we can define the
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
-module

(103)

T^{0}_{0}(\vec{0}):=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)/{\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}.

Remark: Volume element as a quotient

To view the volume element
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(x)
as “
d^{m}ud^{n}\xi
”
is wrong, but in some works this wrong notation is used to this day. Observe that the superdeterminant of the Jacobi matrix can never appear as a factor
under the changes of “variables”,
i.e.
, indeterminates, in “
d^{m}ud^{n}\xi
”. We can try to use the usual notation
of differentials provided
all
the differentials
anticommute and
\deg du_{i}=1
while
\deg d\xi_{j}=-1
for all
i,j
. Then, at least the linear transformations that do not
intermix the even
u
’s with the odd
\xi
’s multiply the volume
element
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}
, viewed as the fraction
\frac{du_{1}\cdot...\cdot du_{m}}{d\xi_{1}\cdot...\cdot d\xi_{n}}
, by the correct factor, the
Berezinian
\mathop{\mathrm{ber}}\nolimits(A)
, see
[
Lber
]
, of the transformation
A
. But how to justify this? Let

x:=(u,\xi)
. If we consider the exterior differential
forms, then the
dX_{i}
’s super anticommute, hence, the
d\xi_{i}

commute and so should
(d\xi_{i})^{-1}
; whereas if we consider the
symmetric
product of the differentials, as in metrics, then the
dx_{i}
’s
supercommute, hence, the
du_{i}
commute, so neither the exterior, nor the
symmetric product is what we need: all factors should anticommute.

Note that the
{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}
anticommute, and moreover, from the point
of view of linear transformations,

{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}=\frac{1}{d\xi_{i}}
. The expression
du_{1}\cdot...\cdot du_{m}\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{1}}}}\cdot\ldots\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{n}}}}
is,
nevertheless, still wrong: many transformations
A:(u,\xi)\longmapsto(v,\eta)
send
du_{1}\cdot....\cdot du_{m}\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{1}}}}\cdot...\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{n}}}}
to the correct
element,
\mathop{\mathrm{ber}}\nolimits(A)(dv_{1}\cdot...\cdot dv_{m}\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{1}}}}\cdot...\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{n}}}})
plus extra terms. Indeed, the term

dv_{1}\cdot...\cdot dv_{m}\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{1}}}}\cdot...\cdot{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\eta_{n}}}}
is the highest weight vector of an

indecomposable

{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)
-module and
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{x}:=\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(u,\xi)
is the
notation of the image of this vector in the 1-dimensional quotient
module by the invariant submodule that consists precisely of all
the extra terms. Deligne, Witten, and their followers denote the volume element — the generator of the quotient module — by
[dx/\partial_{\xi}]
, see
[
Del
, Ch.1]
.

2.11.
A. Sergeev’s central extension of
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)

In 1970’s A. Sergeev proved (cited in
[
FuLe
]
, see also
[
BGLL1
]
) that over
{\mathbb{C}}
there is just one
nontrivial central extension of
{\mathfrak{spe}}(n)
for
n>2
. It exists only
for
n=4
and we denote it by Sergeev’s initials:
{\mathfrak{as}}
. Let us represent an arbitrary
element
\widehat{X}\in{\mathfrak{as}}
as a pair
\widehat{X}=X+a\cdot 1_{4|4}
, where
X\in{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)
and

a\in{{\mathbb{C}}}
.
The bracket in
{\mathfrak{as}}

is

(104)

\left[\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}+a\cdot 1_{4|4},\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\cr C^{\prime}&-A^{\prime}{}^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}+a^{\prime}\cdot 1_{4|4}\right]=\left[\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\cr C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$},\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A^{\prime}&B^{\prime}\cr C^{\prime}&-A^{\prime}{}^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}\right]+\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits(C\widetilde{C}^{\prime})\cdot 1_{4|4},

where
\ \widetilde{}\
is extended by linearity from matrices

C_{ij}:=E_{ij}-E_{ji}
, on which
\widetilde{C}_{ij}=C_{kl}
for any
even permutation
(1234)\longmapsto(ijkl)
.

The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{as}}
can also be described with the help of
the
spinor representation
, see
[
LSh4
]
. Consider the particular case of
{\mathfrak{o}}(6)=({\mathfrak{po}}(0|6))_{0}
. The superspace of
{\mathfrak{po}}(0|6)
is the Grassmann superalgebra
\Lambda(\xi,\eta)

generated by
\xi=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})
and
\eta=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})
and the Lie superalgebra structure given by the
Poisson bracket (
68
).

Recall that
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(H_{f}\mid f\in\Lambda(\xi,\eta))
. Now,
note that
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)
can be embedded into
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)
. Indeed,
setting
\deg\xi_{i}=\deg\eta_{i}=1
for all
i
we introduce
a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading on
\Lambda(\xi,\eta)
which, in turn, induces
a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading on
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)
of the form

{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ {\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)_{i}
. Since

{\mathfrak{sl}}(4)\simeq{\mathfrak{o}}(6)
, we can identify
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)_{0}
with

{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)_{0}
.

It is not difficult to see that the elements of degree
-1
in the
standard gradings of
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)
constitute isomorphic

{\mathfrak{sl}}(4)\simeq{\mathfrak{o}}(6)
-modules; moreover, it is possible to embed
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)_{1}
into
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)_{1}
.

Sergeev’s extension
{\mathfrak{as}}
is the result of the restriction to

{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)\subset{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)
of the cocycle that turns
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|6)
into

{\mathfrak{po}}(0|6)
, see eq. (
179
). The quantization
{\mathcal{Q}}
deforms
{\mathfrak{po}}(0|6)
into

{\mathfrak{gl}}(\Lambda(\xi))
; the through maps

T_{\lambda}:{\mathfrak{as}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{po}}(0|6)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{Q}}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathfrak{gl}}(\Lambda(\xi))

are representations of

{\mathfrak{as}}
in the
4|4
-dimensional modules
\mathop{\mathrm{spin}}\nolimits_{\lambda}
isomorphic
to each other for all
\lambda\neq 0
, so we assume
\lambda=1
and do not indicate it. The explicit form of

T_{\lambda}
is

(105)

T_{\lambda}\colon\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A~&B\cr C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}+a\cdot 1_{4|4}\longmapsto\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A~&B-\lambda\widetilde{C}\cr C&-A^{t}\end{pmatrix}$}+\lambda a\cdot 1_{4|4},

where
\widetilde{C}
is defined in
the line under eq. (
104
). Clearly,
T_{\lambda}
is
irreducible for any
\lambda
.

2.12.
Deformations of the Buttin superalgebra (after

[
Ko1
,
LSh3
]
)

As is clear from the definition of the
Buttin bracket (and
[
G
]
from where it went unnoticed), there is a regrading (namely,
{\mathfrak{b}}(n;n)
given by

\deg\xi_{i}=0,\deg q_{i}=1
for all
i
) under which
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)
,
initially of depth 2, takes the form

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\simeq\Pi({\mathbb{C}}[\xi])
. Now let us replace
the
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
of functions (with inverted
parity) by the rank-1 module (over the algebra of functions) of

\lambda
-densities (also with inverted parity),
i.e.
, set

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\simeq\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(0|n))
, where the

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
-action on the generator
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}^{\lambda}
of the module
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(0|n)
is given
by the formula

(106)

L_{D}(\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}^{\lambda})=\lambda\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}^{\lambda}\;\text{ and }\;p(\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}^{\lambda})={\bar{1}}\text{~~when considered in ${\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}$}.

Define
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n)
to be the Cartan prolong

(107)

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n):=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}=(\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(0|n)),{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n))_{*}.

Clearly, this is a deform of
{\mathfrak{b}}(n;n)
. Kotchetkov called he collection of these prolongs

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n)
for all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
the
main
deformation
, see
[
Ko1
]
; the other deformations are called
singular
, see Theorem
2.12.1.  Theorem
. The deformation of
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)
with odd parameter is called
quantization
: it is a splitting image of the quantization of the Poisson (super)algebra.

The deform
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
of
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)
is a regrading of

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n)
described as follows. Set

(108)

{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n):=\{M_{f}\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\mid a\;\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits M_{f}=(-1)^{p(f)}2(a-bn)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}\}.

Using the explicit form of the divergence of
M_{f}
we see that

(109)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n)&=\{M_{f}\in{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\mid(bn-aE)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}=a\Delta f\}=\\
&\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|n+1)\mid L_{D}(\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{q,\xi,\tau}^{a}\alpha_{0}^{a-bn})=0\},\text{~~where $\Delta:=\mathop{\sum}_{i\leq n}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}$
}.\end{array}

It is subject to a direct verification that
{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
for
\lambda=\frac{2a}{n(a-b)}
. This isomorphism
shows that
\lambda
actually runs over
{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}
, not
{\mathbb{C}}
, as
one might hastily think.

For future use, we will denote the operator that singles out

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
in
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
as follows:

(110)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits_{\lambda}:=(bn-aE)\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial f}{\partial\tau}$}}-a\Delta\;\text{ for
$\lambda:=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2a}{n(a-b)}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2a}{n(a-b)}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2a}{n(a-b)}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2a}{n(a-b)}$}}$ and $\Delta:=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}$}}$}.

As follows from the description of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
-modules
(
[
BL4
]
) and the simplicity criteria for
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie
superalgebras (
[
K2
]
), the Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)

are simple for
n>1
and
\lambda\neq 0
, 1,
\infty
, see eq. (
111
). It is also clear that the Lie superalgebras

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
are non-isomorphic for distinct values of
\lambda
, except for the
isomorphisms already described in list (
23
).

The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)={\mathfrak{b}}_{0}(n)
is not simple: it has an

\varepsilon=(0|1)
-dimensional, center. At

\lambda=1
and
\infty
, the Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)

are not simple either: each of them has an ideal of codimension

\varepsilon^{n+1}
and
\varepsilon^{n}
, respectively. The corresponding exact
sequences are

(111)

\begin{array}[]{c}0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}M_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{le}}(n)\longrightarrow 0,\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot M_{\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}}\longrightarrow 0,\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot M_{\tau\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}}\longrightarrow 0.\\
\end{array}

Clearly, at the exceptional values of
\lambda
,
i.e.
, 0, 1, and

\infty
, the deformations of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
should be
investigated extra carefully. As we will see immediately, this pays:
at each of the exceptional points we find additional deformations.
The following is an
Open problem
:

What is the reason for the
exceptional deformation at
\lambda=-1
?

Other
exceptional values (
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
and
-\frac{3}{2}
) come from the
isomorphisms

{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2;2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{1/2}(2|2)={\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)
and

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1-\lambda}(2|2)
, see list (
23
),

and the existence of an even non-degenerate bilinear form on
\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{1/2})
which gives rise to a central extension

0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}K_{1}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)\longrightarrow 0.

The restriction of the well-known deformation
{\mathcal{Q}}
— quantization — of
{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2)
to
{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)
explains the exceptional deformation at
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
. For the proof of the next theorem, see §
10
.

Theorem

(Deformations of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, after
[
Ko1
,
LSh3
]
)
.

For
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, set
H=H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})
for brevity. Then,

1)

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits~H=(1|0)
for
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, except for

\lambda=0
,
-1
,
1
,
\infty
for
n>2
.

1_{2}
)
For
n=2
, in addition
to the above,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits~H\neq(1|0)
at
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
and

\lambda=-\frac{3}{2}
.

2)
At the exceptional values of
\lambda
listed in heading

1)
, we have

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits~H=(2|0)
at
\lambda=\pm 1
and
n
odd, or

\lambda=\infty
and
n
even, or
n=2
and
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
or

\lambda=-\frac{3}{2}
.

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits~H=(1|1)
at
\lambda=0
or
\lambda=\infty
and
n
odd,
or
\lambda=\pm 1
and
n
even.

Let
k=(k_{1},\dots,k_{n})
; we set

q^{k}=q_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots q_{n}^{k_{n}}
and
|k|=\sum k_{i}
. The corresponding cocycles
C
are given by the following
non-zero values in terms of the generating functions
f
and
g
,
where
d_{{\bar{1}}}(f)
is the degree of
f
with respect to odd
indeterminates only:

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)

p(C)

C(f,g)

{\mathfrak{b}}_{0}(n)

odd

(-1)^{p(f)}(d_{{\bar{1}}}(f)-1)(d_{{\bar{1}}}(g)-1)fg

{\mathfrak{b}}_{-1}(n)

n+1\pmod{2}

f=q^{k},\ g=q^{l}\longmapsto(4-|k|-|l|)q^{k+l}\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n}+

\tau\Delta(q^{k+l}\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n})

{\mathfrak{b}}_{1}(n)

n+1\pmod{2}

f=\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n},\ g\longmapsto\begin{cases}(d_{{\bar{1}}}(g)-1)g,&\text{if $g\neq af$, $a\in{\mathbb{C}}$}\\
2(n-1)f,&\text{if $g=f$ and $n$ is even}\end{cases}

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}(n)

n\pmod{2}

f=\tau\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{n},\ g\longmapsto\begin{cases}(d_{{\bar{1}}}(g)-1)g,&\text{if $g\neq af$, $a\in{\mathbb{C}}$}\\
2f,&\text{if $g=f$ and $n$ is odd}\end{cases}

{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2)

even

induced by the quantization
{\mathcal{Q}}
of
{\mathfrak{po}}
, see
[
Fu
,
Vo
,
Tyut
]

\lambda
generic

even

see (
112
), (
113
)

On
{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{-3/2}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2;1)
, the
cocycle
C
is the one induced on
{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)={\mathfrak{po}}(2|2)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot K_{1}

by the usual deformation (quantization) of
{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2)
with cocycle
Q
applied to the nonstandard regrading
{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2;1)
with subsequent passage to the quotient modulo the center

(spanned by constants)
.

3)
The space
H
is diagonalizable with respect to the Cartan
subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{der}}~~{\mathfrak{g}}
. Let the cocycle
M
corresponding to the
main deformation be one of the eigenvectors. Let
C
be another
eigenvector in
H
, it determines a singular deformation from
item
2)
. The only cocycles
kM+lC
, where
k,l\in{\mathbb{C}}
, that
can be extended to a global deformation are those for
kl=0
, i.e.,
either
M
or
C
.

All the singular deformations of the bracket
\{-,-\}_{old}
in
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, except the ones for

\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
or
-\frac{3}{2}
and
n=2
, are linear in
\hbar
even if
\hbar
is even:

(112)

\{f,g\}_{\hbar}^{sing}=\{f,g\}_{old}+\hbar\cdot C(f,g)\text{ for any }f,g\in{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n).

2.12.2.
Remark: A relation with the Vey cocycle.

C. Roger observed that the singular
deformation (quantization) of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{0}(n)={\mathfrak{b}}(n)
is, up to a sign,
the wedge product of two 1-cocycles, the derivations
f\longmapsto(d_{{\bar{1}}}(f)-1)f
. He also noted that the cocycle on

{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2;1)
induced by the quantization of

{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2)
is a straightforward superization of the well-known
Vey’s cocycle
[
V
]
.

Since the elements of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
are encoded by functions
(for us: polynomials or formal power series) in
\tau
,
q
and

\xi
subject to one relation with an odd left-hand side which contains

\tau
, it seems plausible that the bracket in

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
can be, at least for generic values of the parameter

\lambda
, expressed solely in terms of
q
and
\xi
. This is
indeed the case, and here is an explicit formula (in which
\{f,g\}_{B.b.}
is the antibracket and

\Delta=\mathop{\sum}_{i\leq n}\ \frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}
):

(113)

\begin{array}[]{l}\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{\lambda}^{main}=\{f_{1},f_{2}\}_{B.b.}+\lambda(c_{\lambda}(f_{1},f_{2})f_{1}\Delta f_{2}+(-1)^{p(f_{1})}c_{\lambda}(f_{2},f_{1})(\Delta f_{1})f_{2}),\\
\text{where $c_{\lambda}(f_{1},f_{2})=\frac{\deg f_{1}-2}{2+\lambda(\deg f_{2}-n)}$},\end{array}

and
\deg
is computed with respect to the standard grading
\deg q_{i}=\deg\xi_{i}=1
.

2.12.3.
The exceptional nonstandard regradings

\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
.

This is a regrading
of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(2)
given by the formulas:

(114)

\deg\tau=0,\;\;\deg\xi_{1}=\deg\xi_{2}=-1,\;\;\deg q_{1}=\deg q_{2}=1.

We have the following two cases:

(115)

\begin{array}[]{ll}1)&\text{\lx@text@underline{$b=0$ or $a=b$}: ~${\mathfrak{b}}_{a,0}(2;\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{le}}(2)$ and
${\mathfrak{b}}_{a,a}(2;\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(2)$,}\\
&\text{in particular,
${\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}=0$;}\\
2)&\text{\lx@text@underline{$a$ and $b$ generic}: }{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}})\text{ and
}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{1}},\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{2}},\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{Q_{1}},\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{Q_{2}}),\end{array}

where
Q_{1}=A\xi_{1}\xi_{2}q_{1}+B\tau\xi_{2}
,
Q_{2}=A\xi_{1}\xi_{2}q_{2}-B\tau\xi_{1}
with some coefficients
A
and
B

determined by
a
and
b
. The bracket on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is determined
by the odd form
\omega=c\sum dQ_{i}d\xi_{i}
, so
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
must be
contained in
{\mathfrak{m}}(2)_{0}
. Direct calculations show that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=4|4
and

(116)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{spe}}(2)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}X,\text{ where }X=\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{a\tau+b\sum q_{i}\xi_{i}}.

Indeed,

(117)

{\mathfrak{spe}}(2)_{0}\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{q_{1}\xi_{2}},\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{q_{2}\xi_{1}},\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{q_{1}\xi_{1}-q_{2}\xi_{2}}),\quad{\mathfrak{spe}}(2)_{-1}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{1},

and

(118)

{\mathfrak{spe}}(2)_{1}={\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\alpha\xi_{1}\xi_{2}P(q)+\beta\tau\Delta(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}P(q))},

where
P(q)
is a degree-2 monomial,
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}}
and
\Delta=\sum\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial q_{i}\partial\xi_{i}}
, see eq. (
80
).

The eigenvalues of
X
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
are:
b-a
on
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{\bar{0}}

and
b+a
an
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{\bar{1}}
. Therefore,

(119)

{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(2;\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{-b,-a}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{b,a}(2).

For
n>2
, as well as for
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
with
n>1
, similar regradings
are not of Weisfeiler type, as is not difficult to see.

The exceptional grading
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2)
defines
the exceptional grading
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
of the isomorphic algebra

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
, see eq. (
23
).

Thus, the exceptional regradings
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{b}}}
or
\mathop{\mathrm{Reg}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{h}}}
do not
provide us with new W-graded vectorial algebras. Still, they are
needed to interpret the automorphisms (
119
).

2.13.
The exceptional Lie subsuperalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|6)

Like
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|m;m)
, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
is not
determined by its non-positive part and requires a closer study. The
Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n)
is generated by the elements of

{\mathbb{C}}[t,\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n},\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n}]
. The standard

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
is induced by the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathbb{C}}[t,\xi,\eta]
given by

(120)

\deg K_{f}=\deg f-2,\text{ where }\deg t=2,\quad\deg\xi_{i}=\deg\eta_{i}=1\ \ \text{for all $i$}.

Clearly, in this grading,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of depth
2. Let us consider the functions that generate several first
homogeneous components of
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-2}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
:

(121)

\begin{array}[]{|c|c|c|c|c|}\hline\cr\text{component}&{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}&{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}&{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}&{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\\
\hline\cr\text{its generators}&1&\Lambda^{1}(\xi,\eta)&\Lambda^{2}(\xi,\eta)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot t&\Lambda^{3}(\xi,\eta)\oplus t\Lambda^{1}(\xi,\eta)\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

As one can prove directly, the component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
generates the whole
subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i>0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
.

The component

{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
splits into two
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-modules
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}=\Lambda^{3}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{12}=t\Lambda^{1}
. It is obvious that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{12}
is always
irreducible and the component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}
is trivial for
n=1
.

The partial Cartan prolongs of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{12}
are
well known (here
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{po}}(0|2n))
is the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{der}}~({\mathfrak{po}}(0|2n))={\mathbb{C}}E\rtimes{\mathfrak{po}}(0|2n)
, where
E=\sum\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{i}}+\sum\eta_{i}\partial_{\eta_{i}}
is the grading operator):

\begin{matrix}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},{\mathfrak{g}}_{11})_{*}^{mk}\simeq{\mathfrak{po}}(0|2n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot K_{t}\simeq{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{po}}(0|2n));\\
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},{\mathfrak{g}}_{12})_{*}^{mk}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{12}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot K_{t^{2}}\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}(2n|2).\end{matrix}

Observe a remarkable property of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|6)
: only for
2n=6
the
component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}
splits into 2 irreducible modules; we will
denote by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\xi}
the module containing

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}
, and by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\eta}
the module containing
\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}
.

Observe further, that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{o}}(6)\simeq{\mathfrak{gl}}(4)
. As

{\mathfrak{gl}}(4)
-modules,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\xi}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\eta}
are the
symmetric squares
S^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
and
S^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*})
of the tautological
4-dimensional
{\mathfrak{gl}}(4)
-module
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and its dual, respectively.

Theorem

(The partial Cartan prolong
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
, see
[
Sh5
]
,

[
Sh14
]
)
.

The partial Cartan prolong
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\xi}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\xi}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{12})_{*}^{mk}
is infinite-dimensional and
simple. It is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\eta}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},{\mathfrak{g}}_{11}^{\eta}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{12})_{*}^{mk}
.

Convention
. When it does not matter which of the isomorphic algebras

{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\xi}\simeq{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\eta}
to take, we will simply write

{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
, but by default we are always dealing with
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{\xi}
.

2.14.
The fifteen W-regradings of exceptional simple vectorial
Lie superalgebras

Theorem

(The 15 W-regradings (
[
Sh14
]
,
[
CK2
]
))
.

All W-regradings of the exceptional simple vectorial Lie
superalgebras are the regradings of their “standard” ambients
listed in table
(
101
)
.

Thus, there are
5

isomorphism classes
of abstract
Lie superalgebras consisting of
15
W-filtered Lie superalgebras. For a proof, see Section
8.1.4
.

Table (
125
) lists the terms
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}

for
-2\leq i\leq 0
of the 15 exceptional W-graded algebras.

Observe that none of the simple W-graded vectorial Lie
superalgebras is of depth
>3
and only two algebras are of depth 3:

{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{b}}(4|5;K)
, for which

(122)

{\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{b}}(4|5;K)_{-3}\simeq\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}),

and another one,
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6;CK)={\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|11)
, for which

(123)

{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|11)_{-3}\simeq\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\boxtimes{\mathbb{C}}[-3]),

where
{\mathbb{C}}[-3]\simeq{\mathbb{C}}\,\mathbbmss{1}
is the module over
{\mathbb{C}}\,\theta\partial_{\theta}\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|1)\subset{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|11)_{0}
, such that
(\theta\partial_{\theta})\mathbbmss{1}=-3\mathbbmss{1}
. Define the representation
T^{1/2}
of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
by setting (cf. (
47
))

(124)

T^{1/2}_{D}(f\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{1/2})=(D(f)+(-1)^{p(f)p(D)}f\cdot\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D)\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{1/2}

for any
D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
. In table (

), the star

) and (

2.15.
W-gradings of serial simple vectorial Lie superalgebras

Theorem

(34 W-gradings)
.

Any Weisfeiler regrading
{\mathfrak{h}}
of a given simple vectorial Lie
superalgebra is one of regradings from Tables (
131
),
(
132
), (
136
) and (
125
).

For its proof, see Section
8.1.4
. To facilitate
the comparison of various vectorial superalgebras, we offer the
following table (
131
). The most interesting phenomena occur for extremal
values of
r
and small values of the superdimension
m|n
.

The central element
z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is supposed to be chosen so that
it acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
as
k\cdot\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
.

Let
\Lambda(n):={\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}]
be the Grassmann
superalgebra generated by the elements
\xi_{i}
, each of degree 0. We denote (recall Examples

2.10.1
)

(126)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)\text{-modules}:&\Lambda(0):={\mathbb{C}},\quad T(\vec{0}):=\Lambda(n),\quad T_{0}(\vec{0}):=\Lambda(n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1,\\
&\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n):=\{v\in\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n)\mid\int v=0\};\\
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)\text{-modules}:&T_{0}^{0}(\vec{0}):=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1\ .\end{array}

Over
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
, it is convenient to consider
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)

as a submodule of
\Lambda(n)
.

For the range of the parameter
r
in tables (
131
) and (
132
),
see table (
99
). Certain values of
r

(namely,
r=k-1
for
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k)
, as well as
r=n-1
for
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)

and its subalgebras) are excluded because, for these values of
r
,
the corresponding grading is not a W-grading: the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is reducible.

For
N=5^{\prime},3^{\prime}
and 9
: The
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-modules
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}

are described as
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2)
-modules.

For
N=16
: we set
p(\mu)\equiv n\pmod{2}
, so
\mu
can
be an odd indeterminate. The Lie superalgebras
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}_{\mu}(0|n)
are isomorphic for non-zero values of
\mu
; hence, so are the
algebras
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})
. Hence, for
n

even, we can set
\mu=1
, whereas if
\mu
is odd, we should
consider it as an additional indeterminate on which the coefficients
depend.

For
N=1
, 3, etc.
: the symbol
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}}
is short for
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits;\Lambda(r))}
.

For
N=11
, etc.
: the symbol

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{osp}}}\boxtimes{\mathbb{C}}[-1]
denotes the tensor product of
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{osp}}(m-2r|2n;\Lambda(r))}
by the
{\mathfrak{c}}
-module
{\mathbb{C}}[-1]
, where

{\mathfrak{c}}\subset{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{osp}}(m-2r|2n))\subset{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{osp}}(m-2r|2n;\Lambda(r))),

with the natural action of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
in
\Lambda(r)
. Line
14
should be similarly understood.

For
N=21
, 22
: the terms “
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-i}
” denote the
superspace isomorphic to the one in quotation marks, but with the
action given by the rules (
129
) and (
130
).

For
N=21-23
: denote the tautological

k|k
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{spe}}(k)
-module by
V
; let
D=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}\nolimits(1_{k},-1_{k})\in{\mathfrak{pe}}(k)
. Let
W=V\otimes\Lambda(r)
and
X\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
. Let

(127)

\Xi=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}\in\Lambda(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n}).

Denote by
T^{r}
, where
r
is integer, the representations of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
in

W
in
{\mathfrak{spe}}(n-r)\otimes\Lambda(r)
and
{\mathfrak{spe}}(n-r)\otimes\Lambda(r)
given by the formula

(128)

T^{r}(X)=1\otimes X+D\otimes\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{n-r}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{n-r}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{n-r}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{n-r}$}}\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits X.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n;r)_{0}
for
r\neq n-2
. For
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
,
we have:

(129)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{${\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)$ acts on the ideal ${\mathfrak{spe}}(n-r)\otimes``\Lambda(r)^{\prime\prime}$ via
$T^{r}$, see eq.~\eqref{1.3.4}};\\
\text{any $X\otimes f\in{\mathfrak{spe}}(n-r)\otimes\Lambda(r)$ acts in
${\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}$ as
$\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes f$ and in ${\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}$ as $0$};\\
\text{any $X\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)$ acts in ${\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}$ via $T^{r}$ and in
${\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}$ as $X$.}\end{array}

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n;n-2)
. For
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, we note that

{\mathfrak{spe}}(2)\simeq{\mathbb{C}}(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{q_{1}\xi_{1}-q_{2}\xi_{2}})\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}},

whereas
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
are as above, for
r<n-2
. Set

{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathbb{C}}(\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{q_{1}\xi_{1}-q_{2}\xi_{2}})
. In this case

(130)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\simeq\underline{``\left({\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda(n-2)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}\mathop{\mathrm{Le}}\nolimits_{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}\otimes(\Lambda(n-2)\setminus{\mathbb{C}}\xi_{3}\cdots\xi_{n})\right)^{\prime\prime}}\ltimes T^{1}({\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n-2)).

The action of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n-2)
, the quotient of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
modulo the
underlined ideal in quotation marks in (
130
) is performed via (
129
). In the subspace

\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\otimes\Lambda(n-2)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, this action is the same
as in the space of volume forms. So we can consider everything, except for
the terms proportional to
\Xi
, see eq. (
127
), or, speaking correctly, take
the irreducible submodule of functions with integral 0.

For
N=27
, 32, 33
: the terms “
{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
” denote the
superspace isomorphic to the one in quotation marks, but with the
action given by eqs. (
129
) and (
134
).

In the exceptional case
ar=bn
,
i.e.
,

\lambda=\frac{2}{n-r}
, we see that the

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
-action on the ideal

({\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{spe}}(n-r)\otimes\Lambda(r))\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, and
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
, is the same as for
{\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}
, see eq. (
129
).

In table (
131
), to save space in line 18 we denote the
{\mathfrak{pe}}(n-r;\Lambda(r))\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|r)
-module
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{pe}}(n-r;\Lambda(r))}
by
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{pe}}}
, and we use similar abbreviations in other lines.

Non-standard gradings in cases 1–23, with non-Weisfeiler gradings excluded,
are shown in tables (

), (

).

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)

2.16.
Grozman’s theorems and a description of
{\mathfrak{g}}
as

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}

It is convenient to
describe the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
of twisted multi-vector fields
as a
{\mathfrak{vect}}
-module. Similarly, in
[
CK2
]
the exceptional
algebras are described as
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
. For most
of the series, such description is of little value because each
homogeneous component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
has a complicated
structure. For the exceptional simple Lie superalgebras,
the situation is totally different!

Observe that apart from being
beautiful, such a description is useful for constructing simple

Volichenko algebras
(inhomogeneous with respect to parity subalgebras of Lie superalgebras), cf.
[
LS
]
, and simple Lie algebras in
characteristic 2.

In relation to this, we recall a theorem of Grozman. He completely
described bilinear differential operators acting in spaces of
tensor fields on any
m
-dimensional manifold
M
and invariant under all changes of coordinates.
Since the problem is local, we can assume that
M\simeq{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
, see
[
BL4
]
. Miraculously, “most” of the first-order invariant operators
determines a Lie superalgebra on its domain of definition. Some of these
superalgebras turn out to be very close to simple. In the
constructions below we use some of these invariant operators.

Let
\rho
be an irreducible representation of the group
GL(n)
in
a finite-dimensional vector space
V
and let
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
be its lowest weight. A
tensor field of
type

\rho
(or of type
V
: depending on the emphasis) on an

n
-dimensional connected manifold
M
is any section
t
of the
locally trivial vector bundle over
M
with fiber
V
such that,
under the change of coordinates,

t(y(x))=\rho\left({\frac{\partial{y}}{\partial{x}}}\right)t(x).

The space of tensor field of type
\rho
or
V
will be denoted
by
T(\rho)
, or
T(V)
, or even
T(\lambda)
. Examples:

\begin{array}[]{l}T(\mu,\dots,\mu)=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu},\text{~~the space of
$\mu$-densities};\\
\text{$\Omega^{r}:=T(0,\dots,0,1,\dots,1)$, with{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\Omega^{r}$}}}{\@index{\@indexphrase{$L^{r}$}}}
$r$ symbols 1, is the space of
differential $r$-forms},\\
\text{in particular, $T(0)=\Omega^{0}$ is the space of
functions also denoted by ${\mathcal{F}}$};\\
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n)=T(-1,0,\dots,0);\\
L^{r}:=T(-1,\dots,-1,0,\dots,0)\text{~~with
symbols 1, is the space of $r$-vector fields,}\\
\text{the
$r$th exterior power (over the algebra of functions ${\mathcal{F}}=\Omega^{0}$) of ${\mathfrak{vect}}(n)$}.\\
\end{array}

The spaces of twisted
r
-forms and twisted
r
-vector
fields with twist

\mu
are defined to be, respectively,

\Omega^{r}_{\mu}=\Omega^{r}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu}\text{ and
}L^{r}_{\mu}=L^{r}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu}.

Obviously,
L^{r}_{\mu}\simeq\Omega^{n-r}_{\mu-1}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{1}=\Omega^{n}
.

The following statements are excerpts from Grozman’s difficult
result
[
G
]
. To describe one of the operators,
P_{4}
, we need
the
Nijenhuis bracket
, defined by the formula

\begin{array}[]{l}\omega^{k}\otimes\xi,\omega^{l}\otimes\eta\longmapsto(\omega^{k}\wedge\omega^{l})\otimes[\xi,\eta]\ +\\
+\left(\omega^{k}\wedge L_{\xi}(\omega^{l})+(-1)^{k}d\omega^{k}\wedge\iota_{\xi}(\omega^{l})\right)\otimes\eta+\left(-L_{\eta}(\omega^{k})\wedge\omega^{l}+(-1)^{l}\iota_{\eta}(\omega^{k})\wedge d\omega^{l})\right)\otimes\xi,\end{array}

where
\iota_{X}
is the inner product by the field
X
and
L_{X}
is the Lie
derivative along
X
. The Nijenhuis bracket admits the
following interpretation which makes its invariance manifest: the
operator
D:(\Omega^{k}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}{\mathfrak{vect}}(M),\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}
given by the formula

\begin{gathered}D(\omega^{k}\otimes\xi,\omega):=d\left(\omega^{k}\wedge\iota_{\xi}(\omega)+(-1)^{k}\omega^{k}\wedge\iota_{\xi}(d\omega)\right)=d\omega^{k}\wedge\iota_{\xi}(\omega)+(-1)^{k}\omega^{k}\wedge L_{\xi}(\omega)\end{gathered}

is, for a fixed
\omega^{k}\otimes\xi
, a superderivation of the
supercommutative superalgebra
\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}
, and the Nijenhuis
bracket is just the supercommutator of these superderivations. So we
can identify
\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}{\mathfrak{vect}}(M)
with the
Lie subsuperalgebra
C(d)\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(\widehat{M})
, where

\widehat{M}
is the supermanifold
(M,\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(M))
,
i.e.
,

C(d)
is the centralizer of the exterior differential on
\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(M))
:

C(d)=\{D\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(\widehat{M})\mid[D,d]=0\}.

Dualizations
. To any mapping
F:T(V)\longrightarrow T(W)
there corresponds the dual mapping

F^{*}:(T(W))^{*}\longrightarrow(T(V))^{*}
. If we consider tensors with
compact support, so integration can be performed, we can identify

(T(V))^{*}\simeq T(V^{*})\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits\simeq T(V^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits),

where
\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits
denotes the 1-dimensional
{\mathfrak{gl}}
-module given by the
trace (or supertrace, if
M
is a supermanifold). The

formal dual
is defined to be

(T(V))^{*}:=T(V^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits).

Given a bilinear mapping
F:T(V_{1})\otimes T(V_{2})\longrightarrow T(W)
, it is possible to
dualize it with respect to each argument:

\begin{array}[]{l}F^{*1}:T(W^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits)\otimes T(V_{2})\longrightarrow T(V_{1}^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits),\\
F^{*2}:T(V_{1})\otimes T(W^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits)\longrightarrow T(V_{2}^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{str}}\nolimits).\end{array}

Theorem

(
[
G
]
)
.

The invariant
under arbitrary changes of variables irreducible differential
bilinear operators
D:T(\rho_{1})\otimes T(\rho_{2})\longrightarrow T(\rho_{3})
of
order
1
are, up to dualizations and permutation of arguments, only
the following ones:

{\bf P_{1}}:\Omega^{r}\otimes T(\rho_{2})\longrightarrow T(\rho_{3}),\qquad(w,t)\longmapsto Z(dw,t),

where
Z
is an
order-
0
operator, the extension of the projection

\rho_{1}\otimes\rho_{2}\longrightarrow\rho_{3}
onto any of the irreducible
components;

\begin{gathered}{\bf P_{2}}:{\mathfrak{vect}}\otimes T(\rho)\longrightarrow T(\rho),\quad\text{ \lx@text@underline{the Lie derivative}};\\
{\bf P_{3}}:T(S^{p}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}))\otimes T(S^{q}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}))\longrightarrow T(S^{p+q-1}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*})),\quad\text{ \lx@text@underline{the Poisson
bracket}};\end{gathered}

{\bf P_{4}}
: On manifolds, the bracket in
C(d)
is called the

Nijenhuis bracket
. This bracket is a linear combination
of operators
P_{1}
,
P_{1}^{*1}
, their composition with the
permutation operator
T(V)\otimes T(W)\longrightarrow T(W)\otimes T(V)
, and
a new, irreducible, operator denoted
P_{4}
;

{\bf P_{5}}:\Omega^{p}\otimes\Omega^{q}\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+q+1};\quad\omega_{1},\ \omega_{2}\longmapsto(-1)^{p(\omega_{1})}a(d\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2})+b(\omega_{1}d\omega_{2}),\text{ where }a,b\in{\mathbb{C}};

{\bf P_{6}}:\Omega^{p}_{\mu}\otimes\Omega^{q}_{\nu}\longrightarrow\Omega^{p+q+1}_{\mu+\nu}
, where
|\mu|^{2}+|\nu|^{2}\neq 0
and
p+q<n

is given by the formula

\omega_{1}\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu},\ \omega_{2}\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\nu}\longmapsto\left(\nu(-1)^{p(\omega_{1})}d\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}-\mu\omega_{1}d\omega_{2}\right)\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu+\nu};

{\bf P_{7}}:L^{p}\otimes L^{q}\longrightarrow L^{p+q-1}\quad\text{ the
\lx@text@underline{Schouten bracket}};

{\bf P_{8}}:L^{p}_{\mu}\otimes L^{q}_{\nu}\longrightarrow L^{p+q-1}_{\mu+\nu}
, a
deformation of the Schouten bracket given by the next formula on
manifolds for
p+q\leq n
, and on supermanifolds of
superdimension
n|1
for
p,q\in{\mathbb{C}}
:

(137)

\begin{array}[]{l}X\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.34445pto\hskip-0.0689ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu},\ Y\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.34445pto\hskip-0.0689ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\nu}\longmapsto\\
\left((\nu-1)(\mu+\nu-1)\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits X\cdot Y+(-1)^{p(X)}(\mu-1)(\mu+\nu-1)X\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits Y-(\mu-1)(\nu-1)\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits(XY)\right)\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.34445pto\hskip-0.0689ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\mu+\nu},\end{array}

where the divergence of a multi-vector field
f
is
defined to be

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits(f)=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i\leq n}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{\check{x}_{i}}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{\check{x}_{i}}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{\check{x}_{i}}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}f}{\partial_{x_{i}}\partial_{\check{x}_{i}}}$}}

in local coordinates
(x,\check{x})
on the supermanifold
\check{M}
associated to the sheaf of sections of the exterior algebra of
the tangent bundle on any supermanifold
M
; here the checked
coordinates on
\check{M}
are

\theta_{i}=\Pi\left({\frac{\partial}{\partial{x_{i}}}}\right)=\check{x}_{i},\emph{~~(seeRemark\ref{rem2.3.7})}.

Theorem

(Lie and associative (super)algebras determined by invariant operators,
[
G
]
)
.

The following
natural invariant operators determine associative or Lie
superalgebras on their domains:

P_{3}
,
P_{4}
,
P_{7}
, and
P_{8}
define Lie
superalgebras. The vectorial Lie
superalgebra
C(d)
is, however, not transitive.

P_{5}
: For
ab=0
and, of course,
|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}\neq 0
, it determines an associative superalgebra
structure.

For
a=b\neq 0
, it determines the structure of a nilpotent Lie
superalgebra on
\Pi(\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}})
.

The bracket given by
d\omega_{1}\cdot\omega_{2}-\omega_{1}d\omega_{2}

determines a Lie algebra structure on the space
\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}
.

P_{6}
multiplied by
\dfrac{\mu-\nu}{\mu\nu}
determines structures
of nilpotent Lie superalgebras on the superspaces

\Pi\left(\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}}\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}/d\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\right),\quad\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}_{+}=\Pi(\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\lambda>0;\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}),\quad\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}_{-}=\Pi(\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\lambda<0;\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}).

P_{6}
multiplied by
\dfrac{\mu-\nu}{\mu+\nu}
determines a nilpotent Lie superalgebra structure on

\Pi\left(d\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\bigoplus\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{\lambda\neq 0;\;\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}}\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}\right).

P_{8}
multiplied by
\dfrac{1}{\mu\nu}
determines a nilpotent Lie
superalgebra structures on
\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}_{+}
and

\Omega^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}_{-}
.

Remarks

The claims of this Theorem are breezily formulated in the short note
[
G
]
, here they are formulated more explicitly. Their proof is done by a direct verification.

The operator
P_{8}
is a deformation of the
Schouten bracket we considered in
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, so this simple Lie superalgebra could have been described 20 years earlier than in
[
LSh3
]
if more attention was paid to Grozman’s results.

The operator
P_{6}
is the “Fourier transform” of the
Schouten bracket.

The divergence of multi-vector fields is the “Fourier transform” of the exterior derivation.

Grozman proved that there are no invariant differential operators of degree
>3
, while the operators of degree 2 and 3 are compositions of degree-1 operators, with one exceptional degree-3 operator
Gz
on the line now bearing Grozman’s name. For a version of Grozman’s theorem over fields of characteristic
p>0
, see
[
BoLe
]
. For the extension of Grozman’s theorem to non-differential operators on the line, see
[
IoMa
]
.

2.17.
Weisfeiler-type gradings with infinite-dimensional
components

It is sometimes convenient to consider
gradings of our vectorial Lie superalgebras which lead them out of
the GLAPG class, but still preserve the finiteness of the
depth and preserve the two characteristic features of the
W-gradings:

(138)

1)
for these grading

\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq 0}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
is maximal;

2)
the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is irreducible. Namely, we set

\deg x_{i}=0
for
r
even indeterminates
x
; we denote this
grading
\bar{r}
.

For the exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, and
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;\bar{n})
, the multiplications are described as follows:

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(5|10)
: here,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(5|0)\simeq d\Omega^{3}(5|0)
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}=\Pi(d\Omega^{1}(5|0))
with the natural
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-action on

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}
and the bracket of any two odd elements is given by their product. Since
{\mathfrak{svect}}(5|0)
preserves the volume element
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits
and all its powers,
we can make, for any permutation

(ijklm)
of
(12345)
, the following identification

dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}\wedge dx_{l}:=dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}\wedge dx_{l}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}\nolimits(ijklm)\partial_{x_{m}}.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{as}}(4|4)
: here,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(4|0)

and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}=\Omega^{1}(4|0)\otimes_{\Omega^{0}(4|0)}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{-1/2}(4|0)
with the
natural
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-action on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}
and the bracket of odd
elements is given by

\left[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}\right]=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}},

where we identify

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}\wedge dx_{k}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}\nolimits(ijkl)\partial_{x_{l}}\text{ for any permutation $(ijkl)$
of $(1234)$}.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{le}}(3|6)
: here,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(3|0)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)^{(1)}_{\geq 0}
, where

{\mathfrak{g}}^{(1)}_{\geq 0}={\mathfrak{g}}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}]
, and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}=\left(\Omega^{1}(3|0)\otimes_{\Omega^{0}(3|0)}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{-1/2}(3|0)\right)\boxtimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)^{(1)}_{\geq 0}},\text{ with the natural
${\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}$-action on ${\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}$.}

Recall that
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}
denotes the irreducible
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
-module

L^{1}
with highest weight 1; its tensor square splits into the
symmetric square
L^{2}\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and the exterior square
— the trivial module
L^{0}
; accordingly, denote by
v_{1}\wedge v_{2}
and
v_{1}\bullet v_{2}
the projections of
v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\in L^{1}\otimes L^{1}
onto the anti-symmetric and symmetric
components, respectively. For any
\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\in\Omega^{1}
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in L^{1}
, we
set

\left[\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{1}\otimes v_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{1}\otimes v_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{1}\otimes v_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{1}\otimes v_{1}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}},\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{2}\otimes v_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\omega_{2}\otimes v_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{2}\otimes v_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\omega_{2}\otimes v_{2}}{\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}}$}}\right]=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})+(d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\bullet v_{2})}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})+(d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\bullet v_{2})}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})+(d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\bullet v_{2})}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{(\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\wedge v_{2})+(d\omega_{1}\wedge\omega_{2}+\omega_{1}\wedge d\omega_{2})\otimes(v_{1}\bullet v_{2})}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}},

where we naturally identify
\Omega^{0}
with
\Omega^{3}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{-1}
; we also naturally identify
\Omega^{2}\otimes_{\Omega^{0}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{-1}
with
{\mathfrak{vect}}(3|0)
by setting

\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{dx_{i}\wedge dx_{j}}{\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits}$}}=\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}\nolimits(ijk)\partial_{x_{k}}\text{
for any permutation $(ijk)$ of $(123)$}.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;\bar{n})
: We have

{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}=\left(\Pi(\Lambda^{i+1}_{\Omega^{0}}({\mathfrak{vect}}(n|0))\right)\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{i\lambda}

for
i=-1,0,\dots,n-1
. The multiplication
is given by Grozman’s operator
P_{8}
, see (
137
).

Consider the case where
n=2
more closely. Clearly, one can
interchange
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
; this possibility explains the
isomorphism

(139)

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;\bar{2})\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{1-\lambda}(2;\bar{2}),

and
hence the mysterious isomorphism
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1-\lambda}(2|2)
mentioned in (
23
).

In particular, we have additional outer automorphisms of

{\mathfrak{b}}_{1/2}(2;\bar{2})
, whereas for
\lambda=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}
and

\lambda=-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{3}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{3}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{3}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{3}{2}$}}
there are nontrivial central extensions of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
.

Therefore, for
n=2
, to the exceptional values of
\lambda=0
, 1 and

\infty
existing for any
n>2
, we should add
\lambda=-1
(for which
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits
, and hence
{\mathfrak{g}}
is not simple) and
\lambda=2
.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{m}}{\mathfrak{b}}(3|8)
: See §
11
.

2.18.
On structures preserved

In the space

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(n)
, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
preserves the tensor
\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits
of valence
(2,1)
that
defines the multiplication. If
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1
,
then the structure preserved is the tensor
\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits^{\prime}
that defines
multiplication on the maximal ideal of
\Lambda(n)
.

The exceptional simple
vectorial Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
preserve various distributions described in
[
KLS
]
. The structures preserved by the serial simple Lie superalgebras are described by the structures preserved by
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. They are as follows:

(1) The tensor products
B\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits
of a bilinear (or a volume)
form
B
preserved (perhaps, conformally, up to multiplication by
a scalar) in the fiber of the vector bundle over a
0|r
-dimensional
supermanifold on which the structure governed by
\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits
is
preserved,

(2) The tensor
\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits^{\prime}
, or
\mathop{\mathrm{mult}}\nolimits
twisted by a
\lambda
-density. Observe
that the volume element
B
can be not only
\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}
, but also

(1+\alpha\xi_{1}\ldots\xi_{n})\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{\xi}
.

The structures of other types, namely certain pseudodifferential
forms preserved by
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
, are already described.

2.18.1.
Remark: On Shmelev’s interpretations of

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)

It seemed that
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2)
had one more
realization: G. Shmelev interpreted
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)
as a deformation
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
of the
Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)
of Hamiltonian vector fields as
preserving the pseudodifferential form

d\eta^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2\lambda-1}{1-\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{2\lambda-1}{1-\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2\lambda-1}{1-\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{2\lambda-1}{1-\lambda}$}}}\left(\lambda dqdp+(1-\lambda)d\xi d\eta\right);

he also described
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)
as preserving the
pseudodifferential form

d\eta^{\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\lambda}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{\lambda}$}}-2}\left(dqdp+d\xi d\eta\right).

These interpretations are cited (but not used) in
[
ALSh
,
Po1
]
, but verification shows that Shmelev made a mistake
calculating Lie derivatives, so these interpretations are wrong.

3.
Step 2. Criterion of infinite dimensionality of
the Cartan prolong

In this section, we superize Wilson’s proof
of the following Guillemin–Quillen–Sternberg theorem.

Theorem

(Main technical theorem)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be an
infinite-dimensional transitive Lie algebra. Then, there exists an
element
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits(\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{g})|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}=1
.

The converse statement is obvious: if such
g
exists, then,
clearly, the Cartan prolong of
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
is infinite-dimensional. Indeed, in some basis,
g
takes the form

x\partial_{y}
and all the elements

x^{n}\partial_{y}
belong to the Cartan prolong.

3.2.
A reformulation of Theorem
3.1.  Theorem

Let
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\leq 0}\ {\mathcal{A}}_{i}
be a commutative

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded algebra with unit generated by
A:={\mathcal{A}}_{-1}
and let

M=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq k}\ M_{i}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded

{\mathcal{A}}
-module. (In the case we are interested in,
{\mathcal{A}}=S^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})
and
M={\mathfrak{g}}
.) We will say that
M
is a

transitive module
if

Am\neq 0~~\text{ for any non-zero }\ m\in M_{i}\text{ and any }i\geq k+1.

Notice that each element
m\in M
determines a linear mapping

m:A\longrightarrow M,\quad a\longmapsto am.

Clearly,
M
is transitive if and
only if there exists an
i\geq k+1
and a non-zero
m\in M_{i}
such
that
\ker m\neq A
.

Now it is clear that Theorem
3.1.  Theorem
can be reformulated in the
following equivalent form that is more convenient for us.

Theorem

(Main technical theorem, a version)
.

Let
M=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq k}\ M_{i}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
infinite-dimensional transitive
{\mathcal{A}}
-module. Then, there exists

m\in M_{k+1}
such that
{\mathop{\mathrm{codim}}\nolimits\ker m=1}
; hence,
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits~m=1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we can assume that
k=-1
,
i.e.
,
M=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ M_{i}
and
m\in M_{0}
.

1)
Since
{\mathcal{A}}
is commutative,
\ker am\supset\ker m
for any

a\in A
and
m\in M
.

2)
Set
r_{i}=\max_{m\in M_{i}}\ \{\dim\ker m\}
. By the above,

r_{i+1}\leq r_{i}
; hence, the sequence
r_{i}
stabilizes:

r_{N}=r_{N+1}=\dots:=r
for some
N
. Due to transitivity,
r<\dim A
, so it remains to prove that
r=\dim A-1
.

3)
We will say that
m
is
maximally degenerate
if
\dim\ker m=r
.

By step
1)
, if
a\in A
and
m\in M_{n}
for
n\geq N+1
is
maximally degenerate, then
am
is maximally degenerate too and

\ker am=\ker m
.

4)
Let
m_{0}\in M_{N+l}
for
l\geq 0
be a maximally degenerate
element. For any
n\geq N
, set

V_{n}(m_{0}):=\{m\in M_{n}\mid\ker m\supset\ker m_{0}\}.

It is clear that any non-zero element
m\in V_{n}(m_{0})
is
maximally degenerate and
\ker m=\ker m_{0}
.

5)
The following lemma is crucial in the next item. Actually, this
lemma is the key point of the whole proof of Theorem
3.2.1.  Theorem
.

Lemma

(A useful lemma)
.

Let
V
and
W
be finite-dimensional vector spaces, and let
{U\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits(V,W)}
be a subspace such
that any non-zero homomorphism
u\in U
is a monomorphism. Then,

(140)

\dim U\leq\dim W-\dim V+1.

Proof.

(This elegant proof due to J. Bernstein replaces an
original elementary but a bit cumbersome proof.) Let
P(X)
be the
projective space corresponding to a vector space
X
.

Since the mapping

{U\otimes V\longrightarrow W}
is a monomorphism for any non-zero
u\in U
, there
exists a mapping
{i:P(U)\times P(V)\longrightarrow P(W)}
which for
each fixed
a\in P(U)
and
b\in P(V)
induces embeddings

{i_{1}:a\times P(V)\longrightarrow P(W)}
and
{i_{2}:P(U)\times b\longrightarrow P(W)}
.

Let
h\in H^{2}(P(W))
,
h_{1}\in H^{2}(P(U))
and
h_{2}\in H^{2}(P(V))
be the generators of the respective cohomology rings.
Since
i_{1}
and
i_{2}
are embeddings, it follows that
i^{*}(h)=h_{1}+h_{2}
.

On the other hand, the only relations
h
,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
satisfy
are

h^{\dim W+1}=0,\quad h_{1}^{\dim U+1}=0,\quad h_{2}^{\dim V+1}=0.

Hence, replacing
h
with
i^{*}(h)=h_{1}+h_{2}
we see that the
first equation,
i.e.
,
h^{\dim W+1}=0
, is a new relation only if the inequality
(
140
) holds.
∎

6)
Each
m\in V_{n}(m_{0})
determines a monomorphism
A/\ker m_{0}\longrightarrow V_{n-1}(m_{0})
. By Lemma
3.2.1a.  Lemma
we have

\begin{array}[]{l}\dim V_{n}(m_{0})\leq\dim V_{n-1}(m_{0})-(\dim A-r)+1\Longrightarrow\\
\dim V_{n-1}(m_{0})\geq\dim V_{n}(m_{0})+(\dim A-r)-1.\end{array}

7)
Suppose
r<\dim A-1
. Then, by step
6)
,
\dim V_{n-1}(m_{0})\geq\dim V_{n}(m_{0})+1
. Let us take
m_{0}\in M_{N+s}
,
where
s>0
is arbitrary. Then,
V_{N+s}(m_{0})\neq 0
; hence,

\dim M_{n}\geq\dim V_{N}(m_{0})\geq V_{N+1}(m_{0})+1\geq\cdots\geq\dim V_{N+s}(m_{0})+s\geq 1+s.

Since
\dim M_{N}<\infty
, we get a contradiction. Thus,
r=\dim A-1
.

8)
So for some
s\geq N
, we get an element
m_{0}\in M_{s}
with

\mathop{\mathrm{codim}}\nolimits\ker m_{0}=1
. By transitivity there are
a_{1},\ldots,a_{s}\in A
such that the element
m=a_{1}\cdots a_{s}m_{0}\in M_{0}
is non-zero. Due to step
1)
,

\ker m\supset\ker m_{0}
and, by transitivity,
\ker m\neq A
. Then,

\ker m=\ker m_{0}
and
m
is the sought-for element.
∎

3.3.
A superization of Wilson’s theorem

Let
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\leq-1}\ {\mathcal{A}}_{i}
be the
supercommutative superalgebra generated by
A:={\mathcal{A}}_{-1}
. Let
\ M\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ M_{i}
be an
{\mathcal{A}}
-module. As above,
we will say that
M
is
transitive
if
\ker m\neq 0
for
any non-zero
m\in M_{i}
, where
i\geq 0
.

Theorem

(A superization of Wilson’s theorem)
.

In any infinite-dimensional transitive graded
{\mathcal{A}}
-module
M
,
there exists an element
m\in M_{0}
such that
\mathop{\mathrm{codim}}\nolimits\ker m=1|0
.

Proof.

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits A_{\bar{1}}=0|n
.

1)
Due to supercommutativity of
{\mathcal{A}}
, we have

(141)

a^{2}m=0~~\text{ for any $a\in A_{\bar{1}}$ and $m\in M$};

(142)

a_{1}\cdots a_{i}m=0~~\text{ for any }m\in M\text{ and
}a_{1},\ldots,a_{i}\in A_{{\bar{1}}}\text{ if }i>n.

2)
In view of condition (
142
) and the transitivity of
M
, we see that

A_{\bar{0}}m\neq 0
for any
m\in M_{i}
with
i\geq n
.

3)
Let
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}\subset{\mathcal{A}}
be the subalgebra generated by

A_{{\bar{0}}}
. Let
\widetilde{M}=M/\oplus_{-1\leq i\leq n-1}\ M_{i}
. Let us
consider
\widetilde{M}
as an
{\mathcal{A}}_{0}
-module and apply
Theorem
3.2.1.  Theorem
to
\widetilde{M}
. We get an element
m\in M_{n+1}
such that
\dim(\ker m\cap A_{{\bar{0}}})=\dim A_{{\bar{0}}}-1
. If

\ker m\cap A_{{\bar{1}}}\neq A_{{\bar{1}}}
, then there exists
a_{1}\in A_{{\bar{1}}}
such
that
a_{1}\notin\ker m
. Then,
a_{1}m\neq 0
and, thanks to condition (
141
), we see that

\ker a_{1}m\supset\ker m\oplus{\mathbb{C}}a_{1}
.

Similarly, for
i\leq n
, there exist
a_{1},\dots,a_{i}
such that

m_{1}=a_{1}\cdots a_{i}m\neq 0\text{~~ and $\ker m_{1}\supset\ker m+A_{{\bar{1}}}$.}

Notice that
m_{1}\in M_{s}
, where
s=n+1-i>0
. Hence,

\ker m_{1}\neq A
; thus,
\mathop{\mathrm{codim}}\nolimits\ker m_{1}=1|0
. Now we can complete
the proof in the same way as in step
8)
of the
proof of Theorem
3.2.1.  Theorem
. ∎

Corollary

(Existence of a rank-1 operator with even covector)
.

Let

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be a transitive
infinite-dimensional
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebra with
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}<\infty
for all
i
. Then, there exists
v^{*}\otimes w\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, where
v\in({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{{\bar{0}}}
and
w\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
.

4.
Step 3. Main reduction: the three cases

Lemma

(Corollaries of simplicity)
.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}_{*}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i\leq k}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
, where
k\geq 1
, is
simple, then

1)

[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}]={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
;

2)
the representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is
irreducible, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\geq}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}

is a maximal Lie subsuperalgebra;

3)
the representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is faithful.

Proof.

When one of the conditions

1)-3)
is violated, it is easy to find an ideal in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{*}
.

If 1) fails, take
{\mathfrak{i}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}]\oplus\left(\mathop{\oplus}_{i>0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}\right)
.

If 2) or 3) fails, take
{\mathfrak{i}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{n\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
, where the
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}
are recursively defined (here
\rho
is
the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-action on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
) by

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{i}}_{-1}=\begin{cases}\text{a ${\mathfrak{g}}_{0}$-invariant
subsuperspace},&\text{in case $2)$},\\
0,&\text{in case $3)$};\end{cases}\\
{\mathfrak{i}}_{0}=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{g}}_{0},&\text{in case $2)$},\\
\ker\rho,&\text{in case $3)$};\end{cases}\\
{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}=\{g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{n}\mid[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},g]\subset{\mathfrak{i}}_{n-1}\}\quad\text{
for }\quad n\geq\begin{cases}0,&\text{in case
$2)$},\\
1,&\text{in case $3)$}.\end{cases}\end{array}

The Jacobi identity implies the inclusion

[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},[{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}]]\subset[{\mathfrak{g}}_{k-1},{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}]+[{\mathfrak{g}}_{k},[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{i}}_{n}]];

the double induction on
k
and
n
shows that
{\mathfrak{i}}
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{*}
.
∎

The conditions 2) and 3) of Lemma
4.1.  Lemma
allow us to think of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
as of
an irreducible linear Lie superalgebra,
i.e.
, as a subalgebra of

{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
, where
V={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, such that the tautological
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
-module
V
is irreducible.

4.1.1.
Notation. The four cases

Consider the following four cases:

1) Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be linear superspaces,
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
.
The tensor product of the
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{i})
-actions on
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}

gives us a representation of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})
:

\rho(A,B)=A\otimes 1_{V_{2}}+1_{V_{1}}\otimes B\quad\text{ for
any }\;A\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1}),\ \ B\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2}).

It is clear that
\rho
has a
1
-dimensional kernel

{\mathbb{C}}(1_{V_{1}},\ -1_{V_{2}})
. Denote:

{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2}):=\rho({\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})).

2) The space of all operators in a superspace
V
can be endowed
with the following two structures: that of the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
and that of
the associative superalgebra
\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V)
. We will carefully
distinguish them. (Recall that we endow with the superscript
L

(resp.,
SL
) the Lie algebra (resp., Lie superalgebra)
constructed on the space of an associative algebra (resp.,
superalgebra) with the dot product replaced by the bracket (resp.,
superbracket). In particular,
\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V)^{L}={\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
. Various

super
structures on
V
induce non-isomorphic, in general,
superstructures on both
\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V)
and the associated Lie superalgebra

{\mathfrak{gl}}(V):=\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V)^{SL}
, as is clear in the supermatrix format.)

Let
V_{0}
be a
(1,1)
-dimensional superspace; define the two
associative subsuperalgebras:

\begin{array}[]{l}{{\mathcal{A}}}\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(1|1)=\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
b&a\end{pmatrix}$}\mid a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}\right\},\\
C({\mathcal{A}})\subset\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(1|1)=\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V_{0})=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits\left\{\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}c&d\\
-d&c\end{pmatrix}$}\mid c,d\in{\mathbb{C}}\right\}.\end{array}

Set
J:=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}$}
and
\Pi:=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}$}
; clearly,
1_{2}
and
J
generate
{{\mathcal{A}}}
, while
1_{2}
and

\Pi
generate
C({{\mathcal{A}}})
. Note that

J^{2}=-1,\quad\Pi^{2}=1;\quad J\Pi=\Pi J=0,

and therefore

{{\mathcal{A}}}\simeq C({{\mathcal{A}}})
over
{\mathbb{C}}
and
[{{\mathcal{A}}},C({{\mathcal{A}}})]=0
; this
justifies our notation:
C({{\mathcal{A}}})
is the centralizer of
{\mathcal{A}}
in

\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(1|1)
.

If
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
are linear superspaces of superdimension
m\varepsilon

and
n
, respectively, then the Lie superalgebras
(\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V_{1})\otimes{{\mathcal{A}}})^{SL}
and
(C({{\mathcal{A}}})\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{Mat}}\nolimits(V_{2}))^{SL}
of the
operators acting in
V_{1}\otimes V_{0}
and
V_{0}\otimes V_{2}
,
respectively, can be identified with
{\mathfrak{q}}(m)
and
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
,
respectively.

Now, let
V:=V_{1}\otimes V_{0}\otimes V_{2}
. Then, a natural

{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{1})\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{2})
-action
\rho
in
V
is defined:

(143)

\begin{split}&\rho\left(\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}$},\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}C&D\\
D&C\end{pmatrix}$}\right):=(A\otimes 1_{V_{0}}+B\otimes J)\otimes 1_{V_{2}}+1_{V_{1}}\otimes(1_{V_{0}}\otimes C+\Pi\otimes D)\\
&\text{for any $\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
B&A\end{pmatrix}$}\in{\mathfrak{q}}(m)$ and $\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}C&D\\
D&C\end{pmatrix}$}\in{\mathfrak{q}}(n)$.}\end{split}

This representation has a
1
-dimensional kernel.
Denote

(144)

{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{2}):=\rho({\mathfrak{q}}(n_{1})\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{2})).

3) Let
V_{1}
be a linear superspace of superdimension
(r,s)
, let

{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\otimes\Lambda(n)
,
{\mathfrak{k}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
and let

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{n}}\ltimes{\mathfrak{k}}
with the natural action of
{\mathfrak{k}}
on the ideal

{\mathfrak{n}}
. The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
has a natural faithful
representation
\rho
in the linear superspace
V=V_{1}\otimes\Lambda(n)
defined for any
A\otimes\varphi\in{\mathfrak{n}}
,
D\in{\mathfrak{k}}
, and
v\otimes\psi\in V
by the formulas

\begin{array}[]{l}\rho(A\otimes\varphi)(v\otimes\psi)=(-1)^{p(\varphi)p(v)}Av\otimes\varphi\psi,\\
\rho(D)(v\otimes\psi)=-(-1)^{p(D)p(v)}v\otimes D(\psi).\end{array}

In what follows, we will always identify the elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
with
their images under
\rho
. Therefore, we will consider
{\mathfrak{g}}
as embedded
in
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
.

4) Let
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n};\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{n};z)
be the Heisenberg superalgebra, with the
non-zero relations
[\xi_{i},\eta_{j}]=\delta_{ij}z
. The
nontrivial irreducible finite-dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)
that maps
z
to the operator of multiplication by
1
is
realized in
\Lambda(\xi)
via

\xi_{i}\longmapsto\xi_{i},\quad\eta_{i}\longmapsto\partial_{i}.

The normalizer of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)
in
{\mathfrak{gl}}(\Lambda(n))
is
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)
or, in terms of
differential operators:

{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\xi_{i},\partial_{i},\xi_{i}\partial_{j},\partial_{i}\partial_{j},\xi_{i}\xi_{j})_{i,j=1}^{n}.

Theorem

(Irreducible linear Lie superalgebras)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
be an irreducible linear Lie superalgebra
that is neither almost simple nor a central extension of an almost simple Lie superalgebra. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is contained in one of the following four Lie
subsuperalgebras of
{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
:

1)

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
for any

V_{1}
,
V_{2}
such that
V=V_{1}\otimes V_{2}
and
\dim V_{i}>1
for
i=1,2
;

2)

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{q}}(m)\bigodot{\mathfrak{q}}(n)\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
, where

V=V_{1}\otimes V_{0}\otimes V_{2}
for any
V_{0}
,
V_{1}
,
V_{2}
such
that

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V_{0}=(1,1)
,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V_{1}=m\varepsilon
,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V_{2}=n
and

mn>1
;

3)

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\otimes\Lambda(n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
for any
V_{1}
and
n
such that

V=V_{1}\otimes\Lambda(n)
; the natural representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in

V
is induced;

4)

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
for
n>1
,
where
V\simeq\Lambda(n)
or
\Pi(\Lambda(n))
.

For proof, see Subsection
4.3
.

Corollary

(The 3 cases)
.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
is an irreducible
linear superalgebra with a rank-
1
operator in
V
, then only one
of the following
3
cases is possible:

1)
the tautological representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
V
is induced;

2)

{\mathfrak{g}}
is almost simple or a central extension of an almost simple
Lie superalgebra;

3)

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2,2)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(4)
.

Indeed, none of the following matrix Lie superalgebras

{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})\;\text{ with $\dim V_{i}>1$ \qquad or
\qquad${\mathfrak{q}}(n_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{q}}(n_{2})$ with $n_{1}n_{2}>1$}

have operators of rank 1; the only possibility for the Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)
to have a
rank-1 operator occurs for
n=2
.

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
4.2.  Theorem
(
[
ShM
]
)

In this Subsection,
