{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
is an irreducible linear Lie
superalgebra, and
\rho
is its tautological representation in superspace

V
. We will assume that
\dim V>1
, because the case where
\dim V=1
is
trivial. By hypothesis,
{\mathfrak{g}}
contains an ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Our proof of Theorem
4.2.  Theorem
is based on the following result
— a superization of a statement well-known from
Dixmier’s book
[
Di
, Subsection 5.4.1]
.

Theorem

(The 3 cases of irreducible linear Lie superalgebras: A, B, C)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
be an irreducible linear Lie superalgebra,
\rho
its
tautological representation (in particular,
\rho
is faithful). Let

{\mathfrak{k}}
be an ideal of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\dim{\mathfrak{k}}>1
. Then, only one of the
following
3
cases is possible:

A)

\rho|_{\mathfrak{k}}
is a multiple of an irreducible
{\mathfrak{k}}
-module
\tau
and the multiplicity of
\tau
is
>1
;

B)
there exists a subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
such that

{\mathfrak{k}}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\rho\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}\nolimits^{{\mathfrak{g}}}_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\sigma
for an
irreducible
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module
\sigma
;

C)

\rho|_{\mathfrak{k}}
is irreducible.

Proof.

The argument, see
[
ShM
]
, follows the lines of
[
Di
, Subsection 5.4.1]
with one novel case:
irreducible modules of
Q
-type might occur. Fortunately, to
treat them is quite straightforward and we would rather save paper
by omitting the verification. ∎

We will say that the
representation
\rho
is of type A,
B, or C with respect to the ideal

{\mathfrak{k}}
if the corresponding
case of Theorem
4.3.1.  Theorem
holds. Lemmas

4.3.1a.  Lemma
,
4.3.1b.  Lemma
, and
4.3.3a.  Lemma
deal with types A, B, and C,
respectively.

Lemma

(Case A)
.

If
\rho
is of type A with respect to the
ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
, then for some
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, either
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})
or
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{q}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{q}}(V_{2})
.

Proof.

Let
V
be the superspace on which
\tau
acts.
Then,
V=V_{1}\otimes U
for some
U
and

\rho(h)(v\otimes u)=\tau(h)v\otimes u\;\text{ for any }\;h\in{\mathfrak{k}},\;v\in V_{1},\;u\in U,\text{~~{i.e.},}

(145)

the operators
\rho(h)
are of the form

\tau(h)\otimes 1_{U}
.

On the other hand,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the stabilizer of
\tau
. So, for any

g\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, there exists
s(g)\in\mathop{\mathrm{End}}\nolimits V_{1}
such that
\tau([g,h])=[s(g),\tau(h)]
for any
h\in{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Let us consider the operator
T(g)=\rho(g)-s(g)\otimes 1_{U}
in
V
.
For
h\in{\mathfrak{k}}
, we have

[T(g),\rho(h)]=[\rho(g)-s(g)\otimes 1_{U},\ \rho(h)]=\rho([g,h])-[s(g),\tau(h)]\otimes 1_{U}=0\,,

i.e.
,
T(g)
commutes with all the operators of the form
\rho(h)
, where

h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, and, since
\tau
is irreducible,
T(g)
is of the form

T(g)=\begin{cases}1_{V_{1}}\otimes A(g),&\text{ if }\;\tau\text{ is of $G$-type},\\
1_{V_{1}}\otimes A(g)+J\otimes B(g),&\text{ if $\tau$ is of $Q$-type},\end{cases}

where
J
is an odd generator of
C(\tau)
, the centralizer of
\tau
.

Therefore,
\rho(g)
is of the form

(146)

\rho(g)=\begin{cases}s(g)\otimes 1_{U}+1_{V_{1}}\otimes A(g),&\text{if $\tau$ is of $G$-type},\\
s(g)\otimes 1_{U}+1_{V_{1}}\otimes A(g)+J\otimes B(g),&\text{if $\tau$
is of $Q$-type}.\end{cases}

Formulas (
145
)–(
146
) show that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
contained in

\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{gl}}(V_{2})\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V),\text{ where $V_{2}=U$,}&\text{if $\tau$ is
of $G$-type},\\
{\mathfrak{q}}(V_{1})\bigodot{\mathfrak{q}}(V_{2})\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V),\text{ where $V_{2}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,J)\otimes U$,}&\text{if $\tau$ is of $Q$-type}.\qed\end{cases}

Lemma

(Case B)
.

If
\rho
is of
type B with respect to the ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes\Lambda(n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)\;\text{ for
some $V$ and $n$.}

Proof

of this lemma follows from the definition of the induced
representation. ∎

4.3.2.
Completion of the proof of Theorem
4.2.  Theorem

It remains to consider the case where
\rho
has the following
property: for any ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
, either
\rho
is of type

C
with respect to
{\mathfrak{k}}
or
\rho|_{\mathfrak{k}}
is the multiple
of a character.

Let
{\mathfrak{r}}
be the radical of the linear Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Lemma

(On the radical
{\mathfrak{r}}
in cases A and C)
.

If the radical
{\mathfrak{r}}
is commutative and
\rho
is of type A or C with respect to

{\mathfrak{r}}
, then
{\dim{\mathfrak{r}}=0}
or
1
.

Proof

follows from the facts that any irreducible module over
a commutative Lie superalgebra is 1-dimensional (even or odd) and
\rho
is faithful. ∎

4.3.3.
When the radical
{\mathfrak{r}}
is not commutative

In this case, consider the derived series of
{\mathfrak{r}}
:

{\mathfrak{r}}\supset{\mathfrak{r}}_{1}\supset\dots\supset{\mathfrak{r}}_{k}\supset{\mathfrak{r}}_{k+1}=0,\;\text{
where ${\mathfrak{r}}_{i+1}=[{\mathfrak{r}}_{i},{\mathfrak{r}}_{i}]$. }

Clearly, each
{\mathfrak{r}}_{i}
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{g}}
and the last non-zero
ideal,
{\mathfrak{r}}_{k}
, is commutative.

Lemma

(Case C)
.

If
\rho
is of type
C with respect to

{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1}
and
\rho|_{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k}}
is scalar, then

either
{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1}\simeq{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)
or
{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1}\simeq{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n-1)
,
and

either
V\simeq\Lambda(n)
or
V\simeq\Pi(\Lambda(n))
.

In these cases,
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)
.

Proof.

We see that if the restrictions
\rho|_{{\mathfrak{r}}_{i}}
are
irreducible for all
i
, then

1)
\dim{\mathfrak{r}}_{k}=1
because
\rho
is faithful;

2)
{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1}={\mathfrak{hei}}(0|m)
for some
m
;

3)
\rho|_{{\mathfrak{r}}_{k-1}}
is irreducible and faithful, so it can be
realized in the superspace of functions,
\Lambda(n)
, or in

\Pi(\Lambda(n))
, where
n=\lfloor\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}\rfloor+1
;

4)
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(2n)
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is contained
in the normalizer of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|2n)
in the spinor representation of
{\mathfrak{o}}(n)
.
∎

Lemma

(Ideal of type A or B)
.

Let
\dim{\mathfrak{r}}\leq 1
, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either semi-simple or a central
extension of a semi-simple Lie superalgebra, but NOT almost simple
or a central extension of an almost simple Lie superalgebra. Then, there exists an ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
such that
\rho
is of type A or B
with respect to
{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Proof.

Let
\dim{\mathfrak{r}}=0
,
i.e.
, let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be semi-simple. As

{\mathfrak{g}}
is not almost simple, then, see Subsection
2.4
, the following alternative
arises:

(147)

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{either ${\mathfrak{g}}$ contains an ideal ${\mathfrak{k}}$ of the form
${\mathfrak{k}}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda(n)$ with simple ${\mathfrak{h}}$ and
$n>0$},\\
\text{or ${\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{j\leq k}\ {\mathfrak{s}}_{j}$, where each
${\mathfrak{s}}_{j}$ is almost simple and $k>1$.}\end{array}

Lemma

(Irreducible representation of type B)
.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{j\leq k}\ {\mathfrak{s}}_{j}
and
k\geq 2
, then
any irreducible faithful representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of type B with
respect to any its ideal
{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}
.

Proof.

Since the stabilizer of any irreducible representation
of
{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}
is the whole
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the type of any irreducible
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
with respect to
{\mathfrak{s}}_{j}
can be either B or
C. Due to faithfulness of the representation, type C is ruled
out.
∎

Lemma

(No faithful irreducible representations)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a simple Lie superalgebra and let

{\mathfrak{k}}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda(n)
. Then,
{\mathfrak{k}}
has no faithful irreducible
finite-dimensional representations.

For proof, see Subsection
4.3.3
; let us pass to corollaries.

Corollary

(No type A irreducible representations: conditions)
.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
contains an ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
of the form described in eq. (
147
), then

{\mathfrak{g}}
cannot have any faithful irreducible finite-dimensional
representation of type A with respect to the ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Corollary

(Lemma

4.3.4.  Lemma
holds for semi-simple Lie superalgebras)
.

Lemma
4.3.6.  Lemma
and Corollary
4.3.7.  Corollary
prove Lemma

4.3.4.  Lemma
for semi-simple Lie superalgebras.

Lemma

(A useful fact)
.

For any simple Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
, we have

[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}\quad\textit{and}\quad[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}]={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\,.

Proof

is obvious and so is left to the reader. (Or see
[
K3
]
.)
∎

Proof of Lemma
4.3.6.  Lemma

For
n>0
, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}
contains a supercommutative
ideal

{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}.

Moreover, if
n>2
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}
contains a supercommutative ideal

{\mathfrak{n}}_{2}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda^{n-1}(\xi)\oplus{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}.

Thanks to Theorem
4.2.  Theorem
, any irreducible representation
\rho

of
{\mathfrak{k}}
in the superspace
V
is obtained as follows: take an irreducible representation
(character
\chi
) of an ideal
{\mathfrak{n}}_{i}
, the stabilizer
{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{k}}}(\chi)
, and an irreducible representation
\widetilde{\rho}

of
{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{k}}}(\chi)
whose restriction to
{\mathfrak{n}}_{i}
is a multiple
of
\chi
. Then,

\rho=\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}\nolimits^{{\mathfrak{k}}}_{{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{k}}}(\chi)}(\widetilde{\rho})\;\text{~~~ for
some $\widetilde{\rho}$.}

Let us show that if
\dim V<\infty
, then

\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=0
. Since

{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{k}}
, this would
contradict faithfulness of
\rho
. Indeed,
\dim V<\infty
if
and only if
{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{k}}}(\chi)\supset{\mathfrak{k}}_{{\bar{0}}}
.

1)
n=2k+1
. For any
f\in{\mathfrak{h}}
and
g=h\otimes 1\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}\otimes 1
, we have

0=\chi([g,f\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}])=\chi([h,f]\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}),

implying that

\chi|_{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}]\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}\overset{\text{ (Lemma \ref{l3365})}}{=}\chi|_{({\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})}=0.

Since
\chi
is a character, it follows that

\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=0
, hence

\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=0
.

2)
n=2k>2
. For any pair

\begin{array}[]{l}g=h\otimes l_{1}(\xi)\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\xi)\subset{\mathfrak{k}}_{{\bar{0}}},\\
g_{1}=h_{1}\otimes l_{n-1}(\xi)\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes\Lambda^{n-1}(\xi)\subset({\mathfrak{n}}_{2})_{{\bar{0}}},\end{array}

we have

0=\chi([g,g_{1}])=\pm\chi([h,h_{1}])\otimes l_{1}(\xi)l_{n-1}(\xi),

i.e.
,

\chi|_{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}]\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=0\ .

But
\chi
is a character; therefore,

\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}}=0,\text{~~ and,
finally,~~}\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}=0}.

3)
n=2
. Then,
{\mathfrak{k}}
contains a commutative ideal

{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2}
; let
\chi
be a character of
{\mathfrak{n}}
.

If
\chi
generates a finite-dimensional representation of
{\mathfrak{k}}
,
then
{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{k}}}(\chi)\supset{\mathfrak{k}}_{{\bar{0}}}
,
i.e.
,

(148)

\chi([f,g\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2}])=0~~\text{~~~~for any~~}f\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},\;g\in{\mathfrak{h}}.

If
g\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}
, then eq. (
148
) holds automatically by
the parity considerations. Let
g\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}
. Then, nevertheless,

\chi([f,g\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2}])=\chi([f,g]\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2})=0,

and therefore
\chi|_{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}]\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2}}=0
.

Set

{\mathfrak{m}}:={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes(\Lambda^{1}(\xi)\oplus\Lambda^{2}(\xi)).

Clearly,
{\mathfrak{m}}
is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{k}}
, and
{\mathfrak{st}}_{{\mathfrak{n}}}(\chi)={\mathfrak{m}}
.
Therefore, the irreducible representation
\rho
of
{\mathfrak{m}}
given by

\chi
is such that
\rho|_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
is a multiple of
\chi
. Let

{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}:={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\xi)\oplus([{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}]\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}})\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\xi).

Clearly,
{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{m}}
, and

[{\mathfrak{n}}_{1},{\mathfrak{m}}]\subset([{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}]\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}})\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\xi)\subset\ker\;\chi.

Thus,
{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}\subset\ker\;\rho
since the subspace

V^{\rho({\mathfrak{n}}_{1})}=\{v\in V\mid\rho({\mathfrak{n}}_{1})v=0\}
is

\rho({\mathfrak{n}}_{1})
-invariant and non-zero. But
{\mathfrak{m}}/{\mathfrak{n}}_{1}
is a solvable, and by the super analog of
Lie’s theorem
(for its
formulation and proof over
{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
S0
]
) any of its irreducible finite-dimensional
representations is of the same form as
\chi
. Let it be
\chi
, for
definiteness sake.

This means that
\chi|_{[{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}]\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\xi)}=0
,
i.e.
,

\chi|_{{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\xi)}=0
, and since
{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\xi_{1}\xi_{2}
is an ideal in
{\mathfrak{k}}
, the representation of
{\mathfrak{k}}
given by

\chi
cannot be faithful. Lemma is proved. ∎

4.4.
Central extensions of semi-simple Lie
superalgebras

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie superalgebra, and let
{\mathfrak{r}}
be
its radical of dimension 1. Then,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{r}}
is semi-simple by definition.

First, consider the case where
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\leq k}\ {\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
with each
{\mathfrak{s}}_{i}
being almost
simple and
k>1
. Let
\pi:{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
be the natural
projection. The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{k}}:=\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{1})
is an ideal
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
\dim{\mathfrak{k}}>1
.

Lemma

(No type C irreducible representations)
.

If
k>1
, then
\rho
cannot be irreducible of type C with respect
to
{\mathfrak{k}}=\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{s}}_{1})
.

Proof.

Set
{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 1}\ ({\mathfrak{s}}_{i})_{{\bar{0}}}
. Clearly,
[{\mathfrak{s}}_{1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}]=0
. Since
\rho({\mathfrak{r}})

acts by scalar operators and
\rho
is a finite-dimensional
representation, it follows that
[\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{g}}_{+}),{\mathfrak{k}}]=0
,
i.e.
,

\rho(\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{g}}_{+}))
consists of intertwining operators of

\rho|_{{\mathfrak{n}}}
,
i.e.
,
\rho(\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{g}}_{+}))={\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1
and, since

\rho
is faithful,
\pi^{-1}({\mathfrak{g}}_{+})={\mathfrak{r}}
implying
{\mathfrak{g}}_{+}=0
.
∎

4.4.2.
The case where
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
contains an ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
of
the form described in eq. (
147
).

The central extension is
defined by a cocycle
c\colon\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}
. The cocycle condition reads

c(f,[g,h])=c([f,g],h)+(-1)^{p(f)p(g)}c(g,[f,h])~~\text{~~for
any~~}f,g,h\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}.

As earlier, we assume that
{\mathfrak{g}}
has a faithful finite-dimensional
representation; so the restriction of
c
to
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
is zero. Moreover,
c|_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}}=0
, by parity
considerations. Therefore,

(149)

c|_{\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}\times\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}}=0.

Lemma

(Technical)
.

In the notation of Lemma
4.3.6.  Lemma
, set
{\mathfrak{L}}^{m}:={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda^{m}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
. Then,

{c|_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}\times{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}}=0}
.

Proof.

If
n=2k+1
, the condition (
149
)
means that

c|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}\otimes\Lambda^{n}(\xi)\times{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}\otimes\Lambda^{n}(\xi)}=0.

Let
g_{\bar{1}},h_{\bar{1}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}
and
h_{\bar{0}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{l}c(f_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[g_{\bar{1}},h_{\bar{1}}]\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})=-c(f_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[g_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},h_{\bar{1}}])=\\
-c([f_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},g_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}],h_{\bar{1}})-c(g_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[f_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},h_{\bar{1}}])=0.\end{array}

Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}=[{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}]
(by Lemma

4.3.9.  Lemma
), it follows that
c|_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}\times{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}}=0
.

If
n=2k
, the condition (
149
) means that

c|_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}\otimes\Lambda^{n}(\xi)\times{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}\otimes\Lambda^{n}(\xi)}=0.

Let
f_{\bar{1}},h_{\bar{1}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}
and
g_{\bar{0}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{l}c(f_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[g_{\bar{0}},h_{\bar{1}}]\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})=-c(f_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[g_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},h_{\bar{1}}])=\\
-c([f_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},g_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}],h_{\bar{1}})-c(g_{\bar{0}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},[f_{\bar{1}}\otimes\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n},h_{\bar{1}}])=0.\end{array}

Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}=[{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}},{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}]
(by Lemma

4.3.9.  Lemma
), it follows that
c|_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}\times{\mathfrak{L}}^{n}}=0
.
∎

Lemma

(Technical)
.

For any
m=0,1,\dots,n

and
k=n-m
,
n-m+1,\dots,n
, we have
c|_{{\mathfrak{L}}^{k}\times{\mathfrak{L}}^{m}}=0
.

Proof.

We will argue by reverse double induction on
k
and

m
. For
k=m=n
, Lemma
4.4.2a.  Lemma
gives the statement.

Suppose the statement is true for all
k>k_{0}
and
k_{0}+m\geq n
. Let us
show that it is true for
k=k_{0}
as well. Observe that, see Subsection
2.4
,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
contains
n
elements
\eta_{i}
such
that

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{\eta_{i}}|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}=\partial_{\xi_{i}}+D_{i},\;\;\text{where
$D_{i}\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)$ and $\deg D_{i}>0$}

in the standard grading
of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
.

Let
\varphi\in\Lambda^{k_{0}+1}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
,
\psi\in\Lambda^{m}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{n})
; let
g,h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
be such that

p(g\otimes\psi)=\bar{0}
and
p(h\otimes\psi)=\bar{1}
. Then, we
have

\begin{gathered}c([\eta_{i},g\otimes\varphi],h\otimes\psi)=(-1)^{p(g)}c(g\otimes\eta_{i}(\varphi),\ h\otimes\psi)=\\
\phantom{xxxxxxxx}\hskip 56.9055pt(-1)^{p(g)}\left(c(g\otimes\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}},\ h\otimes\psi)+c(g\otimes D_{i}\varphi,\ h\otimes\psi)\right).\end{gathered}

The term
D_{i}\varphi\in\left(\mathop{\oplus}_{s\geq k_{0}+1}\ \Lambda^{s}(\xi)\right)
from the last summand vanishes by
the induction hypothesis.

On the other hand,

(150)

c([\eta_{i},g\otimes\varphi],\ h\otimes\psi)=c(\eta_{i},\ [g\otimes\varphi,h\otimes\psi])+c([\eta_{i},\ h\otimes\psi],g\otimes\varphi).

Since

\deg\varphi+\deg\psi=k_{0}+1+m>n
, we see that
[g\otimes\varphi,h\otimes\psi]=0
. The second summand in the right-hand side of
(
150
) vanishes thanks to condition (
149
). So

c(g\otimes\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}},\ h\otimes\psi)=0~~\text{~~
for any
$g\otimes\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial\varphi}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}\in{\mathfrak{L}}_{\bar{1}}^{k_{0}}$ and $h\otimes\psi\in{\mathfrak{L}}_{\bar{1}}^{m}$.}\hskip 85.35826pt\qed

Lemma

(Technical)
.

The ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
has no faithful irreducible finite-dimensional representations.

Proof

reproduces the proof of Lemma
4.3.6.  Lemma
word-for-word
with the help of Lemmas
4.4.2a.  Lemma
and
4.4.2b.  Lemma
. ∎

Corollary

(No type C representations)
.

The representation
\rho
cannot be of type C with respect to the
ideal
{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Lemmas A, B, C (
i.e.
,
4.3.1a.  Lemma
,
4.3.1b.  Lemma
,
4.3.3a.  Lemma
) combined with Lemmas
4.3.2a.  Lemma
and
4.3.4.  Lemma
prove
Theorem
4.2.  Theorem
. ∎

5.
Step 4. Prolongation of the induced
representations

In this step we describe all the simple infinite-dimensional

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebras of depth 1 for which the irreducible
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is induced from
a representation of a subsuperalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.

But, first of all, we describe certain universal Lie superalgebras.

5.1.
Universal property of
{\mathfrak{vect}}
,
{\mathfrak{k}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded transitive Lie superalgebra, and let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
be
an irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module. The following universal properties are immediate consequences of
the definition of Cartan prolongations.

1) If
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=(m|n)
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
.

2) If
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-2\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{<0}={\mathfrak{hei}}(2m|n)
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)
.

3) If
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-2\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{<0}={\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
.

The following are all the Lie superalgebras with “big”
nonpositive part:

1^{\circ})
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=(m|n)
. Moreover, let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)
and

\dim{\mathfrak{g}}=\infty
. If
m>1
, then,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
, or

{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
, or
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)):={\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}E
, where
E:=\sum_{1\leq i\leq m+n}\ x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}
.

If
m=1
, two more possibilities appear:

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)
or
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)):={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}E
.

2^{\circ})
Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-2\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)
be such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\leq 0}={\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)_{\leq 0}
and
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}=\infty
. Let either
m\neq 0
or
m=0
but
n\neq 6
; then,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)
or
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n)):={\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}E
, where
E:=\sum_{1\leq i\leq 2m+n}\ x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}
, where the
x_{i}
are all indeterminates except
t
. If
m=0

and
n=6
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
.

3^{\circ})
If
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-2\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}\subset{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
is such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{<0}={\mathfrak{ba}}(n)
,
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}=\infty

and
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple, then
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{m}}(n)
, or
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}(n)
, or

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
for
\lambda\neq 0
.

Lemma

(Technical)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be
a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded transitive Lie superalgebra, and let
{W\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}
be
a subsuperspace. Let
F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
, where
k>0
, be such that

[w,F]=0
for any
w\in W
. Then, for any
u\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, the
element
[u,F]
commutes with any
w\in W
.

Proof.

By the Jacobi identity

[w,[u,F]]=(-1)^{p(w)p(u)}[u,[w,F]]+[[w,u],F]=0.

More explicitly, Lemma
5.2.  Lemma
says that if the expression
of
F
does not involve certain indeterminates, then
[\partial_{i},F]
does not involve those indeterminates either, for any
i
. ∎

Theorem

(On 3 prolongations: cases a), b), c))
.

Let
\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=m|n
, where
(m|n)\neq(1|0)\text{ or }(0|1)
. Then,

a)

(\Lambda(\xi),\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi))_{*}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)
.

b)

(\Pi(\Lambda(\xi)),\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi))_{*}={\mathfrak{m}}(k;k)
.

c)

(V\otimes\Lambda(\xi),{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi))_{*}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)
.

Proof.

It is clear that the Lie superalgebras in the right-hand sides of the isomorphisms a)–c) are subalgebras of the respective Lie superalgebras in the left-hand
side.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq l}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
be one
of the superalgebras in the left-hand sides of the isomorphisms a)–c).
Let

(151)

\begin{array}[]{l}y=-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{V}\otimes 1\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes\Lambda(\xi),\\
x=\Sigma\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}.\\
\end{array}

Then,
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{y}

determines the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
we consider in cases a)–c), while
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{x}

determines a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of each component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
:

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l,i}=\{g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\mid\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{x}(g)=i\cdot g\}.

Note that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}

and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0,i}
. Set

W:={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}=(V\otimes 1)\oplus(V\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\xi)).

We see that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
does not contain non-zero elements that commute
with
W
. Hence, by Lemma
5.2.  Lemma
, none of the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
for
l>0

contains non-zero elements that commute with
W
. But if

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-s}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l,i}
, then the
components
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l+1,t}
with
t<-s-1
should commute with
W
.
Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l+1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-s-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l+1,i}

and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-l-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l,i}
.

5.3.1.
Cases a) and b) of Theorem
5.3.  Theorem

Consider the operator
T=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{2y+x}
, see eq. (
151
).
Its eigenvalues are integers and, in each
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
, there are
\geq l-1
linearly independent eigenvectors. Set

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{s}=\{g\in{\mathfrak{g}}\mid T(g)=s\cdot g\}.

Clearly,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{s}\subset\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq l\leq s+1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
and
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{s}<\infty
.
Hence, we obtain a new
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading:
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-2\leq s}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{s}^{T}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}
. The
negative part of this
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading is as follows, where
\pi
is a natural projection, see Subsection
4.4
:

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-2}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}=\begin{cases}{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1,&\text{in case
a)},\\
{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\pi(1),&\text{in case b)};\end{cases}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,-1}=\begin{cases}\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k},\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{k}}),&\text{in case a)},\\
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\pi(\xi_{1}),\dots,\pi(\xi_{k}),\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{k}}),&\text{in case b)}.\end{cases}\end{array}

Let us check that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
is transitive,
i.e.
,

(152)

if
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{l}
with
l\geq 0
and

[{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1},g]=0
, then
g=0
.

Indeed, for
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying (
152
), we have

[W,g]=0
; hence,
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. But only the subspace

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-2}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
commutes with the

\partial_{\xi_{i}}
for all
i
.

Due to the universal property of the Lie superalgebras of series

{\mathfrak{k}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
(see Subsection
5.1
) this means that

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}\subset\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{k}}(1|k),\text{ and thus
${\mathfrak{g}}^{T}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|k)$},&\text{in
case a)},\\
{\mathfrak{m}}(k),\text{ and thus ${\mathfrak{g}}^{T}={\mathfrak{m}}(k)$},&\text{in case b)}.\end{cases}

5.3.2.
Case c) of Theorem
5.3.  Theorem

Set

W_{0}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}=V\otimes 1
.

Lemma

(Technical)
.

If
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V\neq(1|0)\text{ or }(0|1)
and
F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
is such
that
[F,W_{0}]=0
, then
F=0
. In particular,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}=0
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.2.  Lemma
, the element
[F,v\otimes\varphi]\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

commutes with
W_{0}
for any
v\in V
and
\varphi\in\Lambda(\xi)
.
Hence,
[F,v\otimes\varphi]
belongs to
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
.

Denote
D_{v,\varphi}:=[F,v\otimes\varphi]
. By transitivity,

D_{v,\varphi}\psi\neq 0
for some
v\in V
and
\varphi,\psi\in\Lambda(\xi)
. Take
u\in V
linearly independent with
v
. Then
(the symbol
\pm
stands for a sign inessential to
us here),

(153)

\begin{gathered}{}[[F,v\otimes\varphi],u\otimes\psi]\pm[v\otimes\varphi,[F,u\otimes\psi]]=\pm u\otimes D_{v,\varphi}\psi\pm v\otimes D_{u,\psi}\varphi\neq 0.\end{gathered}

By the Jacobi identity, the expression (
153
) should vanish. This
contradiction completes the proof. ∎

Lemmas
5.2.  Lemma
and
5.3.2a.  Lemma
together mean that none of the components

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
with
l>0
contains elements that commute with
W_{0}
. Hence,
the least degree of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading with respect to
\xi
in

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l+1}
cannot be less than same in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
for
l\geq 0
. Thus,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{l,i}
.

Consider the operator
T=\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{y+x}
, see eq. (
151
). Its eigenvalues are integers
and, in each
{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}
, there are
\geq l-1
linearly independent
eigenvectors. So, as in cases a) and b) of Theorem
5.3.  Theorem
,

\dim{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{s}=\dim\{g\in{\mathfrak{g}}\mid T(g)=s\cdot g\}<\infty.

Hence, in

{\mathfrak{g}}
, we get a new
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
.
The negative part of this
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading is

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,-1}=V\otimes 1\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{k}}).

Moreover, by Lemma
5.3.2a.  Lemma
, the elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
that commute
with
W_{0}=V\otimes 1\subset{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1}
form the subspace

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)
. We can directly verify that the elements
of this subspace that commute with all the
\partial_{\xi_{i}}
span

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1}
. Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
is transitive. Due to the universality
property of the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k)
, see
Subsection
5.1
, this means that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k)
;
thus,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k)
.
∎

Corollary

(Technical)
.

Let

\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})
.

1)
If
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)
with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)_{-1}=\Lambda(\xi)
is such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
does not
contain non-zero elements that commute with all elements of
W_{0}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|k;k)
.

2)
If
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{m}}(k;k)
with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{m}}(k;k)_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(\xi))
is such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
contains no non-zero elements that commute with all elements of
W_{0}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot\Pi(1)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k+1;k)
; hence,
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}<\infty
.

Proof

is the same as that in case c) of Theorem
5.3.  Theorem
. ∎

5.5.
Back to the classification

Let, as above,

\xi=(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k})
and let
V
be an
m|n
-dimensional linear
superspace with coordinates
u=(u_{1},\dots,u_{n+m})
.

Theorem

(3 more cases)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i}^{\infty}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be a simple
infinite-dimensional transitive
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebra with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=V\otimes\Lambda(\xi)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)
.

1)
If
\dim V>1
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|n+k;k)
,
or
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n+k;k)
for
m>1
, or
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n+k;k)
;

2)
if
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(1,0)
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either
{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|k;k)
,
or
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|k;k)
, or
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)
, or
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}(;3\xi)
.

3)
if
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(0,1)
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either
{\mathfrak{m}}(k;k)
, or

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(k;k)
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proof.

Case 1)
. By Theorem
5.3.  Theorem
we see that
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)
.
Thus, we can use the realization of the elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}
by vector
fields in indeterminates
u,\xi
. The
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading with respect to
\xi
in each
degree-
l
component
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l}
determines a filtration in

{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq l}^{\infty}(\mathop{\oplus}_{i}\ \mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{l,i})
be the corresponding bigraded
Lie superalgebra,
i.e.
,

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{l,i}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j\geq i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j>i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j\geq i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j>i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j\geq i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j>i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j\geq i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}{{\mathfrak{g}}_{l}\cap\mathop{\oplus}_{j>i}\ {\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{l,j}}$}}.

By Lemma
5.3.2a.  Lemma
, the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is irreducible.
This gives the following conditions on
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,*}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i}\ \mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,i}
:

(154)

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{k}})={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{0,-1},\\
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{0,0}={\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(\xi)={\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(0|k),\\
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}\text{ acts irreducibly in }V\otimes 1\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}.\end{array}

Without loss of generality we can assume that

(155)

\text{the $\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}$-module
$\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{\xi_{i}}\mid i=1,\dots,k)$ is
irreducible}.

Indeed, if it is reducible and,
e.g.
,
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{s}})
is a submodule, then

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset\Big(\left({\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\otimes\Lambda(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{s})\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{s})\right)\otimes\Lambda(\xi_{s+1},\dots,\xi_{k})\Big)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi_{s+1},\dots,\xi_{k}).

So we set
\widetilde{V}=V\otimes\Lambda(\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{s})
and

\widetilde{\xi}=(\xi_{s+1},\cdots,\xi_{k})
. Now, use the condition

[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}]={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. As we saw in the proof of Theorem

5.3.  Theorem
,

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{1}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i\geq-1}{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{1,i},

and
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,-1}
can be obtained only by bracketing

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{-1,0}=V\otimes 1=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{u_{1}},\dots,\partial_{u_{m+n}})

with

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{1,-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(u_{i}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\mid\text{all possible }i,j).

By condition (
154
), for each
j=1,\dots,k
, there exist elements

u_{\alpha}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-1}
of the form

u_{\alpha}\partial_{\xi_{j}}\text{~~for any $\alpha=1,\dots,m+n$ and
$j=1,\dots,k$,}

and
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}
irreducibly acts on
V\otimes 1
, see assumption (
155
).

Hence,

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-1}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{1,-1}
.

Thus, for any
\alpha,\beta=1,\dots,m+n
and
i,j=1,\dots,k
, we have

[\xi_{i}\partial_{u_{\beta}},u_{\alpha}\partial_{\xi_{j}}]=\delta_{\alpha\beta}\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{j}}-(-1)^{(p(u_{\alpha})+1)(p(u_{\beta})+1)}\delta_{ij}u_{\alpha}\partial_{u_{\beta}}\in\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}.

Consequently,

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}\supset({\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(\xi))\cap{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n+k).

Now, consider the regrading
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}

of
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
into the standard
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k)
.
Then,

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{-1,0}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,-1}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n+k;k)_{-1},\\
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{-1,1}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,0}\oplus\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-1}\supset{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n+k).\end{array}

Next, by the universality property
1^{\circ})
, see Subsection
5.1
, we
see that

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
, or
{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
, or

{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n))
, where
m>1
.

If
m=1
, then there are two more possibilities:

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)
and
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)):={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}E
for
E:=\sum x_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}
.

Case 2)
. If
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(1|0)
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(\xi)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\subset\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)
.

By Corollary
5.4.  Corollary
, if the component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
does not
contain non-zero elements that commute with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|k;k)
and, by the above arguments, either
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(1|k;k)
or
{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|k;k)
.

Suppose the component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
contains non-zero elements that commute with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}:={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)
.

In terms of the standard indeterminates
t,\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k},\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
for
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k)
, we have

\begin{gathered}{\mathfrak{k}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(\xi),\\
\Lambda(\xi)\otimes(t-\sum\xi_{i}\eta_{i})\simeq\Lambda(k)\subset{\mathfrak{k}}_{0},\\
\Lambda(\xi)\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\eta)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)\subset{\mathfrak{k}}_{0};\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}=t\otimes\Lambda(\xi)\bigoplus t\otimes\Lambda(\xi)\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\eta)\bigoplus\Lambda(\xi)\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\eta).\end{gathered}

The subspace
W_{1}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}_{1}
of elements that commute with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}

coincides with
\Lambda(\xi)\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\eta)
.

Consider
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
. By conditions (
154
),
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k})
, and
\eta_{i}
acts in
W_{1}
as
\partial_{\xi_{i}}
.

Since
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\cap W_{1}\neq 0
, we see that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\cap{\mathfrak{k}}_{1,-2}\neq 0
, where
{\mathfrak{k}}_{1,-2}=\Lambda^{2}(\eta)\otimes 1
. Hence,

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-2}\neq 0
. Set

(156)

{\mathfrak{h}}:=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{-1\leq i\leq k-1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{i},\text{~~where~~}{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}=\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,*}\cap\Lambda^{1}(\eta)\otimes\Lambda(\xi).

Clearly,
{\mathfrak{h}}\supset[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-2}]
.

Lemma

(Technical)
.

The component
{\mathfrak{h}}_{k-2}
, see eq. (
156
), is not contained in

{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|k)_{k-2}
. In particular,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{k-2}\neq 0
.

Proof.

Let
X=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{k}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. Then,

\begin{gathered}{}\left[X,\ \mathop{\sum}\limits_{i<j}\alpha_{ij}\eta_{i}\eta_{j}\right]=(-1)^{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i<j}\alpha_{ij}\left(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}\eta_{j}-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}\eta_{i}\right)\longmapsto\\
D:=(-1)^{k+1}\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i<j}\alpha_{ij}\left(\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{i}}$}}\partial_{\xi_{j}}-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial X}{\partial\xi_{j}}$}}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\right).\end{gathered}

But
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D=2(-1)^{k+1}\mathop{\sum}_{i<j}\ \alpha_{ij}\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}X}{\partial\xi_{i}\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\partial^{2}X}{\partial\xi_{i}\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}X}{\partial\xi_{i}\partial\xi_{j}}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\partial^{2}X}{\partial\xi_{i}\partial\xi_{j}}$}}
. So
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits D=0
if and only if
\alpha_{ij}=0
for all
i,j
. Since
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-2}\neq 0
, this proves the lemma.
∎

We have established that:

1)
{\mathfrak{h}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)
,

2)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)_{-1}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is an irreducible

{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-module,

3)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{k-2}
is not contained in
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|k)_{k-2}
.

Comparing these properties 1)–3) with the list of simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras, we conclude that
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|k)
for

k>3
. If
k=3
, then
{\mathfrak{h}}
can also be equal to

{\mathfrak{sl}}(1|3)\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|3)
.

Anyway,
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,*}\supset\Lambda^{1}(\eta)\otimes\Lambda^{1}(\xi)\simeq{\mathfrak{gl}}(k)
. Thus, by the irreducibility of the
exterior square of the tautological
{\mathfrak{gl}}(k)
-module,

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{1}\supset\Lambda^{2}(\eta)
.

The irreducibility of the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
means that

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,*}
should contain at least some elements of

\Lambda(\xi)\otimes(t-\sum\xi_{i}\eta_{i})
. Hence,
t\in\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})_{0,*}
.

Now we are able to consider the regrading
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
of
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
into
the standard
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k)
. By the above,

{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{\leq 0}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k)_{\leq 0}
. Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
is either

{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k)
or
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}\subset{\mathfrak{k}}(1|6)
, and therefore either

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}(1|2k;k)
or
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}(;3\xi)
, respectively.

Case 3)
. If
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=0|1
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(\xi))
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\subset\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)
, so
{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{m}}(k;k)
. The rank-1 operators
with even covector acting in
\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)
are of the following form for
n
even (resp., odd)

g_{i}=\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}\partial_{i},\text{ (resp., $h_{i}=\xi_{1}\dots\widehat{\xi_{i}}\dots\xi_{n}\partial_{i}$) for }\ i=1,\dots,n.

By Theorem
3.1.  Theorem
, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
contains at
least one of them. Assumption (
155
) implies that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

contains either all the
g_{i}
or all the
h_{i}
.

a) Let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
. Then, from
conditions (
154
) we deduce that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi_{1},\ldots\xi_{n})
. Since the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is irreducible, it follows that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)
.

b) For the same reason as in case a), if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(h_{1},\dots,h_{n})
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset{\mathfrak{svect}}(\xi)
, and the following
cases are possible:

\begin{array}[]{l}1)\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)\text{~~ realized as $\{v+\lambda\cdot\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits\ (v)\mid v\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)$ for $\lambda\neq 0\}$},\\
2)\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{svect}}(\xi),\\
3)\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\simeq\Lambda(\xi)\ltimes{\mathfrak{svect}}(\xi).\end{array}

In all these cases,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded by powers of

\xi
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0}\supset{\mathfrak{sl}}(0|k)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(k)
. Due to
Corollary
5.4.  Corollary
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}\neq 0
; hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}=S^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
.

Consider the regrading
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
into the standard

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{m}}(k)
. We get
{\mathfrak{g}}^{T}_{<0}={\mathfrak{m}}_{<0}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\supset{\mathfrak{spe}}(k)
. Then, by the universality property
3^{\circ})
, see
Subsection
5.1
,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either
{\mathfrak{m}}(k)
or
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(k)
, where

\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}\setminus\{0\}
.
∎

Corollary

(Solvable
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
)
.

For the simple vectorial Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
with W-filtration, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

is solvable only in the following cases:
17
17

17

Note that if
p=2
, there are examples of simple Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
with solvable
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
, see
[
BGL
,
BGLLS
]
.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(1|1),\quad{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|2;1),\quad{\mathfrak{vect}}(1|2;1)

and the respective
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is isomorphic to

{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|1),\quad{\mathfrak{sl}}(1|1)\otimes\Lambda(1)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|1),\quad{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|1)\otimes\Lambda(1)\ltimes{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|1).

6.
Step 5. Prolongations of almost simple Lie
superalgebras and their central extensions

In this step, we consider two essentially different cases: vectorial
and non-vectorial Lie superalgebras (Steps 5B and 5A, respectively).

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be an almost simple Lie superalgebra or a central
extension of an almost simple finite-dimensional
Lie superalgebra (they are listed in table (
264
)).
Let
T
be a faithful irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a
finite-dimensional superspace
V
. Our goal is to describe all the
collections
({\mathfrak{g}},T,V)
for which there exists an element
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}
such that

T(g)\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)
is a rank-1 operator with an even covector.

Irreducible representations of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras
and their “relatives” — central extensions of almost simple Lie
superalgebras connected to them — are completely described, see Subsection
12.5
.
From this description we easily deduce
the list of admissible collections
({\mathfrak{g}},T,V)
.

For non-vectorial Lie superalgebras, there is a complete description
of the typical (
i.e.
, generic) representations (see
[
S1
]
, where
the pioneering results of F. Berezin and general ones due to V. Kac
are presented with corrections and more lucidly; for a modern
treatment of the problem, see
[
SeVe
]
). But (bar a couple of
exceptions) the rank-1 operators occur, if ever, in atypical
representations whose description was only conjectural when this
paper was being written (for reviews covering various aspects of the
problem, see
[
L2
]
,
[
KW
]
,
[
Se5
]
).

Though we believed in the validity of the remarkable general
character formula due to Penkov and Serganova (at that time conjectural, now proved), our
proof in Step 5A did not appeal to the description of irreducible
representations. Inevitably then, the proof is rather lengthy, it occupies the rest of this Section.

Our method of description and of search for such collections
({\mathfrak{g}},T,V)
is based on pretty straightforward considerations which,
nevertheless, provide us with essential restrictions on the weight
diagram of
T
.

Proposition

(List of rank-1 operators: Non-vectorial Lie
superalgebras)
.

Let
T
be a faithful irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a
finite-dimensional superspace
V
. For the almost simple non-vectorial Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
or any
central extension of such, the representations
(T,V)
with a rank-
1
operator
T(g)
, where
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, with an even covector are
exactly those from Table (
157
), where the action of the
generator
z
of the center of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is normalized to
be equal to
-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
.

(157)

V={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
or
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}

{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)
and
{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)

{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
and

{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)

\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
or
\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*})

{\mathfrak{sl}}(n|m)
and

{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|m)

{\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m)
and
{\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)

{\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)

{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{osp}}^{a}(m|2n)

{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m))

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

{\mathfrak{spe}}^{a}(n)
and

{\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n)

{\mathfrak{sle}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{le}}(n)

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{spe}}^{a}(n))
and
{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{pe}}^{a}(n))

{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{sle}}(n))

and
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{le}}(n))

\mathop{\mathrm{spin}}\nolimits_{\lambda}
,
\lambda\neq 0

{\mathfrak{as}}

{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{le}}(4|4)

Proof

of Step 5A. We only have to test the simplicity of the prolongs
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
.
∎

Proposition

(On rank-1 operators)
.

Let
T
be a faithful irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a
finite-dimensional superspace
V
. If
T({\mathfrak{g}})
contains an odd operator of rank
1
, then either

(1)

T({\mathfrak{g}})
contains an even operator of rank
(1,0)
and an even operator of rank
(0,1)
, or

(2)

T({\mathfrak{g}})
contains an even operator of rank
(1,1)
.

Proof.

Let
\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
be such that

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T(\xi)=1
,
i.e.
,
T(\xi)v=\alpha(v)\partial_{\xi}
, where
\alpha\in V^{*}
. In other words,

T(\xi)=\alpha\otimes v_{\xi}
. Let
\eta\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
, where
v_{\xi}\in V
is a fixed non-zero vector. Then,

T([\eta,\xi])=T(\eta)T(\xi)+T(\xi)T(\eta).

Consider the two operators

(158)

\begin{array}[]{l}A_{\eta}(v)=T(\eta)T(\xi)v=\alpha(v)\cdot T(\eta)v_{\xi},\\
B_{\eta}(v)=T(\xi)T(\eta)v=\alpha(T(\eta v))\partial_{\xi}.\end{array}

Clearly, for each fixed
\eta
, both
A_{\eta}
and
B_{\eta}
are
of rank
\leq 1
; besides, they act in the spaces of opposite
parity.

Lemma

(Technical)
.

Let
T
be a faithful irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a
finite-dimensional superspace
V
. Let
W=\mathop{\cap}_{\eta\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}}\ \ker T(\eta)
and

U=T({\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}})V
. Then, the subspaces
U
and
W
are

{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant.

Proof.

Since
T
is irreducible, it follows that
U=V
and
W=0
. Now, the

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
-invariance is obvious. Let
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
,

\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
,
v\in V
and
u=\xi v\in U
. We claim that

gu\in U
. Indeed,

gu=g\xi v=\xi gv+[g,\xi]v\in T({\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}})V=V,

because
gv\in V
and
[g,\xi]\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
.

Let
w\in W
,
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
. Let us show that

gw\in W
. Indeed,

\eta(gw)=g(\eta w)+[\eta,g]w=0

because
\eta w=0
and
[\eta,g]\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
. Hence,
gw\in W
.
∎

Corollary

(On rank-1 operators)
.

There exist
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
such that

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A_{\eta_{1}}=1
and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits B_{\eta_{2}}=1
, see (
158
).

To complete the proof of Proposition
6.1.1.  Proposition
, note that if

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits B_{\eta_{1}}=1
(resp.,
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A_{\eta_{2}}=1
), then

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T([\eta_{1},\xi])=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits(A_{\eta_{1}}+B_{\eta_{1}})=(1,1)\ \ \ \text{~~(resp., $\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T([\eta_{2},\xi])=(1,1)$},

whereas if
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits B_{\eta_{1}}=\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits A_{\eta_{2}}=0
, then the operators

T([\eta_{1},\xi])
and
T([\eta_{2},\xi])
are of rank 1 and act
on the spaces of opposite parity.
∎

Corollary

(On infinite prolongation of depth 1)
.

Let
T
be a faithful irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
in a
finite-dimensional superspace
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. If
\dim({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}=\infty
, then there exists an element
g_{0}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
such
that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits(T(g_{0}))|_{({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{{\bar{0}}}}=1
and
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits(T(g_{0})|_{({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{{\bar{1}}}}\leq 1
.

Proposition

(On rank-1 operators in reductive algebras)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{G}}_{j}
for
j=1,\dots,k
be simple Lie algebras
(
not
super
algebras)
, let
{\mathfrak{r}}
be a solvable Lie algebra and let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{r}}\oplus(\bigoplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{j})
be their direct sum. Let
T
be a
(perhaps,
reducible)
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in a space
V
with a rank-
1

operator. Then, either

1)
there exists a
1
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant subspace
of
V
, or

2)
up to renumbering,
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
or
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
and

V=W\oplus U
, where
U=\ker T({\mathfrak{G}}_{1})
and
W
is isomorphic, as a

{\mathfrak{g}}
-module, to either
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}\otimes\lambda
or

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\chi_{0}\otimes\lambda
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
is the tautological
representation of
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
and
\chi_{0}
is the zero
1
-dimensional
representation of
{\mathfrak{G}}_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{k}
, and
\lambda
is
a character of
{\mathfrak{r}}
.

Proof.

For any
semi-simple Lie algebra
{{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}:={\mathfrak{G}}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{k}}
, any of its finite-dimensional representations is
completely reducible, and hence the sum of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
with
{\mathfrak{r}}
is
direct; therefore,
V
can be represented in the form

V=\mathop{\oplus}_{i}\ V_{i}
, where
T|_{V_{i}}=\tau_{1}^{i}\otimes\dots\otimes\tau_{k}^{i}\otimes\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}
and
\tau_{j}^{i}
is an
irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{G}}_{j}
and
\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i}
is an
irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{r}}
.

Since
T
contains a rank-1 operator, so does the restriction

T|_{V_{i_{0}}}
for some
i_{0}
. Therefore, at most one of
the modules given by
\tau_{1}^{i_{0}}
, …,

\tau_{k}^{i_{0}}
,
\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i_{0}}
can be of dimension
>1
.

If
\dim\tau_{1}^{i_{0}}=\dots=\dim\tau_{k}^{i_{0}}=\dim\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i_{0}}=1
, then
\dim V_{i_{0}}=1
and possibility 1) of Proposition

6.2.  Proposition
is realized.

If
\dim\tau_{1}^{i_{0}}=\dots=\dim\tau_{k}^{i_{0}}=1
, but
\dim\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i_{0}}>1
, then, since
{\mathfrak{r}}
is solvable,
V_{i_{0}}
contains a
1
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{r}}
-submodule

V_{0}
. Since
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}V_{i_{0}}=0
, it follows that
V_{0}
is
a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-submodule,
i.e.
, once again the same possibility 1) of
Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
is realized.

Finally, let
\dim\tau_{j_{0}}^{i_{0}}>1
for some
j_{0}
. Let us show
that in this case possibility 2) or Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
is realized.
Indeed, up to a renumbering of the subalgebras
{\mathfrak{G}}_{j}
, we can assume
that
j_{0}=1
. Then,

\dim\tau_{2}^{i_{0}}=\dots=\dim\tau_{k}^{i_{0}}=\dim\tau_{\mathfrak{r}}^{i_{0}}=1

and the image

T({\mathfrak{g}})|_{V_{i_{0}}}
is isomorphic to either
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
or to its trivial
central extension
{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{G}}_{1})
. Moreover,

T({\mathfrak{G}}_{2}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{k})|_{V_{i_{0}}}=0
and

T({\mathfrak{r}})|_{V_{i_{0}}}=\lambda
is a character.

Since there are only three series of simple infinite-dimensional Lie
algebras with W-filtration of depth one, it follows that
a
faithful irreducible
representation
\tau
of the Lie
algebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains a rank-1 operator if and only if for
a character
\lambda
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
we have one of the following:

\begin{array}[]{rl}{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)&\text{and }\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\text{ or }\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*},\\
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{gl}}(n)&\text{and }\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\lambda\text{ or }\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\lambda,\\
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)&\text{and }\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*},\\
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)&\text{and }\tau=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\lambda.\end{array}

In our case of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, the role of
{\mathfrak{h}}
is played by the image

T({\mathfrak{G}}_{1})|_{V_{i_{0}}}
and we see that
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
is isomorphic to
either
{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
or
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
, while
T({\mathfrak{G}}_{1})|_{V_{i_{0}}}
is
either
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
or
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
.

Thus, we take
V_{i_{0}}
for the role of
W
. To complete the proof,
it suffices to observe that the rank of any operator
T(g)
is
calculated by the formula

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T({\mathfrak{g}})=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq k}\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T({\mathfrak{g}})|_{V_{i}},

and therefore
T({\mathfrak{g}})|_{V_{i}}=0
for
i\neq i_{0}
.
∎

6.3.
The cases of Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
: two
regular and two exceptional subcases

Now, let us return to the situation of interest to
us:
{\mathfrak{g}}
is an almost simple Lie superalgebra or a central
extension thereof, and
T
its a faithful representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}
in
a finite-dimensional superspace
V
such that among the operators

T(g)
with
g\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, there is a rank-1 operator with an even
covector.

Consider the restriction
T|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}}
. Thanks to Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
, the
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
in the subspace
V_{{\bar{0}}}
must have
a rank-1 operator, whereas the representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
in the
whole space
V
has either a rank
(1,0)
operator or a rank
(1,1)

operator. Moreover, from Table (
264
) we see that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}

is always of the form
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{k}\oplus{\mathfrak{r}}
, where
the
{\mathfrak{G}}_{i}
are simple and
{\mathfrak{r}}
is a solvable Lie algebra (
{\mathfrak{r}}

can be zero and is almost always commutative). Hence, Proposition

6.2.  Proposition
is applicable to the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-action in the spaces

V_{{\bar{0}}}
and
V_{{\bar{1}}}
.

Let, as in Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{G}}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathfrak{G}}_{k}
. Consider, separately, the
two cases corresponding to the possibilities of Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{1) among the operators $T({\mathfrak{g}}_{ss})$, there are no operators
of rank $(1,0)$ or $(1,1)$;}\\
\text{2) among the operators $T({\mathfrak{g}}_{ss})$, there is an operator
of rank $(1,0)$ or $(1,1)$.}\\
\end{array}

6.3.1.
Case 1) of Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
.

As is clear from the above discussion of the possible cases of this Proposition, if we
are in the setting of case 1), then the operator of rank

(1,0)
or
(1,1)
is in this case of the form
T(h)
, where

h\in{\mathfrak{r}}
. Moreover, since
T
is irreducible, then

h
cannot belong to the center of the whole algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

This possibility 1) is realized in the following regular subcases (as follows from
the classification of finite-dimensional simple Lie superalgebras and
their central extensions, see Table
(
264
)):

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)\text{ for }m\neq n,\;{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n),\;{\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2n),\;{\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2n))

and also in the
exceptional subcases
, to be considered
separately:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{a)~}\;{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\text{
and }1\leq\dim{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\leq 2,\\
\text{b) the same as in a), but with
${\mathfrak{c}}({\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2))$ instead of ${\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)$.}\\
\end{array}

The regular subcases

In these subcases,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
, and therefore

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
. Then, under a proper normalization, the
operator
h
acts as the grading operator:

[h,g]=\pm g~~\text{~~ for any }g\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}.

Let

W_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathbb{C}}w_{0}
be a 1-dimensional
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-invariant
subspace of
V_{{\bar{0}}}
and
h\cdot w_{0}=\lambda w_{0}
. Then,

h(gw_{0})=g(hw_{0})+[h,g]w_{0}=\lambda(gw_{0})\pm(gw_{0})=(\lambda\pm 1)(g\cdot w_{0}),

i.e.
, on each of the subspaces
W_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W

and
W_{1}={\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W
the operator
h
acts as multiplication by
a scalar.

Consider the action of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
on
W_{\pm 1}
. If

g_{0}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
and
\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}
, then

g_{0}(\xi w_{0})=\xi(g_{0}w_{0})+[g_{0},\xi]\cdot w_{0}=[g_{0},\xi]w_{0}.

Hence, the subspaces
W_{\pm 1}
are
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-invariant
and the representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
in
W_{\pm 1}
is isomorphic to
the quotient representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}
.

Since in all the cases considered the
representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}
is
irreducible, we see that either
W_{\pm 1}=0
or
\dim W_{\pm 1}=\dim{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}>1
.

To ensure that
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T(h)=1
or
(1,1)
, we should have
\lambda=\pm 1
; moreover, if
\lambda+k\neq 0
, then
W_{k}=0
for
k=\pm 1
.
Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{-\lambda}
and
h|_{W_{-\lambda}}=0
.
Then,
h|_{{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}\cdot W_{-\lambda}}=\pm\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\pm 1}W_{-\lambda}\subset V_{{\bar{0}}}
.

But since
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits T(h)|_{V_{{\bar{0}}}}=1
, we get

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\lambda}W_{-\lambda}=W_{{\bar{0}}},\quad{\mathfrak{g}}_{-\lambda}W_{-\lambda}=0~~\text{ and }~~{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}.

Therefore,
W_{\lambda}\oplus W_{{\bar{0}}}
is invariant under the whole
{\mathfrak{g}}
; hence,
V=W_{\lambda}\oplus W_{{\bar{0}}}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(1,n)
, where
n=\dim{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}=\dim{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}
.

Thus,

{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|n)\text{ and }\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}=2n=\dim{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|n)_{{\bar{1}}}.

Therefore, either
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(1|n)
or
{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|n)
, and either
T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits

or
T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
(recall how the action of the center is normalized, see
Proposition
6.1.  Proposition
).

The exceptional subcases

These cases are
related to the simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
whose Lie
superalgebra of outer derivations is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
.

Subcase a):
{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
and

1\leq\dim{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\leq 2
.

Subcase b):
{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\subset{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{der}}~{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
and

1\leq\dim{\mathfrak{g}}/{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\leq 2
.
Let
z
be the central element of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Let
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}\subset{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
be a subalgebra of the Lie algebra of
outer derivations of
{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
such that
1\leq\dim{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}\leq 2
.
Let

{\mathfrak{r}}=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{r}}_{0},&\text{ in subcase a)},\\
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z,&\text{ in subcase b).}\end{cases}

Denote by
R_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}|_{{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}}
the restriction of the
tautological representation of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
to
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
. Then,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{o}}(4)
, where the indices label copies of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and — in what follows shortly — their tautological modules. Let
\chi_{0}
be the
trivial character of
{\mathbb{C}}z
; then, as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-module,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
is isomorphic to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{1}\boxtimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{2}\boxtimes R
, where

R=\begin{cases}R_{0},&\text{ in subcase a)},\\
R_{0}\otimes\chi_{0},&\text{ in subcase b).}\end{cases}

Set
X=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}$}\in{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and
Y=\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}$}\in{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
. Clearly, up to the choice of a basis in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
, there are
three essentially distinct possibilities for
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
:

1)
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(X,Y)
;

2)
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}={\mathbb{C}}X
;

3)
{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}={\mathbb{C}}Y
.

Recall that we are looking for an irreducible representation
T
of

{\mathfrak{g}}
in a superspace
V
such that
T({\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}})
contains an
operator of rank
(1,0)
or
(1,1)
whereas

T({\mathfrak{g}}_{ss})|_{V_{{\bar{0}}}}
has no such operators.

Then, the operator of rank
(1,0)
or
(1,1)
is
T(r)
, where

r\in{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}
. Without loss of generality we can assume that either

r=X
or
r=Y
.

If
r=X
, then the arguments used in the regular case are applicable and we
conclude that
{\mathfrak{g}}
has no such representations.

Let
r=Y
. Let, as in the general case,
W_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathbb{C}}w_{0}\subset V_{{\bar{0}}}
be a
1
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-invariant subspace. Then,

T({\mathfrak{g}}_{ss})w_{0}=0
, and the representation
T
determines
a character
\chi_{\lambda,\alpha}
of
{\mathfrak{r}}
via the formulas

\begin{array}[]{l}T(z)w_{0}=\chi_{\lambda,\alpha}(z)w_{0}=\lambda w_{0}\text{ for
}\lambda\neq 0,\\
T(Y)w_{0}=\chi_{\lambda,\alpha}(Y)w_{0}=\alpha w_{0}.\end{array}

(Clearly, if
\dim{\mathfrak{r}}_{0}=2
, then
\alpha=0
.)

Consider the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-submodule
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}\subset V_{{\bar{1}}}
.
It is isomorphic to a quotient of the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-module

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{1}\boxtimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{2}\boxtimes(R\otimes\chi_{\lambda,\alpha}).

Therefore, the following three cases are possible:

1)
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
. Then,
W_{{\bar{0}}}
is a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant. Hence,
V=W_{{\bar{0}}}
and
T
is not faithful.

2)
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{0}
. Then,

\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits~T(Y)|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{0}}=\begin{cases}4,&\text{if }\alpha=0\\
8,&\text{if }\alpha\neq 0.\end{cases}

Both these
possibilities contradict our assumption on the rank.

3)
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq_{1}\boxtimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{2}\boxtimes\chi_{\lambda,\alpha}
. If
\alpha\neq 0
, then
\mathop{\mathrm{rk}}\nolimits~T(Y)|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}}=4
.
Hence,
\alpha=0
, in other words,
T(Y)|_{W_{0}}=T(Y)|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}}=0
. Set

(159)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}:=\mathop{\text{\rm Im}}\nolimits~\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{Y}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}.

Then,

T(Y)W_{{\bar{0}}}=T(Y){\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}-{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}T(Y)W_{{\bar{0}}}=0.

Thus,
w_{0}
is the highest weight vector in
T
.

In subcase a) this means that
T
is
1
-dimensional, and therefore
not faithful.

In subcase b), let
c
be the cocycle
that determines the central extension of
{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
to
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2|2)
. As
is clear from Table (
264
),

(160)

c|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}}=0.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-Y}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
be a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-invariant subspace
complementary to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-Y}W_{{\bar{0}}}
by definition (
159
). Take some non-zero
\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}
and
\eta\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-Y}
. Then,

\xi\eta w_{0}-\eta\xi w_{0}=[\xi,\eta]w_{0}=c(\xi,\eta)\cdot\lambda w_{0}.

By condition (
160
), both
Y
and all the elements
\xi\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{Y}

annihilate the space
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
. Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}

generates a
{\mathfrak{g}}
-invariant subspace in
V
which does not contain

W_{{\bar{0}}}
. This contradicts irreducibility of
T
.

Conclusion: in none of the exceptional subcases
{\mathfrak{g}}
has irreducible
representations we are looking for.

6.3.2.
Case 2) of Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
.

Due to Proposition
6.2.  Proposition
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{G}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{comp}
, where

{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
or
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
and the complementary Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}_{comp}
is either semi-simple or zero. So
V=W\oplus U
for
(decomposable)
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
–module
W
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{comp}
-module
U
,
where
T({\mathfrak{G}}_{1})|_{U}=0
, the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module
W_{{\bar{0}}}
is
isomorphic to either
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}
or
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\chi_{0}
and
the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module
W_{{\bar{1}}}
is either zero or isomorphic
to either
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}
or
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\chi_{0}
.

Consider the weight diagrams of
V_{{\bar{0}}}
and
V_{{\bar{1}}}
with respect
to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
. Denote by
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
the standard
vectors that span the space dual to the Cartan subalgebra of

{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
, see
[
OV
]
, and by
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{N}
the
similar vectors for
{\mathfrak{g}}_{comp}
. (Clearly, if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
is simple,
and therefore
{\mathfrak{g}}_{comp}=0
, these complementary vectors
\delta_{i}

are absent.) Then, the collection of weights (with respect to

{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
) in
W_{{\bar{0}}}
and
W_{{\bar{1}}}
is as follows (with
multiplicity one):

{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{sl}}(n):&\text{either $\varepsilon_{1}$, \ldots,
$\varepsilon_{n}$ or $-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}$;}\\
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n):&\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}\;\text{ and $-\varepsilon_{1}$, \ldots,
$-\varepsilon_{n}$.}\end{cases}

In the spaces
U_{{\bar{0}}}
and
U_{{\bar{1}}}
, all the weights are of the
form
\varphi(\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{N})
,
i.e.
, depend only on the

\delta
’s. The weight diagram of such a form will be referred to as
a
desirable
one. Let
\alpha
be an odd root of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and

\xi_{\alpha}
the corresponding root vector; let
\lambda
be
a weight of
V
and
v_{\lambda}
a corresponding weight vector.
Then,
\xi_{\alpha}v_{\lambda}
is a weight vector of weight

\lambda+\alpha
.

6.3.3.
The scheme of our further considerations. Case-by-case study.

1
) We start with a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-invariant subspace
W_{{\bar{0}}}
whose
weights we know.

2
) We apply
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
to this subspace and get a subspace of

V_{{\bar{1}}}
. By comparing the desirable weight diagram of
V_{{\bar{1}}}

with weights
\lambda^{\prime}
of the form
\lambda+\alpha
we get an
information about possible weight diagrams of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
.

3
) By applying item
2
) to a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
-invariant subspace of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
, we deduce that:

a)
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
, and therefore

V=W_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
;

b) a description of the weight diagram of
V
.

4
) Since the dimension of
V
is very low and its homogeneous
components are irreducible with respect to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
, a direct
verification and dimension considerations quickly bring about the result.
If these considerations are not conclusive, we compare the
superspace
V
obtained, the highest weights with
respect to
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
of its homogeneous components we know, with the irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules
with the same highest weights. Since all such modules are quotients
of the induced modules, it suffices to investigate only what Rudakov called

singular vectors
(see the review
[
GLS3
]
) of two induced modules.

{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)

In this case,

{\mathfrak{g}}
is either
{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)
or
{\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
. The weights

\lambda
of
W_{{\bar{0}}}
are
\pm\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\pm\varepsilon_{n}
.

m>2
. For
{\mathfrak{osp}}(2m|2n)
, the odd roots are of the form

\alpha=\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\delta_{j}
. For

{\mathfrak{osp}}(2m+1|2n)
, to the roots of this form we must add the
roots
\alpha=\pm\varepsilon_{i}
.

Thus, the only sums
\lambda+\alpha
that are desirable weights are

\lambda^{\prime}=\begin{cases}\pm\delta_{j},&\text{for
${\mathfrak{osp}}(2m|2n)$},\\
\pm\delta_{j}\text{ and $0$},&\text{for ${\mathfrak{osp}}(2m+1|2n)$. }\end{cases}

By considering the sums
\lambda^{\prime\prime}
of the form
\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha
,
we see that, among them, the only desirable weights are those of the
form
\pm\varepsilon_{i}
. Since they are of multiplicity
1
in
V_{{\bar{0}}}
, it follows that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
; hence,

V=W_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
.

Consider the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
more closely.
Clearly, it is isomorphic to the quotient of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}
. As
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{o}}(m)}
and

W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\otimes\chi_{0}
. Thus,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}
is the quotient of

\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\right)\bigotimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{o}}(m)}

by the zero
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
-action.

Now, observe that the tensor square of the tautological

{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
-module is reducible:

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}=S^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)})\oplus\Lambda^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}).

Moreover,
S^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)})\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}
(hence,
irreducible) and
\Lambda^{2}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)})
is the direct sum of
the trivial
1
-dimensional module
\mathbbmss{1}
and the
irreducible submodule complementary to it. Therefore, the subspace
of invariants in the tensor square

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}
is
1
-dimensional. Therefore,
the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}
can be either
trivial or isomorphic to
\chi_{0}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{o}}(m)}
.

Thus,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2n|1)
or
(2n|m)
.
Thanks to the faithfulness of the
{\mathfrak{o}}(m)
-action, dimension considerations show that only
the second possibility can be realized; the representation
T
is,
actually,
\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
.

m=2
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
. The weights
\lambda

of
W_{{\bar{0}}}
are
\pm\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\pm\varepsilon_{n}
and the odd roots
are also
\pm\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\pm\varepsilon_{n}
. Each root is of
multiplicity 2, and the center of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}
separates the odd roots
into the negative and positive ones:
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
; the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-action in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
, the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-action on

{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
is
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
.

The desirable weight
\lambda^{\prime}
is of the form
\alpha+\lambda
only for

\lambda^{\prime}=0
. The subspace
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}
is the quotient of

{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{{\bar{0}}}

is the quotient of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. As
already noted, each of the modules
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
contains only a
1
-dimensional subspace of
invariants. Thus,

\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0,1\text{ or }2.

The desirable weights of the form
\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha
are equal to

\pm\varepsilon_{i}
. Hence,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}\text{~~and~~}V=W_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}.

As above, dimension considerations imply that

T({\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}})={\mathfrak{gl}}(2n|1)_{-}
in the standard format. Hence,

\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2n|2)\text{~~and~~}T=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits).

m=1
. Then,
