{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
,
as a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module. The same arguments as above show that
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=1
and
V=W_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
. Thus,

T=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
.

{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)

This case splits into the following subcases A) –
F).

A)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{spe}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{spe}}(n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
,
{\mathfrak{as}}
,

{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
. We will assume that
n>3
: as

{\mathfrak{spe}}(3)\simeq{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|3)
, this case and all it relatives are
considered in the study of vectorial Lie superalgebras.

For these Lie superalgebras,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
and the weights of

W_{{\bar{0}}}
are either
\lambda=\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
or

\lambda=-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}
. The odd roots are

\alpha=\pm(\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j})
for
i\neq j
, and
2\varepsilon_{i}
. Observe
that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}
naturally splits into the sum of a positive and a negative
parts:
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
.

The roots in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}
are
\alpha=-\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}
while those in

{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
are
\alpha=\varepsilon_{i}+\varepsilon_{j},2\varepsilon_{i}
.

The weights
\lambda^{\prime}
of the form
\lambda+\alpha
that occur in
the desirable diagram are as follows.

If
\lambda=\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
, then
\lambda^{\prime}=-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
.

If
\lambda=-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}
, then
\lambda^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
.

Recall that the multiplicity of the roots of the form
\pm\varepsilon_{i}
in

V_{{\bar{1}}}
is necessarily equal to 1. Therefore,
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=n
.

Similarly, the desirable weights
\lambda^{\prime\prime}
of the form

\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha
are as follows.

If
\lambda^{\prime}=\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
, then
\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha=-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
.

If
\lambda^{\prime}=-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}
, then
\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha=\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{2}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
.

Thus,
V=W_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(n|n)
.
Hence, if
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{spe}}(n)
or
{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)
, then either
T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
or

T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
. Both these representations contain (odd) operators of
rank 1, but, for
T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
, the covector is odd.

For the Lie superalgebras
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{spe}}(n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
and

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{pe}}(n)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
, we see that
T=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi
, where

\chi
is an arbitrary character of the center, and, for
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{as}}
,
there is the one-parameter family
T_{\lambda}
of
(4|4)
-dimensional
representations that differ from each other by the value on the
center.

B)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{psq}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{sq}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{pq}}(n)
,
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
. In
these cases,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
.

The odd roots are
\alpha=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}
. Let, as above,
\lambda

denote a weight of
W_{{\bar{0}}}
, let
\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda+\alpha
run over
the weights of desirable form from
V_{{\bar{1}}}
, let

\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha
be the weights of desirable form in

V_{{\bar{0}}}
. Then,

(161)

\lambda

\lambda^{\prime}

\lambda^{\prime\prime}

\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}

\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}

\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}

-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}

-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}

-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}

Thus,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
and
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}=n
.
Hence,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(n|n)
. But, for
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{psq}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{pq}}(n)
, there
are no such representations at all (as follows from Fact

2.5
), while for
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sq}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{q}}(n)
this is the
tautological representation which has no rank-1 operators.

C)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n|m)
,
{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|m)
,
{\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)
.
Observe
that, for
n=m=1
, the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{gl}}(1|1)
is solvable;
hence, all its irreducible representations are induced
[
S0
]
; the
case considered in Step 4, see Section
5
. Due to the isomorphism
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2|1)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2)
, we can and will consider this case in Step 5B, see Subsection
6.5
.

m>1
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
, where the roots of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}
are
of the form
\alpha_{-1}=-\varepsilon_{i}+\delta_{j}
and those of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
are of the form

\alpha_{1}=\varepsilon_{i}-\delta_{j}
.

First, suppose
n>2
. Denote by
\lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1}
the
desirable weights of the form
\lambda+\alpha_{\pm 1}
and by

\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{\pm 1}
the desirable weights of the form
\lambda^{\prime}_{\pm 1}+\alpha_{\pm 1}
. We get:

(162)

\lambda

\lambda^{\prime}_{-1}

\lambda^{\prime}_{1}

\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{-1}

\lambda^{\prime\prime}_{1}

\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}

\emptyset

\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}

-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}

\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}

-\varepsilon_{1},\dots,-\varepsilon_{n}

\emptyset

Thus, we see that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=W_{{\bar{0}}}
. Let us consider
the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
in more detail. First, let

W_{{\bar{0}}}
, as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module, be
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}
(the weights
are
\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{n}
). Then, as is clear from table (
162
),

{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}
; hence,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}W_{{\bar{0}}}
is isomorphic to a quotient of the tensor
product

{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\right)\bigotimes\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}\right).

But the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-module
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
is the sum of the
adjoint module and the trivial
1
-dimensional module. Since the

{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-action on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}
is trivial, we deduce
that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\otimes W_{{\bar{0}}}=(0|m)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(n|m)
.

Therefore,

T=\begin{cases}\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits,&\text{ for }{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n|m)\text{ and }{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n),\\
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi,\text{ where $\chi$ is an
arbitrary character,}&\text{ for }{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{gl}}(n|m).\end{cases}

For
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{psl}}(n|n)
, there are no such representations, as follows
from dimension considerations and Fact
2.5
.

The case where
W_{{\bar{0}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\chi_{0}
is
considered in the same manner. We obtain:

T=\begin{cases}\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*},&\text{ for }{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(n|m)\text{ and }{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n),\\
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}\otimes\chi,\text{ where $\chi$ is an arbitrary
character,}&\text{ for }{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{gl}}(n|m).\end{cases}

n=2
. In this case, by studying only the weight diagram
we cannot conclude that either
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
or

{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
. We will consider this case in
Lemma
6.3.4.  Lemma
.

m=1
and
n>2
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
, where the roots of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}

are
\alpha_{-1}=-\varepsilon_{i}
and the roots of
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}
are

\alpha_{1}=\varepsilon_{i}
. The list of desirable weights in this case is
obtained from table (
162
) by replacing
\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}

with
0
. The remaining arguments are the same as in the above
subcase.

Lemma

(Technical, on
{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-modules)
.

Let the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{0}}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}
be such that

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{0}}\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}\simeq(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)})^{*}
, as

{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-module. Let
V
be an
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module and
W\subset V
its

{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-invariant subspace isomorphic to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)}
. Let,
moreover,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{\bar{1}}W
be a
1
-dimensional trivial
{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-module.
Then,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W=0
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}
and
W
can be considered
as dual linear spaces. Let
w_{1},\dots,w_{n}
be a basis of
W
and

h_{1},\dots,h_{n}
the dual basis of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}
. As already
noted, the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)
-module
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}W
is the quotient of the
tensor product
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W
by the adjoint submodule.
The adjoint submodule is singled out as the kernel of the natural
bilinear form
B(h\otimes w)=h(w)
on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\otimes W
. Then, the
generator
u
of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}W
can be expressed as
u=h_{1}w_{1}=\dots=h_{n}w_{n}
. For
i\neq j
, we get

h_{i}u=h_{i}h_{j}w_{j}=-(-1)^{p(h_{i})p(h_{j})}h_{j}h_{i}w_{j}=0.\hskip 85.35826pt\qed

Corollary

(Particular cases of Lemma
6.3.4.  Lemma
)
.

1)
The statement of Lemma
6.3.4.  Lemma
holds also if
{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and

W=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
.

2)
For
n=2
, the modules
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}
are isomorphic,
so Lemma
6.3.4.  Lemma
holds also if
{{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\simeq W\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits}

and
{{\mathfrak{h}}_{{\bar{1}}}\simeq W\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*}}
.

6.4.
Continuation of the story

Thus, let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2|m)
,

{\mathfrak{gl}}(2|m)
for
m>2
or let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be any almost simple Lie superalgebra related
to
{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)
, except for
{\mathfrak{der}}~~{\mathfrak{psl}}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}_{0}(4|2)
, see eqs. (
265
). Then,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)
, and it remains to consider the case
where
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
,
i.e.
, the subspace
W_{\bar{0}}\subset V
is
isomorphic, as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}
-module, to
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\chi_{0}
.

Observe that the submodules
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{{\bar{0}}}

cannot vanish simultaneously. Let, for example,

{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}\neq 0
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}}=\chi_{0}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)}
and, thanks to Lemma

6.3.4.  Lemma
,
({\mathfrak{g}}^{-1})^{2}W_{{\bar{0}}}=0
. But then

{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}({\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}})\neq 0
due to irreducibility of the

{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
V
and our usual arguments based on the weight
diagram show that
{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}({\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}})=W_{{\bar{0}}}
. Moreover,

W_{{\bar{0}}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes\chi_{0}
arises as the quotient
module of the tensor product

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}^{1}\otimes({\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{{\bar{0}}})\simeq\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)}^{*}\right)\bigotimes\left(\chi_{0}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)}\right)=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\bigotimes\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)}^{*}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m)}\right).\end{array}

Applying Lemma
6.3.4.  Lemma
once again we deduce that

({\mathfrak{g}}^{1})^{2}({\mathfrak{g}}^{-1}W_{\bar{0}})={\mathfrak{g}}^{1}W_{\bar{0}}=0
,
i.e.
,
{\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2|m)}
and
T({\mathfrak{g}})={\mathfrak{sl}}(2|m)
or
{\mathfrak{gl}}(2|m)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{osp}}(4|2n)
for
n>1
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{o}}(4)\oplus{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n),\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}\simeq\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\right)\bigotimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}.\end{array}

Let
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
. Our routine arguments yield

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}\simeq\left(\chi_{0}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2))\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\right)\bigotimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)}\text{ and }{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}^{2}W_{\bar{0}}=W_{\bar{0}}.

This implies that
V=W_{\bar{0}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}
and
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2|4n)
.

Let us show that there are no such
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules. To this end, in the
space dual to the Cartan subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{o}}(4)
, select basis
elements
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}
; for the roots of
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}
take
\pm 2\varepsilon_{1}
and let the roots of the complementary to
{\mathfrak{G}}_{1}

subalgebra in
{\mathfrak{o}}(4)
be
\pm 2\varepsilon_{2}
; let

\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}
be the standard basis of the dual space to
the Cartan subalgebra of
{\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)
. Then, the weight basis of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}
consists of the vectors
v_{\lambda}
of weight
\lambda=\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}
, whereas the weight basis of
W_{\bar{0}}

consists of
v_{\pm\varepsilon_{1}}
. Let
\xi_{\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}
be an odd root vector of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then,

v_{\pm\varepsilon_{1}}=\xi_{\pm\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}v_{\mp\varepsilon_{2}\mp\delta_{i}}\text{ and }\xi_{\alpha}v_{\lambda}=0\text{ if
}\alpha+\lambda\neq\pm\varepsilon_{1}.

For
i\neq k
, we get

\begin{array}[]{l}\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}v_{\varepsilon_{1}}=0,\\
\xi_{-\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}v_{\varepsilon_{1}}=\xi_{-\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}(\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{k}}v_{\mp\varepsilon_{2}\mp\delta_{k}})=-\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{k}}\cdot\xi_{-\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}v_{\mp\varepsilon_{2}\mp\delta_{k}}=0.\end{array}

The penultimate equality holds because
[\xi_{\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{k}},\ \xi_{-\varepsilon_{1}\pm\varepsilon_{2}\pm\delta_{i}}]=0
since
\pm 2\varepsilon_{2}\pm(\delta_{k}+\delta_{i})
is not
a root of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Thus,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}v_{\varepsilon_{1}}=0
. Similarly,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}v_{-\varepsilon_{1}}=0
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}=0
and
V=W_{0}
. This is a contradiction.

D)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{ab}}(3)
. Then,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{o}}(7)
. For this case, we modify our
notation somewhat. Let the roots of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)

be
\pm 2\varepsilon
.
Then, the weights of
W_{\bar{0}}
are
\lambda=\pm\varepsilon
; the odd roots are

\alpha=\pm\varepsilon+\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}(\pm\delta_{1}\pm\delta_{2}\pm\delta_{3})
and, as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-module,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{spin}}\nolimits_{7}
.

The desirable weights are

\lambda^{\prime}=\lambda+\alpha=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}(\pm\delta_{1}\pm\delta_{2}\pm\delta_{3})\text{ and }\lambda^{\prime\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}+\alpha=\pm\varepsilon.

Therefore,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}^{2}W_{\bar{0}}=W_{\bar{0}}
and
V=W_{\bar{0}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}
. Note that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}
is the quotient of the

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-module

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}\otimes W_{\bar{0}}\simeq\left(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\right)\bigotimes\left(\mathop{\mathrm{spin}}\nolimits_{7}\otimes\chi_{0}\right).

The dimension of the trivial
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
-submodule in the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
-module

\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
is equal to 1 and, since the
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
-action in

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}
should be trivial, we deduce that
\dim{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}W_{\bar{0}}=1\cdot\dim(\mathop{\mathrm{spin}}\nolimits_{7})=8
,
i.e.
,
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2|8)
, but
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{ab}}(3)
has no such irreducible
representations, see Fact

2.5
.

E)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{ag}}(2)
. Then,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}(2)
and, as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
-module,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{1}}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)}\otimes R(\pi_{1})
, where

R(\pi_{1})
is the irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{g}}(2)
with
highest weight
\pi_{1}
, see
[
OV
]
. Recall that
\dim R(\pi_{1})=7
. Arguments similar to those in the above case show
that the desirable representation should be of superdimension

(2|7)
; but
{\mathfrak{ag}}(2)
has no such irreducible representations, see
Fact
2.5
.

F)
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{osp}}_{a}(4|2)
. Then,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{ss}={\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and arguments similar to those
used in the above two subcases yield that
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits V=(2|4)
; there
are no such irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules for
\alpha\neq 1
, except for
the equivalent to
a=1
values
a=-\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{1}{2}$}}
,
-2
, see formulas (
43
).

6.5.
Step 5B. Vectorial Lie superalgebras. The four cases

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
be a finite-dimensional Lie superalgebra.
Let
z
be either its outer derivation or a generator of the trivial center. Set

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}\cdot z
(if
z
is central, this sum is
direct).

Lemma

(Cartan prolongs with the same negative part)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
be an irreducible finite-dimensional

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module. Consider the two Cartan prolongs:

{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i\geq-1}{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}\text{ and }{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{i\geq-1}{\mathfrak{g}}_{i}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}.

Either
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
, and then

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
, or
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}^{*}
, direct sum of
vector spaces.

Proof.

For any
F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
and
v\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, the decomposition

[F,v]=\alpha_{F}(v)z+h,

where
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
, determines a linear form
\alpha_{F}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}^{*}
. By the definition of Cartan prolongation,

\alpha_{F}=0
if and only if
F\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. Hence, as
a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module, the space
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}/{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
is a quotient of the

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}^{*}
which is irreducible together with

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

1)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
, where
n\geq 3
, and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{cvect}}(0|n):={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot z

(Recall, see Table
(
264
), that
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
has no outer derivations or
nontrivial central extensions.) The rank-1 operator with even
covector exists only for the following
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-modules
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
:

1a)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1)

\Longrightarrow

{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{le}}(n;n)
;

1b)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)

\Longrightarrow

{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;n)
;

1c)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n))

\Longrightarrow

{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n;n)
;

1d)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(n))

\Longrightarrow

{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n)
for
\lambda\neq 0,1
.

The element
z
acts in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
as a non-zero scalar. We normalize

z
so that
\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{z}|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}}=-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
and use for such

{\mathfrak{cvect}}(0|n)
-modules the same notation as for

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
-modules.

Proposition

(Prolongations with
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{cvect}}(0|n)
)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{cvect}}(0|n)
, where
n\geq 3
, let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
be
a
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module of type
1a) – 1d)
below. Let
{\mathfrak{g}}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
be the Cartan prolongation. Then, in the respective cases,
we have:

1a)
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(n)/{\mathbb{C}}\cdot 1)
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{le}}(4|3),&\text{for $n=3$,}\\
{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{le}}(n;n)),&\text{for $n>3$;}\end{cases}

1b)
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}=\begin{cases}{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}(;3\eta),&\text{for
$n=3$,}\\
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;n)),&\text{for $n>3$;}\end{cases}

1c)
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n))
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n;n))
;

1d)
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(n))
, then

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;n))
for
\lambda\neq 0,1
.

Proof.

Note that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is bigraded: each component
{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
is

{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded by powers of
\xi
(call it
\xi
-grading
) so
that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i\leq n-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0,i}
and

z\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0}
; we have
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{k\leq i\leq m}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
, where

k=\begin{cases}0,&\text{in cases 1b) -- 1d)},\\
1,&\text{in case 1a)};\end{cases}\quad\text{~~ and~~}m=\begin{cases}n,&\text{in cases 1a), 1d)},\\
n-1,&\text{in cases
1b), 1c).}\end{cases}

By Lemma
6.5.1.  Lemma
, if
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
, then the subspace

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}^{*}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
contains the components of degree from
-m
to
-k
with respect to the
\xi
-grading. In particular,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-m}\neq 0
.

But, in each case, we have a subspace
W=\mathop{\oplus}_{k\leq i\leq s}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
such that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
does
not contain operators commuting with the whole
W
. By Lemma

5.2.  Lemma
, this means that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,i}=0
for all
i<-1-s
,
i.e.
,
the necessary condition for
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
is
-m\geq-1-s
,
i.e.
,

m\leq 1+s
. Specifically,

(163)

1a)

s=2
,
m=n

\Longrightarrow

n\leq 3
;

1b)
,
1c)

s=1
,
m=n-1

\Longrightarrow

n\leq 3
;

1c)

s=1,m=n

\Longrightarrow

n\leq 2
.

In cases 1a), 1b) for
n=3
, we get the exceptional simple Lie superalgebras

{\mathfrak{v}}{\mathfrak{le}}
and
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
.

The case 1c) for
n=3
calls for subtler arguments. We have

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i=0,1,2}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
, where

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,0}={\mathbb{C}}\Pi(1),\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\xi_{j}\mid j=1,2,3)),\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}=\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\xi_{j}\xi_{l}\mid j,l=1,2,3)).\end{array}

Suppose
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
and let

F=\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})^{*}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
,
i.e.
,

[F,\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})]=z+h,\text{ where }h\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0},\text{
{i.e.}, }h=\mathop{\sum}\limits_{i,j}a_{ij}\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{j}}.

Then,
F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}
, and thus,
[F,\Pi(1)]=0
. By the Jacobi
identity,

0=[[F,\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})],\Pi(1)]=(z+h)\Pi(1)=-\Pi(1)+h(\Pi(1)).

Therefore,
h(\Pi(1))=\Pi(1)
and

(164)

\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits h=-\mathop{\sum}\limits_{1\leq i\leq 3}a_{ii}=1.

Since
p(\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}))={\bar{1}}
, we have

0=[[F,\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})],\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})]=(z+h)\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})=-\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})+h(\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})).

Hence,
h(\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}))=\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})
and

a_{11}+a_{22}+\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits h=1
or

(165)

a_{11}+a_{22}=0.

Clearly,
[F,\Pi(\xi_{3})]\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,-1}
,
i.e.
,

[F,\Pi(\xi_{3})]=\sum b_{j}\partial_{\xi_{j}}
.
By the Jacobi identity

\begin{array}[]{ll}0=&[[F,\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})],\Pi(\xi_{3})]-[\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}),[F,\Pi(\xi_{3})]]=\\
&-\Pi(\xi_{3})+h(\Pi(\xi_{3}))-(b_{1}\Pi(\xi_{2})-b_{2}\Pi(\xi_{1})).\end{array}

Hence,
a_{33}+\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits h=1
,
i.e.
,
a_{33}=0
. The last equality
contradicts conditions (
164
) and (
165
).
∎

2)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}={\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
for
n\geq 3

A rank-1
operator with even covector exists only for the
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-module

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\Pi(T^{0}_{0}(\vec{0}))
, hence,
{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n;n)
.

From Table (
264
) we know that
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
has no nontrivial
central extensions, but it has an outer derivation,
D=\sum\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\in{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|n)
. So we have to consider the
following cases:

(166)

2a)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
as
spaces and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}\otimes(-1)
as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module;

2b)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}D
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
as
spaces, where
D
acts as
\sum\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{i}}+\lambda\cdot 1
;

2c)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z)\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}D
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}

as spaces,

where
z
acts as multiplication by
-1
and
D

as
\sum\xi_{i}\partial_{\xi_{i}}
;

2d)

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\ltimes{\mathbb{C}}(az+bD)
for
a,b\in{\mathbb{C}}
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
.

Proposition

(Prolongations of relatives of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}={\mathfrak{svect}}(0|n)
)
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})
be one of the pairs
2a)
–
2c)
of cases
(
166
)
and

{\mathfrak{g}}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
its Cartan prolong. Then, in case
2b)
for

n=3
and
\lambda=0
, we have
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{le}}(3)
. In all other cases,

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
.

Proof.

In all cases,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{1\leq i\leq n-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
. By Lemma
6.5.1.  Lemma
, if
{\mathfrak{g}}\neq{\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-n+1}\neq 0
. On the other hand,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i\leq n-2}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}

contains no elements that commute with the subspace
W={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}
. Hence, by Lemma
5.2.  Lemma
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,s}=0
for
all
s
such that
s+2<-1
. Thus,
-n+1\geq-3
,
i.e.
,
n\leq 4
.

(Note that
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|3)\simeq{\mathfrak{spe}}(3)
, and therefore this case of
Proposition is a particular case of a case already considered.)

Let us examine the case where
n=4
more carefully. We have

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{1\leq i\leq 3}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
, where

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\Pi(\xi_{i}))_{i=1}^{4},\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\Pi(\xi_{i}\xi_{j}))_{i=1}^{4},\\
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,3}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3})\}.\end{array}

Suppose
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\neq{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
. In the cases 2a) – 2d) of table (
166
), there should exist
elements
F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-3}
and
\alpha z+\beta D\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
such that

g=[F,\Pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3})]=-\alpha\cdot 1+\beta D+h,

where
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\alpha\beta\neq 0

and
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,0}={\mathfrak{sl}}(4)
.

By grading considerations,
[F,{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}]=0
. Further, by the Jacobi
identity,

h|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}}=\alpha\cdot 1-\beta D|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,1}}=(\alpha-\beta)\cdot 1.

Since
h\in{\mathfrak{sl}}(4)
, it follows that
\alpha-\beta=0
and
h=0
.
Without loss of generality we can assume that
\alpha=\beta=1
. Then,

\mathop{\mathrm{ad}}\nolimits_{g}|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}}=-1+D|_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}}=1
. Hence,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0}={\mathfrak{gl}}(4)\simeq{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{o}}(6)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{o}}(6)}\otimes 1
as
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0}
-modules. Note that
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}]\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0,0}
. Set

V=\{F\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}\mid[F,{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,2}]=0\}.

Then, the quotient
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1,-2}/V
naturally embeds into the first
component of Cartan prolong
(\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits),{\mathfrak{c}}{\mathfrak{o}}(6))_{*}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}}(0|6))
.
Due to Lemma
6.5.1.  Lemma
this contradicts the assumption

{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\neq{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
.
∎

3)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|n)
for
n\geq 3

A
rank-1 operator with even covector exists only for the

{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-module

{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n))}{{\mathbb{C}}(b+\alpha\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(\xi)}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n))}{{\mathbb{C}}(b+\alpha\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.43057pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits(\xi)}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-1.65764pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n))}{{\mathbb{C}}(b+\alpha\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits(\xi)}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-1.65764pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits(0|n))}{{\mathbb{C}}(b+\alpha\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n})\mathop{\mathrm{{v\hskip-0.3014pto\hskip-0.06029ptl\/}}}\nolimits(\xi)}$}}
.
Hence, with
\mu=\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\alpha}{b}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{\alpha}{b}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\alpha}{b}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{\alpha}{b}$}}
, we have

(167)

{\mathfrak{h}}=\begin{cases}({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}=\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})&\text{for $n$ even,}\\
({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}=\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)&\text{for $n$ odd}.\\
\end{cases}

From Table (
264
) we know that
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
has no
nontrivial central extensions or outer derivations.

Proposition

(Prolongs of relatives of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|n)
)
.

For
{\mathfrak{h}}
as in formula (
167
), let

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}z
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
, as

{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-modules; let
z
act on
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
as
-\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
. Then,
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}={\mathfrak{d}}({\mathfrak{h}})
.

Proof.

The components
{\mathfrak{g}}_{k}
are filtered by powers of
\xi
.
Consider the corresponding bigraded Lie superalgebra
\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}
. If

{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\neq{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
, then, by Lemma
6.5.1.  Lemma
,

(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{1}=(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{h}})_{1}\oplus(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{-1}^{*}.

But
(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{-1}=\mathop{\oplus}_{0\leq i\leq 2n-1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
.
Hence,
(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{1,-2n+1}\neq 0
.

On the other hand,

(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{0}=\mathop{\oplus}_{-1\leq i\leq 2n-2}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{0,i}
and

\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
contains no elements that commute with the subspace

W=\mathop{\oplus}_{i=0,1}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{-1,i}
. Hence,
(\mathop{\mathrm{gr}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}})_{1,s}=0
for all
s
such that
s+1<-1
. Therefore,
-2n+1+1\geq-1
or

2n\leq 3
. This contradicts the fact that
n\geq 2
.
∎

4)
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)
for
n\geq 5
and related
subcases

As follows from the description of the irreducible representations
of
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|n)
, see
[
Sha1
,
Sha2
]
, no induced
representation has a rank-1 operator.

7.
Step 6. A particular case: case 3) of Corollary

4.2.1.  Corollary

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
or
\Pi(\Lambda(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}))
;
let
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\subset{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)\ltimes{\mathfrak{o}}(4)
, where
{\mathfrak{o}}(4)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2)
with the isomorphic copies of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
numbered for convenience:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)&=&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\partial_{1},\partial_{2}),\text{~~where $\partial_{i}:=\partial_{\xi_{i}}$},\\
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2)&=&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\xi_{1}\partial_{2},\xi_{2}\partial_{1},\xi_{1}\partial_{1}-\xi_{2}\partial_{2}),\\
{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2)&=&\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{1}\partial_{2},\xi_{1}\xi_{2},\xi_{1}\partial_{1}+\xi_{2}\partial_{2}-1).\end{array}

We realize the elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
by vector fields,

i.e.
, we embed
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
into
{\mathfrak{vect}}(2|2)
. Redenote
the respective vectors of the monomial basis of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(2))
:

\pi(\xi_{1})\longmapsto\partial_{x},\;\pi(\xi_{2})\longmapsto\partial_{y};\ \ \pi(1)\longmapsto\partial_{\eta},\;\pi(\xi_{1}\xi_{2})\longmapsto\partial_{\zeta}.

To the basis vectors from
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
we assign the following vector
fields of degree 0 that act in the space of functions in the indeterminates
x,y,\eta,\zeta
:

\footnotesize\begin{matrix}\begin{matrix}{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4):&-1&\longmapsto&x\partial_{x}+y\partial_{y}+\eta\partial_{\eta}+\zeta\partial_{\zeta},\\
&\xi_{1}&\longmapsto&\eta\partial_{x}-y\partial_{\zeta},\\
&\xi_{2}&\longmapsto&\eta\partial_{y}+x\partial_{\zeta},\\
&\partial_{1}&\longmapsto&\zeta\partial_{y}-x\partial_{\eta},\\
&\partial_{2}&\longmapsto&-\zeta\partial_{x}-y\partial_{\eta},\end{matrix}\\
\begin{matrix}{\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2):&\xi_{1}\partial_{2}&\longmapsto&-y\partial_{x},&\hskip 8.50012pt{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2):&\partial_{2}\partial_{1}&\longmapsto&-\zeta\partial_{\eta},\\
&\xi_{2}\partial_{1}&\longmapsto&-x\partial_{y},&&\xi_{1}\xi_{2}&\longmapsto&-\eta\partial_{\zeta},\\
&\xi_{1}\partial_{1}-\xi_{2}\partial_{2}&\longmapsto&y\partial_{y}-x\partial_{x},&&\xi_{1}\partial_{1}+\xi_{2}\partial_{2}-1&\longmapsto&-\eta\partial_{\eta}-\zeta\partial_{\zeta}.\end{matrix}\end{matrix}

We see that this realization of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is invariant under the
reversal of parity in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
. So, both
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Lambda(2)
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(2))
bring about the same result (isomorphic
algebras
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*})
.

Consider the following subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
:

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}&={\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)\ltimes{\mathfrak{sl}}_{1}(2)\\
&=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})\ltimes\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{1},\partial_{2},\xi_{1}\partial_{2},\xi_{2}\partial_{1},\xi_{1}\partial_{1}-\xi_{2}\partial_{2})\\
&\simeq(\Lambda(2)\setminus{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\xi_{1}\xi_{2})\ltimes{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|2).\end{array}

Consequently,

({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}(2;2).

Direct calculations show that

({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}\simeq({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}_{2}(2)
and
({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4))_{*}\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)
.

So a necessary condition for simplicity,
i.e.
,
[{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}]={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
, implies that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,a}(2;2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}(2;2)
.

8.
Step 7.
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings of vectorial Lie
superalgebras

8.1.
Weisfeiler regradings

Let

{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded
vectorial Lie superalgebra. By a (
Weisfeiler
)
regrading

of
{\mathfrak{g}}
we understand a transition from
{\mathfrak{g}}
to the Lie
superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-D}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
isomorphic
to
{\mathfrak{g}}
as abstract Lie superalgebra and such that

(168)

\begin{array}[]{ll}a)&\text{${\mathfrak{h}}$ is transitive, {i.e.}, for any non-zero $x\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{k}$ where $k\geq 0$, there is $y\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}$}\\
&\text{such that $[x,y]\neq 0$;}\\
b)&\text{${\mathfrak{h}}_{\geq 0}:=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{i}$ is
a~maximal
Lie subsuperalgebra of finite codimension;}\\
c)&\text{the ${\mathfrak{h}}_{0}$-module ${\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}$ is irreducible};\\
d)&\text{$D<\infty$, {i.e.}, ${\mathfrak{h}}$ is of finite depth.}\end{array}

In what follows in this section,
{\mathfrak{h}}
denotes a Lie superalgebra
obtained from
{\mathfrak{g}}
by a regrading. Clearly, not every two gradings
on the same algebra determine a bigrading; fortunately, for our purposes it suffices
to consider only
compatible gradings
for which (
169
)
is well defined thanks to the
hypothesis
:
all
{\mathbb{Z}}
-gradings of the Cartan prolongations are induced by the degrees of indeterminates
proved for
p>0
by Skryabin, see
[
Sk91
,
Sk95
]
. So, we consider only gradings induced by the degrees of the indeterminates.

The proofs of the next three Lemmas were first published in
[
Sh14
]
; since the article was not put in arXiv, let us reproduce the proofs here.

Lemma

(On the number of
homogeneous components of prolongs)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-D}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
be a W-regrading of
the Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{j\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
that
determines a bigrading:

(169)

{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}=\mathop{\oplus}\limits_{m(i)\leq j\leq M(i)}{\mathfrak{h}}_{i,j},\text{ where ${\mathfrak{h}}_{i,j}={\mathfrak{h}}_{i}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}$. }

Let
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-})=0|k
. Then, the number

M:=M(-1)-m(-1)+1
of the summands
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j}
in the decomposition
(
169
) of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
does not exceed the number of
homogeneous components with respect to the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of

\Lambda({\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-})
induced by the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
.

In particular, if
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}
is homogeneous with respect
to the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
for some
j
, then
M\leq k+1
.

Proof.

The component
V={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,M(-1)}
of the maximal degree is invariant with
respect to the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,\geq 0}:=\oplus_{j\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{0,j}
. Since the
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is irreducible, it must be a quotient of the induced module: as spaces,

\text{ind}^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}}_{{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,\geq 0}}V=\Lambda({\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-})\otimes V.\hskip 113.81102pt\qed

Lemma

(When
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is homogeneous)
.

If
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=0
, then

{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is homogeneous.

Proof.

The Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,0}
transforms
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j}
into itself and the operators in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,k}

send
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j}
into
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j+k}
. Therefore, if the representation of
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
on
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is irreducible, then
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is homogeneous with respect to the grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j_{0}}
for some
j_{0}
. But then
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,k}
for
k>0
sends
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
to
0
. Since
{\mathfrak{h}}
is transitive,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0,k}=0
for all
k>0
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0,0}
.∎

Lemma

(When the gradings coincide)
.

If
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=0
and
there exists a non-zero
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\cap{\mathfrak{h}}_{-}
, then the gradings
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}
coincide.

Proof.

Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{h}}_{-1,j_{0}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{0}}
, it follows that
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-k}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{k\cdot j_{0}}
. If
x\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{-k}
, then
-1=k\cdot j_{0}
, implying
k=1
and
j_{0}=-1
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
, and therefore
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}={\mathfrak{g}}_{i}
for all
i
.
∎

8.1.4.
How to describe all the W-regradings.

Given a W-graded Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, construct its W-regraded Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}
; namely, let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a Lie (super)algebra obtained from
{\mathfrak{g}}
by a regrading. First of all, we should list all the regradings that are not forbidden; our task is to select the ones that can be realized.

1) Determine
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
.

2) Construct a “minimal” (better, though informally, saying: “most
tightly compressed”) regrading with the given intersection,
i.e.
,
such that preserves in
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
all the elements of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}

except for those that are eliminated by the condition on the
intersection.

3) If the “minimal” regrading is of Weisfeiler type, then with the
help of Lemmas
8.1.2.  Lemma
and
8.1.3.  Lemma
we prove that any other
W-regrading with the given intersection coincides with the minimal
regrading.

4) If the “minimal” regrading is not of Weisfeiler type, then with
the help of Lemma
8.1.1.  Lemma
we prove that there are no
W-regradings with the given intersection.

Proof

of Theorem
2.15.1.  Theorem
. Let
1\leq r\leq m
, let
\varphi(r)=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{r}\}
be
a subset of distinct indices in
\{1,\dots,m\}
. Permuting, if necessary, the
indeterminates we can and will assume that

\varphi(r)=\{1,\dots,r\}
. Set
\deg\xi_{1}=\dots=\deg\xi_{r}=0

and define the remaining degrees as in Tables (
100
) and
(
99
). In Tables (
131
), (
132
), (
136
) and
(
125
), the terms
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
are indicated for
the regraded algebras
{\mathfrak{h}}
for the serial and the exceptional
algebras. Now, consider various cases.

1) Let either
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m)={\mathfrak{vect}}(x,\xi)
or

{\mathfrak{g}}:={\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m)={\mathfrak{svect}}(x,\xi)
. Then,

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}={\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}},\partial_{\xi_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\xi_{n}}).

Since the depth of

{\mathfrak{h}}
if finite,
\deg x_{i}>0
for all
i
. Hence,

\partial_{x_{i}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{-}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
for all
i
.

If
\deg\xi_{j}\neq 0
for all
j
, then
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=0
and
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}
by
Lemma
8.1.3.  Lemma
.

Let
\deg\xi_{i}=0
for
i\leq r
and
\deg\xi_{i}\not=0
for

i>r\geq 0
. Denote the thus regraded superalgebras by
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(n|m;\widetilde{\vec{r}})
or
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(n|m;\widetilde{\vec{r}})
.
Then,

\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{x_{1}},\dots,\partial_{x_{n}};\partial_{\xi_{j}}\mid j>r)\otimes\Lambda(\xi_{i}\mid i\leq r).

Let us show that

{\mathfrak{h}}\simeq\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Since
\partial_{x_{i}}\in\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
for all
i
, then by Lemma
8.1.3.  Lemma
it
suffices to check that
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\cap{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=0
. By definition of
{\mathfrak{h}}
,

\left(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{x_{i}},\partial_{\xi_{j}})\otimes 1\right)\cap{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=0\text{ for }j>r.

For
i<r
, we have
\partial_{\xi_{i}}\in{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
. Since
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
is
a subalgebra, the following implication holds:

(170)

if some element of
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
belongs to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
, then all the
elements of
\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{x_{i}},\partial_{\xi_{j}}\mid j>r)

belong to
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
.

Therefore,
\widetilde{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\cap{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=0
.

2) For the other series, take into account that the corresponding preserved (perhaps, conformally) form
(
\omega_{0}
,
\omega_{1}
,
\alpha_{0}
,
\alpha_{1}
,
\widetilde{\alpha_{1}}
, and so on) should be homogeneous.

For the series
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
{\mathfrak{k}}
, it is convenient to split the
indeterminates into dual pairs: the even ones,
p_{1},\dots,p_{m}

and
q_{1},\dots,q_{m}
and the odd ones:
\xi_{1},\dots,\xi_{k}
and

\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{k}
; the remaining odd coordinates are the self-dual

\theta
’s, see eq. (
63
). The corresponding modifications for contact series are obvious.

Observe that the cases of
{\mathfrak{k}}(1|2n;n)
and
{\mathfrak{m}}(n;n)
are obtained
from the general considerations given above by “compressing” the
grading to eliminate the zero components.

The case of the exceptional grading of
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2)
was
considered in Subsection
2.12.3
.
∎

8.1.5.
W-regradings of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6)
.

The W-regrading
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|11)
is the only one overlooked in
[
Sh14
]
. Though the description of all possible regradings of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6)
in

[
CK2
]
is correct, only an outline of the idea and the final result are
given and one W-regrading of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
was overlooked. Let us give a detailed proof of the completeness of the
list of regradings of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6)
.

There are two “least-codimension realizations” for
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6)
.
It is more convenient to describe regradings starting with the
realization where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{0}}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(5)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{{\bar{1}}}=\Pi(d\Omega^{1}(5))
, cf.
Subsection
2.17
.

Set
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{1},\dots,\partial_{5})
and

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{i}dx_{j}\mid 1\leq i<j\leq 5)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be a regrading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then, clearly,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-2}\subset{\mathfrak{h}}_{-}

and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
.

Since, for any permutation
\sigma\in S_{5}
, we have

[dx_{\sigma(1)}dx_{\sigma(2)},\ dx_{\sigma(3)}dx_{\sigma(4)}]=(-1)^{\mathop{\mathrm{sign}}\nolimits(\sigma)}\partial_{x_{\sigma(5)}},

it follows that
dx_{\sigma(1)}dx_{\sigma(2)}
and

dx_{\sigma(3)}dx_{\sigma(4)}
cannot simultaneously appear in

{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
after the regrading. Hence,
\dim{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\leq 4
.

So we have to consider the following cases:

1)
\dim{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=1
. In this case
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{1}dx_{2})
.

2)
\dim{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=2
. In this case
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{1}dx_{2},\ dx_{1}dx_{3})
.

3)
\dim{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=3
. There are the two subcases:

(171)

\begin{array}[]{ll}\text{3a)}&{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{1}dx_{2},\ dx_{1}dx_{3},\ dx_{1}dx_{4}),\\
\text{3b)}&{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{1}dx_{2},\ dx_{1}dx_{3},\ dx_{2}dx_{3}).\end{array}

4)
\dim{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}=4
. In this case,
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(dx_{1}dx_{2},\ dx_{1}dx_{3},\ dx_{1}dx_{4},\ dx_{1}dx_{5})
.

The standard arguments (either based on Lemma 7.1.3 from
[
Sh14
]
,
or see Subsection
8.1.4
) show that in the cases 2) and 3a)
{\mathfrak{h}}
is
not a W-regrading, whereas in case 1) we obtain
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|11)
,
in case 3b) we obtain
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(11|9)
, and in case 4) we obtain

{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}(9|6)
. The corresponding W-gradings listed in
[
CK2
]

are:

1)
\deg x_{1}=\deg x_{2}=3
,
\deg x_{3}=\deg x_{4}=\deg x_{5}=2
;
\deg d=-3
;

3b)
\deg x_{1}=\deg x_{2}=1
,
\deg x_{3}=\deg x_{4}=\deg x_{5}=2
;
\deg d=-2
;

4)
\deg x_{i}=1
for
i=1,\dots,4
,
\deg x_{5}=2
;

\deg d=-\frac{3}{2}
.

9.
The deformations

For a (more or less) conventional theory, see
[
BLW
,
BLLS
]
; for the subtleties that arise over fields of positive characteristic, see
[
BGL1
]
.
18
18

18

For Lie

super
algebras, there is a new type of (co)homology
— the divided power one, depending on a shearing vector —
introduced in
[
BGLL
]
.
If
p=2
, there are 2 types of (co)homology theories for Lie
algebras: with the usual (alternating) cocycles and of new type:
with non-alternating cocycles; accordingly, there are 6 types
of (co)homology theories for Lie superalgebras with non-zero odd part,
see
[
LZ
]
. See also
[
BGL1
]
with partial answers for
p=2
and
open problems
for
p=3
.

9.1.
The tools to compute (co)homology

There
are not many
general theorems which help one to calculate Lie algebra
(co)homology. There
are less than a half dozen general theorems plus the
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence, see
[
Fu
]
. Superization of
these theorems is immediate.

The simplest and most powerful theorem states that

(172)

“Any Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
trivially acts on its own cohomology

H^{i}({\mathfrak{g}};M)
and homology
H_{i}({\mathfrak{g}};M)
for any
i
.”

This theorem is particularly useful when
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded and contains a grading
operator. Most of the results on Lie algebra (co)homology are
obtained by scrutinizing the “zero mode space” of the grading
operator in the space of cycles of the (co)homology to compute.

Observe also that

(173)

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{gl}}(1))=\Lambda^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(c_{1})\text{~~for the element $c_{1}:=1^{*}\in({\mathfrak{gl}}(1))^{*}$};\\
H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{2})\simeq\mathop{\oplus}_{a+b=n}\ H^{a}({\mathfrak{g}}_{1})\otimes H^{b}({\mathfrak{g}}_{2}),\\
H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}};M_{1}\oplus M_{2})\simeq H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}};M_{1})\oplus H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}};M_{2}).\end{array}

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a finite-dimensional reductive Lie algebra, then
H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}};M)=0
for any
finite-dimensional irreducible module
M
and any
n
; the cohomology
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{g}})
with coefficients in the trivial module, are also known. The published
proofs known to us reduce the computation to compact forms of the
complex Lie groups with the given Lie algebra, but using the
Hochschild-Serre spectral sequence and induction on rank one can
easily obtain the answer algebraically; the base of induction being
{\mathfrak{gl}}(1)
and

{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
; for them, the computations can be easily performed by hand.

9.1.1.
(Co)homology with coefficients in the (co)induced
modules.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie superalgebra, let
{\mathfrak{h}}
be its subalgebra, let
M
be an
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module. We construct the induced
and coinduced
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules from
M
by setting

(174)

\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M):=U({\mathfrak{g}})\otimes_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}M,\quad\quad\mathop{\mathrm{Coind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M):=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}\nolimits_{U({\mathfrak{h}})}(U({\mathfrak{g}}),M).

Clearly,
\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M)
and
\mathop{\mathrm{Coind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M)
are
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules.

For any
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module
M
, we have (
[
Fu
]
)

(175)

H^{q}({\mathfrak{g}};\mathop{\mathrm{Coind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M))\simeq H^{q}({\mathfrak{h}};M),\quad\quad H_{q}({\mathfrak{g}};\mathop{\mathrm{Ind}}\nolimits^{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathfrak{h}}(M))\simeq H_{q}({\mathfrak{h}};M).

In particular, for any vectorial Lie superalgebra
{\mathcal{L}}
considered with a
Weisfeiler filtration, the
L_{0}
-module
M
such that
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}M=0
,
and the
{\mathcal{L}}
-module
T(M)
of tensor fields (see Subsection

2.10
), we have

(176)

H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathcal{L}};T(M))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle~\eqref{h2}}}{{\simeq}}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{0};M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{[Fu]}}}{{\simeq}}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}(L_{0})\otimes(H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\otimes M)^{L_{0}},

where
N^{L_{0}}
denotes the space of
{L_{0}}
-invariants of the
{L_{0}}
-module
N
.

For the proof of the second isomorphism in (
176
), see Theorem 2.2.8
(due to Losik) in Fuchs’ book
[
Fu
]
. It is given there for

{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(n|0)
only, but it can be directly translated to any Lie
(super)algebra
{\mathcal{L}}
of vector fields.
We use the
Hochshield-Serre spectral sequence for the algebra
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
and its
ideal
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
; according to
[
Fu
]
,
E_{\infty}=E_{2}
.

Anyway, we always obtain an estimate of
H^{i}({\mathcal{L}}_{0};M)
from above by
just looking at

(177)

\begin{array}[]{l}E_{2}^{p,q}:=H^{p}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}/{\mathcal{L}}_{1};H^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{1};M))=H^{p}(L_{0};(H^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{1};M))^{L_{0}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{since ${\mathcal{L}}_{1}M=0$}}}{{=}}\\
\phantom{XX}=H^{p}(L_{0})\otimes(H^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\otimes M)^{L_{0}}\text{~~for $p+q=i$}.\end{array}

Thus, the computation of
H^{2}({\mathcal{L}};\mathop{\mathrm{Coind}}\nolimits^{\mathcal{L}}_{L_{0}}(M))
is reduced to computation (description) of the following spaces:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(1)&\text{$H^{p}(L_{0})$ for $p=0,1,2$,}\\
(2)&\text{$L_{0}$-modules $H^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})$ for $q=2,1,0$, see \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{GLP}{}{}]},}\\
(3)&\text{$L_{0}$-invariants in the space $H^{q}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\otimes M$ for $q=2,1,0$.}\\
\end{array}

Computing cohomology of Lie
super
algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a more
involved business than computing cohomology of Lie algebras, even if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple, because (except for
{\mathfrak{osp}}(1|2n)
whose properties startlingly resemble those of
{\mathfrak{o}}(2n+1)
, as was first observed by Scheunert and expounded in
[
DLZ
]
) there
are many nontrivial irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules
M
for which

H^{n}({\mathfrak{g}};M)\neq 0
for various values of
n
. Therefore, if possible, we have
to reduce the computation to studying cohomology of the reductive
part of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\bar{0}}
.

9.2.
Cohomology of finite-dimensional classical Lie
superalgebras

For completeness and references, we recall the results of calculation of the cohomology of the finite-dimensional simple Lie superalgebras and their
“close” relatives. The known results with trivial
coefficients are announced in
[
FuLe
]
(for proofs in certain cases,
see
[
Fu
]
and
[
Kz
]
; for a summary with corrections of earlier claims, see
[
BoKN
]
). Nothing is known about cohomology with
nontrivial coefficients, except for the case where the module of coefficients is
“typical” and
{\mathfrak{g}}
admits an invariant non-degenerate
symmetric bilinear form (in which case the arguments from Lie
algebra theory are applicable).

(178)

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n))\simeq\begin{cases}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{gl}}(m))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\dots,\xi_{2m+1}],&\text{if $m\geq n$},\cr H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{gl}}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\dots,\xi_{2n+1}],&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{gl}}(n))\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[h_{2}],\;\text{ where
$h_{2}$ represents the extension}\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|n)\longrightarrow 0.\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))\simeq\begin{cases}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{3},\dots,\xi_{2m+1}],&\text{if
$m\geq n$},\cr H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{3},\dots,\xi_{2n+1}],&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}(n|n))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(n))\otimes{\mathbb{C}}[h_{2}]\text{ for $n>2$, where $h_{2}$ represents the extension}\\
0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{sl}}(n|n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}(n|n)\longrightarrow 0.\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}(2|2))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[y_{2},h_{2},x_{2},\xi_{3}]/(x_{2}y_{2}-h_{2}^{2}).\end{array}

Observe that the odd cocycle
\xi_{3}
in the bottom line of (
178
)
was missed in the original calculations of
[
FuLe
]
and the answer was
rectified when, at our request, A. Shapovalov used Grozman’s

SuperLie
(
[
Gr
]
), see also
[
Kor
]
. The
2-cocycles representing the central extensions in the 1st line of (
263
) are:

\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A_{1}&B_{1}\cr C_{1}&D_{1}\end{pmatrix}$},\text{\footnotesize$\begin{pmatrix}A_{2}&B_{2}\cr C_{2}&D_{2}\end{pmatrix}$}\longmapsto\begin{cases}y_{2}:&\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits C_{1}C_{2},\cr h_{2}:&\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits(B_{1}C_{2}+C_{1}B_{2}),\cr x_{2}:&\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits B_{1}B_{2}.\cr\end{cases}

Clearly, the
degrees
of the cocycles are:

\deg y_{2}=-2,\quad\deg h_{2}=0,\quad\deg x_{2}=2,

and therefore they are precisely the weights of the basis elements of
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
.

Having defined an
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
-structure on the space of these
cocycles, we cook from it, together with
{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}(2|2)
, the superalgebra
{\mathfrak{osp}}_{a}(4|2)
for
a=0
, see eq. (
265
) and

[
BGLL1
]
. The other results of
[
FuLe
]
we might need are as
follows:

(179)

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(1|1))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{1},\eta_{1}]/(\xi_{1}\eta_{1}).\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{pe}}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}[\xi_{1},\xi_{5},\dots,\xi_{4k+1\leq 2n+1};\xi_{2n-1}]\text{~~for $n>1$}.\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{spe}}(2n+3))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{pe}}(2n+3))\text{~~for $n\geq 0$}.\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{spe}}(2n+4))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{pe}}(2n+4))\otimes\begin{cases}{\mathbb{K}}[x_{n+2}],&\text{if $n\equiv 0\pmod{4}$},\cr{\mathbb{K}}[\xi_{n+2}],&\text{if
$n\equiv 2\pmod{4}$},\end{cases}\text{~~for $n\geq 0$}.\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{spe}}(2))\simeq{\mathbb{K}}[x_{1},y_{2}]/(x_{1}^{2}-y_{2}).\end{array}

In particular, only
{\mathfrak{spe}}(4)
has a non-trivial central extension, see Subsection
2.11
and
[
BGLL1
]
.

(180)

H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{osp}}(m|2n))\simeq\begin{cases}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{o}}(m)),&\text{for $m>2n$},\cr H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sp}}(2n)),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For cohomology of series
{\mathfrak{q}}
and its
relatives, see
[
BoKN
]
; we will not need it in this paper.

The cohomology of exceptional simple Lie superalgebras with trivial
coefficients are computed by a remarkably ingenious method due to
Gruson
[
Gr1
]
–
[
Gr3
]
:

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{g}}(2))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{g}}(2)),\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{a}}{\mathfrak{b}}(3))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{o}}(7)),\\
H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{osp}}_{a}(4|2))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{o}}(4))\simeq H^{\mathbin{\hbox{\raise 1.20552pt\hbox{\bf.}}}}({\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\oplus{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)).\end{array}

Gruson’s method has, however, limited applicability (at least, at the moment): sometimes we need various
nontrivial
coefficients in which case Gruson’s method does not work. To calculate (co)homology of the exceptional superalgebras by
hands is hardly possible, but fortunately, for every given algebra
and module, we have Grozman’s
SuperLie
code package, see

[
Gr
]
.

9.3.
Preliminary calculations

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a Lie
superalgebra and let

(181)

0\longrightarrow A\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial_{0}}}C\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial_{1}}}B\longrightarrow 0,\;\;\text{~~where~~}\;\;\;p(\partial_{0})=\bar{0}\;\;\text{ and }\;\;\;\partial_{1}\;\;\text{ is either even or odd},

be a short exact sequence of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules. Let
d
be the
differential in the standard cochain complex of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, cf.

[
Fu
]
.

Consider the following long cohomology sequence induced by the sequence (
181
):

(182)

\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial}}H^{i}({\mathfrak{g}};A)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial_{0}}}H^{i}({\mathfrak{g}};C)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial_{1}}}H^{i}({\mathfrak{g}};B)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial}}H^{i+1}({\mathfrak{g}};A)\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\longrightarrow}\limits^{\partial_{0}}}\dots

where the
\partial_{i}
are the differentials induced by the
namesake differentials in (
181
), and
{\partial=d\circ\partial_{1}^{-1}}
.

Since
\partial_{0}
and
\partial_{1}
commute
with
d
, the sequence (
181
) is well-defined and the same
arguments as for Lie algebras (
[
Fu
]
) demonstrate that the long
cohomology sequence (
182
) induced by (
181
) is exact
(
[
LPS
]
).

Let
\mathbbmss{1}
be the 1-dimensional
trivial
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module and let
\mathbbmss{1}[k]
be the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
trivial on the semi-simple part while the
distinguished central element acts on it by the scalar
k
. Then,

(183)

\begin{array}[]{ll}{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)=&\begin{cases}T(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)}))&\text{ as ${\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)$-module},\\
T(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)}))&\text{ as ${\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)$-module};\end{cases}\\
{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)=&\begin{cases}T(\mathbbmss{1}[-2])&\text{ as ${\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)$-module},\\
T(\mathbbmss{1})&\text{ as ${\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n)$-module};\end{cases}\\
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)=&T(\Pi(\mathbbmss{1}[-2]))\text{ as ${\mathfrak{m}}(n)$\penalty\hskip 0.0pt\hbox{-}\penalty\hskip 0.0pt{} and ${\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n)$-module};\\
{\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n)=&T(\mathbbmss{1})\text{ as an ${\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n)$-module;}\\
{\mathfrak{b}}(n)=&T(\Pi(\mathbbmss{1}))\text{ as a~${\mathfrak{le}}(n)$-, ${\mathfrak{sle}}(n)$-
and ${\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n)$-modules.}\end{array}

We also denote by
{\mathcal{F}}:=T(\mathbbmss{1})=T(\vec{0})
the space of
functions.

The isomorphism (
176
) yields

(184)

H^{2}({\mathcal{L}};T(M))=H^{2}(L_{0})\bigoplus(H^{1}(L_{0})\otimes(H^{1}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\otimes M)^{L_{0}})\bigoplus(H^{0}(L_{0})\otimes(H^{2}({\mathcal{L}}_{1})\otimes M)^{L_{0}}).

Denote for brevity:
{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
,
{\mathfrak{s}}:={\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
.
Thus, we see that

(185)

H^{i}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})\simeq H^{i}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)})=0\text{ for $i=1,2$ and $(m|n)\neq(1|1)$};

(186)

H^{i}({\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n);{\mathcal{F}})\simeq H^{i}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))=0\text{ for
}i=1,2.

So, from Subsection
9.1
, the above formulas
(
183
)—(
186
), and Section
9.2
we get (for
(m|n)
such
that
{\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n)
and
{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)
are simple)

(187)

H^{i}({\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n);{\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n))\simeq H^{i}({\mathfrak{osp}}(n|2m);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{osp}}(n|2m)})=0~\text{for
$i=1,2,3$};

(188)

H^{2}({\mathfrak{le}}(n);{\mathfrak{b}}(n))\simeq H^{2}({\mathfrak{pe}}(n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{pe}}(n)})=0\text{~~for
$n>1$};

(189)

H^{i}({\mathfrak{sle}}(n);{\mathfrak{sb}}(n))\simeq H^{i}({\mathfrak{spe}}(n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{spe}}(n)})=0~\text{for $i=1,2$ and $n>1$};

(190)

H^{2}({\mathfrak{po}}(2m|n);{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n))\simeq H^{2}({\mathfrak{osp}}(n|2m);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{osp}}(n|2m)})=0~\text{for $i=1,2$};

(191)

H^{i}({\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n);{\mathfrak{m}}(n))\simeq H^{i}({\mathfrak{spe}}_{a,b}(n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{spe}}_{a,b}(n)})=0\text{~~for
$n>1$ and $i=1,2$}.

Recall that for any (finite-dimensional) Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
and any irreducible
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module
M
, we have

(192)

H^{0}({\mathfrak{g}};M)\simeq\begin{cases}0&\text{if
$M$ is non-trivial},\\
{\mathbb{C}}&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Recall also that for any simple Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have

(193)

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{0}({\mathfrak{g}})={\mathbb{C}},\quad\quad H^{1}({\mathfrak{g}})\simeq({\mathfrak{g}}/[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}])^{*}=0.\end{array}

Now, let us apply the above preliminary calculations and Subsection
9.2
.

Theorem

(Rigidity of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras)
.

The simple infinite-dimensional serial vectorial Lie
superalgebras are rigid, except for
{\mathfrak{h}}:={\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n)
for
(m|n)\neq(1|2)
with
H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{h}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
,
{\mathfrak{le}}:={\mathfrak{le}}(n)
for
n\neq 2
with
H^{2}({\mathfrak{le}};{\mathfrak{le}})\simeq\Pi{\mathbb{C}}
, and

{\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n)
whose deformations, together with those of
{\mathfrak{h}}(2|2)
and
{\mathfrak{le}}(2)
, are listed in Theorem
2.12.1.  Theorem
and Section
10
. The exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras have no filtered deformations, see
[
GLS2
]
.

It is instructive to compare the above theorem with the description of deformations of Lie superalgebras and their desuperisations over fields of characteristic 2, see
[
BGLLS
]
.

Proof.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
, or
{\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n)
, or
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
. In these cases, we deduce from eq. (
184
) with the help of
[
GLP
]
, Subsection
9.2
, and
[
OV
, Table 5]
that
H^{2}({\mathfrak{g}};{\mathfrak{g}})=0
. Shmelev
[
Sm2
]
(resp., Kotchetkov
[
Ko1
]
) had already proved that
{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
(resp.,
{\mathfrak{m}}(n)
) are rigid by painstaking calculations (repeated
application of the Hochshield-Serre spectral sequence) at the time the results of
[
GLP
]
were not obtained yet.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
. Recall the abbreviations
{\mathfrak{v}}:={\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
,
{\mathfrak{s}}:={\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
.

1{}^{\circ}
:
m\neq 0,1
. The short exact sequence of

{\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n)
-modules

(194)

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{v}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{F}}\longrightarrow 0

gives rise to the long exact sequence

(195)

\begin{array}[]{l}\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

Note that, for any
(m|n)\neq(1|1),\ m+n>2
, we have

(196)

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})=H^{1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n))=0;\\
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})=H^{2}({\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(m|n)})=0.\end{array}

This and (
186
) imply that
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})=0
.

2{}^{\circ}
:
m=0
. In this case (considered in
[
Ld
]
) the divergence is not a surjective
mapping onto
{\mathcal{F}}
, its image is the space
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}(0|n)
of functions with
integral 0:

(197)

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{s}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{v}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}\longrightarrow 0.

In the associated long exact sequence

(198)

\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow\ldots

we have
H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})=0
and
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{v}})=0
by (
196
), so
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathfrak{s}})\simeq H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})
. To compute it, consider the short
exact sequence

(199)

0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow 0

which gives rise to the long exact sequence

(200)

\begin{array}[]{l}0\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

and since

\begin{array}[]{l}H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\simeq{\mathbb{C}},\quad H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})\simeq H^{0}({\mathfrak{sl}}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}};\quad H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};{\mathcal{F}})\simeq H^{1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(n))=0,\end{array}

it follows that
H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\simeq H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
. Since we
already know one global deformation, there are no other deformations.

3{}^{\circ}
:
m=1
,
i.e.
,
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)
for
n>1
. From the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\cdot\xi_{1}\cdots\xi_{n}\partial_{t}\longrightarrow 0

we derive the exact sequence (here
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n)
and

{\mathfrak{s}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(1|n)
)

(201)

\begin{array}[]{l}\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

Since
H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})=0
by (
193
) and
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})=0
, see Subsection
12.3.  Theorem
, we have
{H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\simeq H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})}
. To compute
H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})
we use the exact sequence (
197
) of
{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime}
-modules leading to the exact sequence

(202)

0\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{v}})\longrightarrow\ldots

Since
H^{0}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0})=0
by (
192
) and
H^{1}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{v}})\simeq H^{1}({\mathfrak{sl}}(n);\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{sl}}(n)})=0
, it follows that

H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime})\simeq H^{2}({\mathfrak{s}}^{\prime};{\mathfrak{s}})=0.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
.

1{}^{\circ}
:
\lambda
generic. From the short exact sequence of

{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
-modules

(203)

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{m}}(n)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits_{\lambda}}}{{\longrightarrow}}T(\vec{0})\longrightarrow 0,

where
\mathop{\text{\rm div}}\nolimits_{\lambda}
is given by formula (
110
), we derive
the long exact sequence

(204)

\begin{array}[]{l}\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n))\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{m}}(n))\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);T(\vec{0}))\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n))\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{m}}(n))\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

Observe that for
\lambda=\frac{2a}{n(a-b)}\in{\mathbb{C}}\cup\{\infty\}
,
we have

(205)

H^{1}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);T(\vec{0}))\simeq H^{1}({\mathfrak{spe}}_{a,b}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}.

Together with (
191
),
i.e.
, the fact that
H^{1}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{m}}(n))=0
and
H^{2}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{m}}(n))=0
, this implies that
H^{2}({\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n);{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n))\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
.

2{}^{\circ}
: the exceptional
\lambda=0
, 1,
\infty
(and extra exceptional values
\lambda=\frac{1}{2}
,
-\frac{3}{2}
if

n=2
), see §
10
.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)
. Set
{\mathfrak{po}}:={\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m)
,
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}(2n|m)
,
{\mathfrak{h}}:={\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)
, and
{\mathfrak{k}}:={\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)
.

1{}^{\circ}
: Generic case:
(n|m)\neq(1,2)
,
(n|m)\neq(0,m)
for

m>4
. From the short exact sequence of
{\mathfrak{g}}
-modules

(206)

0\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m)\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{k}}(2n+1|m)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\partial_{t}}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{F}}(2n|m)\longrightarrow 0

we derive the long exact sequence

(207)

\begin{array}[]{l}\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{po}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{k}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{po}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{k}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathcal{F}})\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

From (
190
) we have
H^{i}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{k}})=0
for
i=1,2
and

H^{1}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathcal{F}})\simeq H^{1}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{po}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
. Hence,
H^{2}({\mathfrak{po}};{\mathfrak{po}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
.

Remark

(Different cohomology for different function spaces)
.

The above is the shortest known to us proof of
the uniqueness of quantization of the Poisson Lie superalgebra
realized on the space of polynomials or formal series. Warning: for
other types of functions, say for Laurent polynomials or functions with compact support, the answer
is different, cf.
[
KT1
,
KT
]
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}:={\mathfrak{h}}(2n|m)
and
{\mathfrak{po}}:={\mathfrak{po}}(2n|m)
. From the exact sequence

(208)

0\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{po}}\longrightarrow{\mathfrak{h}}\longrightarrow 0

we derive the long exact sequence

(209)

\begin{array}[]{l}\ldots\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{po}})\longrightarrow H^{1}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{po}})\longrightarrow\\
\longrightarrow H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{3}({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{3}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{po}})\longrightarrow H^{3}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow H^{4}({\mathfrak{h}})\longrightarrow\ldots\end{array}

Let
\omega
be the form preserved by
{\mathfrak{h}}
, let
k=\lfloor\mathchoice{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{0.25pt}{$\dfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}{\raisebox{-0.5pt}{$\tfrac{m}{2}$}}\rfloor
and

1_{n+k}\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(n|k)={\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\subset{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=S^{2}(V)\oplus{\mathfrak{gl}}(V)\oplus S^{2}(V^{*})~~\text{for $m=2k$}

and similarly for
m=2k+1
. Note that
H^{3}({\mathfrak{h}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}[\omega_{0}^{*}\wedge 1_{n+k}^{*}]
, where

\omega_{0}^{*}\wedge 1_{n+k}^{*}=(\sum p_{i}^{*}\wedge q_{i}^{*}+\sum\xi_{j}^{*}\wedge\eta_{j}^{*})\wedge(\sum p_{i}q_{i}+\sum\xi_{j}\eta_{j})^{*}.

Then, from (
187
) we have
H^{i}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{po}})=H^{i}({\mathfrak{osp}};\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{osp}}})=0
for

i=1,2,3
, so
H^{2}({\mathfrak{h}};{\mathfrak{h}})\simeq{\mathbb{C}}
. Clearly, this deformation of
{\mathfrak{h}}
is the restriction of the quantization of
{\mathfrak{po}}
.

2{}^{\circ}
:
(2n|m)=(2|2)
. See Section
10
where this case is considered separately: the above argument fails,
because
{\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2)
:

(210)

H^{3}({\mathfrak{h}}(2|2);{\mathfrak{po}}(2|2))\simeq H^{3}({\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2))=H^{3}({\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2))\simeq H^{3}({\mathfrak{gl}}(2))={\mathbb{C}}.

3{}^{\circ}
:
(n|m)=(0|m)
for

m>4
, see
[
Tyut
]
. There is only one class of deformations. Clearly, this deformation of
{\mathfrak{h}}
is the restriction of the quantization of
{\mathfrak{po}}
.

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{le}}(n)
,
n>2
. Let
{\mathfrak{b}}:={\mathfrak{b}}(n)
and
{\mathfrak{le}}:={\mathfrak{le}}(n)
. From the exact sequence

(211)
