12.5.
Finite-dimensional irreducibles over simple vectorial
Lie superalgebras

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be either
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
, or its
subsuperalgebra
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
, or
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|m)
with their
standard filtrations.

Let us prepare a supply of finite-dimensional irreducible

{\mathcal{L}}
-modules. Take an irreducible
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}/{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
-module
M^{\chi}

with highest weight
\chi
; assume that the highest weight vector
(as well as the whole
M^{\chi}
) is even. Construct the induced

{\mathcal{L}}
-module by setting

I(\chi):=\mathop{\mathrm{ind}}\nolimits^{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{0}}(M^{\chi})=U({\mathcal{L}})\otimes_{U({\mathcal{L}}_{0})}M^{\chi}.

It turns out that, for
\chi
generic, the modules
I(\chi)
are
irreducible; the exceptional — reducible — modules line up into
a sequence which is exact for
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
and

\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|m)
and “almost exact” for
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
,
see
[
BL3
]
.

To formulate the answer graphically, let us pass from the induced
modules
I(\chi)
to their duals, the coinduced modules

T(\chi):=I(\chi^{*}\otimes(-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits))
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits
is the

1
-dimensional
{\mathfrak{gl}}(m)={\mathcal{L}}_{0}
-module determined by the trace also denoted by
\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits
,
and
(-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits)(X)=-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits(X)
for any
X\in{\mathfrak{gl}}(m)
.

Theorem

(Irreducibles over
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
,
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
and
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|m)
, after
[
BL3
]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
,
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
, or
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|m)
.
Then, the
{\mathcal{L}}
-modules
T(\chi)
are irreducible, except for the
cases where
M^{\chi}
is isomorphic to

S^{k}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits)
or
S^{k}(\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits^{*})\otimes(-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nolimits)
, where
\mathop{\mathrm{id}}\nolimits
is the
tautological representation of
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}/{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
. These exceptional
modules, denoted by
\Omega^{k}
and
\Sigma_{-k}
, respectively, are
interpreted as the spaces of
k
th degree differential forms and,
respectively,
(-k)
th degree integral
19
19

19

Having discovered
them, Bernstein and Leites called them
integraBle
since
they can be integrated. P. Deligne, however, calls them

integral
, cf.
[
Del
, Ch.3, §3.12]
; perhaps to rhyme with differential or after the incorrect (since the authors’ interpretation was lost) English translation of the Russian version of
[
BL1
]
.
forms on the
(0|m)
-dimensional
supermanifold. The exterior differential and the Berezin integral
unite the exceptional modules in one — infinite in both directions —
exact sequence:

\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{-k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega^{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\cdots,

and
therefore the kernels and the images of the above maps are the only
irreducible submodules of the exceptional modules if
{\mathcal{L}}
is of
series
{\mathfrak{vect}}
or
\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}
.

For
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
, since
\Sigma_{0}\simeq\Omega^{0}
, the module
of functions,
\Omega^{0}
, has the additional submodule
\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits_{0}
consisting of the functions with integral zero.

We see that, except for
m=2
(since

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{sl}}(1|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{osp}}(2|2)
, this case was already
considered), a rank-1 operator can occur only in the modules

T(\chi)
coinduced from the 1-dimensional module, or in a quotient
of such module, or in the modules obtained from such module by reversing their parity. The corresponding cases are listed in Table
(
157
). For example, the corresponding rank-1 operators from

{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
and
\widetilde{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
are

\xi_{1}\dots\xi_{m}\partial_{\xi_{i}}\;\text{ for any $i$ realized
in}\begin{cases}\text{${\mathcal{F}}:=\Lambda(\xi)$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(\xi)$},&\text{for $m$
even and $\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}$},\\
\text{$\Pi({\mathcal{F}}):=\Pi(\Lambda(\xi))$ or $\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{V\hskip-2.36806pto\hskip-0.08612ptl\/}}}\nolimits^{\lambda}(\xi))$},&\text{for $m$
odd and $\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}$}.\end{cases}

In the case of
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
, the operators
(\prod_{j\neq i,t}\xi_{j}){\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{i}}}}
acting on
{\mathcal{F}}:=\Lambda(\xi)
have rank 1.

Several of our exceptional vectorial superalgebras exist due to the
fact that adding the trivial center to the above-considered
{\mathcal{L}}
we
get, in certain small dimensions, new nontrivial Cartan prolongs. For details and such new (in 1996) examples, see
[
Sh14
]
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{h}}(0|m)
preserve the symplectic 2-form
\omega
. Then, the
exceptional modules are still realized in the spaces of
differential forms (since
\Sigma_{-i}\simeq\Omega^{i}
thanks to
nondegeneracy of
\omega
). The complete description of irreducible
modules over
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|m)
and all its relatives — central
extensions (
i.e.
, finite-dimensional Poisson Lie superalgebras) and
superalgebras of derivations is due to Shapovalov

[
Sha1
,
Sha2
]
. Except for the cases of small dimension where an
occasional isomorphism occurs (such as
{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|4)\simeq{\mathfrak{p}}{\mathfrak{sl}}(2|2)
),
and which, therefore, are already considered, there are no operators
of rank 1.

12.6.
On primitive Lie superalgebras

Recall that
a filtered Lie (super)algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
(or an associated graded
algebra) is called
primitive
if it has a maximal subalgebra of finite codimension which does not contain a homogeneous ideal of

{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Let

(266)

{\mathfrak{r}}\subset{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)\text{~~ be a~subalgebra such that
$\mathop{\mathrm{pr}}\nolimits_{-1}({\mathfrak{r}})={\mathfrak{vect}}_{-1}(\xi)$,}

where
\mathop{\mathrm{pr}}\nolimits_{-1}
is
the projection of
{\mathfrak{vect}}(\xi)
to

{\mathfrak{vect}}_{-1}(\xi)=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits({\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{1}}}},\dots,{\frac{\partial}{\partial{\xi_{n}}}})
.

Let
{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq-d}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
be
a
primitive
Lie superalgebra (with

\mathop{\oplus}_{i\geq 0}\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{i}
being its maximal
subalgebra) such that
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
is an irreducible
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
-module
and
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-}
is generated by
{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}
. Set:

(267)

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{g}}_{min}={\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda(n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{r}},\ \ \ {\mathfrak{g}}_{max}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}\otimes\Lambda(n),\ {\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\otimes\Lambda(n)\ltimes{\mathfrak{r}})_{*},\end{array}

where
(V,{\mathfrak{g}})_{*}
is the Cartan prolong of
(V,{\mathfrak{g}})
. It is not
difficult to see that any Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}
such that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{min}\subseteq{\mathfrak{p}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}_{max}
, is primitive.

Conjecture

(On primitive Lie superalgebras)
.

Every primitive Lie superalgebra
{\mathfrak{p}}
is of the form
{\mathfrak{g}}_{min}
for
a suitable
{\mathfrak{h}}
.

Regardless of whether this Conjecture is true or not, “describe primitive Lie
superalgebras” is a wild problem because there are indescribably many Lie superalgebras
satisfying condition (
266
).

12.8.
On the Lie superalgebra
E(2,2)
from
[
K3
]

While this paper was being written and
[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
refereed, there
appeared a proof of our classification in the case of compatible grading, see
[
Klc
]
. In it,
V. Kac states, among other things, that there exist six
exceptional simple vectorial Lie
superalgebras. In addition to the five (considered as abstract Lie
superalgebras) exceptions already described in
[
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
, Kac
distinguished one more “exception”,
E(2,2)
. Actually, it belongs to a series.

Lemma
.

E(2,2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}_{\infty}(2;2)
.

Proof.

Indeed, by definition,
E(2,2)
is the Cartan prolong

{\mathfrak{g}}=({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{g}}_{0})_{*}
, where “the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
-module
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}

gives rise to the linear Lie superalgebra
\text{spin}_{4}
”. This
means that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
is the semidirect sum

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}={\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)\ltimes{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
is the space of the
unique irreducible representation of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)
for which the
central element corresponds to the identity operator. Hence, we may
identify
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
either with
\Lambda(2)=\Lambda(\xi_{1},\xi_{2})
or
(due to uniqueness) with

\Pi(\Lambda(2))=\Pi(\Lambda(\xi_{1},\xi_{2}))
. The second scenario is
more convenient for our purposes.

The action of
{\mathfrak{hei}}(0|4)
is given by the operators
(1,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\partial_{\xi_{1}},\partial_{\xi_{2}})
, while
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
acts via the
tautological representation on the even component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
and
via the trivial representation on the odd component of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}
.
Now, what is
{\mathfrak{b}}^{\prime}_{\infty}(2;2)
or,
in Kac’s notation, “
SKO(2,3;1)
with subprincipal grading”? It is the Cartan prolong

{\mathfrak{h}}=({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1},{\mathfrak{h}}_{0})_{*}
, where

\begin{array}[]{l}{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}=\Pi(\Lambda(2))=\Pi(\Lambda(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}));\\
{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}=\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\ltimes{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|2)\simeq\left(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\ltimes\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\partial_{\eta_{1}},\partial_{\eta_{2}})\right)\ltimes{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)\simeq{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}.\end{array}

On
\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(\eta_{1},\eta_{2}))
,
i.e.
, on
({\mathfrak{h}}_{-1})_{\bar{0}}
, the subalgebra
{\mathfrak{sl}}(2)
acts via
the tautological representation, and on

\Pi(\mathop{\mathrm{{Span}}}\nolimits(1,\eta_{1}\eta_{2}))
,
i.e.
, on

({\mathfrak{g}}_{-1})_{\bar{1}}
, it acts via the trivial representation.

We get an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}_{-1}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{0}
, and hence an isomorphism
{\mathfrak{g}}\simeq{\mathfrak{h}}
. ∎

In the paper
[
CK2
]
which corrects several statements of

[
K3
]
, the result of this lemma is briefly stated, but the
description of
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
in
[
CK2
]
should still be corrected. The
paper
[
CCK
]
, in its turn, corrects
[
CK2
]
, but not completely: see eq. (
261
).

13.
On history

Lie superalgebras first appeared in topology, in the 1940s, over finite fields, in (co)homology theory. The collection of elements of homotopy groups with the Whitehead multiplication is an example of the Lie superring. These examples drew so little attention of researchers that the difference between “Lie superalgebras” and “graded Lie algebras” was clearly formulated only after
[
WZ
]
introduced super terminology.

Since the 1960s, Berezin tried to describe Bose and Fermi particles in a parallel manner. He conjectured that to do so one has to consider anticommuting indeterminates on equal footing with commuting coordinates. To see that the classical differential and algebraic geometries are particular cases of a wider area of mathematics, one had to define the object on which these indeterminates play the role of functions. This problem (proof of Berezin’s conjecture) was solved in
[
L0
]
, where what is now called
superscheme
was defined. For further mathematical development in smooth and analytic settings, see
[
B3
,
LSoS
,
MaG
,
Ld
]
. The vital inspirational role of Berezin in several areas of mathematics and physics is described in
[
Shi
,
N
,
KNV
,
KMLT
]
.

Meanwhile physicists arrived at the new fundamental philosophical paradigm transcending the limits of pure physics: “
We live on a supermanifold
”. This idea is still being developed; it became contagious thanks to a work by Wess and Zumino
[
WZ
]
where the term “SUSY” was introduced in the currently accepted sense, different from the now abandoned Wheeler’s usage of it, see
[
Whe
]
. In
[
WZ
]
, perspectives of
supersymmetry
were outlined more clearly than in several pioneering works on the subject, see introductions in
[
DSB
]
, and also
[
Del
,
Shi
]
written decades after
[
WZ
]
was published. Note that applications of SUSY to high energy physics are still conjectural, whereas results in solid state physics are already handable, see
[
Ef
]
.

Simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
. After
[
WZ
]
was published, the perspectives in theoretical physics and manifest mathematical beauty elucidating certain classical facts encouraged several researchers to tackle the classification of simpleLie superalgebras. Various

particular
types (with non-degenerate even
form, with reductive even part) were classified by Djoković (with
Hochschild)
[
DH
,
Dj1
,
Dj2
,
Dj3
]
, Scheunert, Nahm, and Rittenberg
[
SNR
]
,
I. Kaplansky (
[
FrK
]
,
[
Kap
,
Kapp
]
). Note that there was no understanding of Lie superalgebras as algebras of operators until mid-1970s when
{\mathfrak{gl}}(m|n)
was defined (
[
Lber
,
Kapp
,
SNR
]
) and the space of superderivations of the Grassmann algebra was interpreted as a (vectorial) Lie superalgebra.
In the talk
[
K0
]
, see Kac’s preface to
[
DSB
]
, V. Kac gave the first examples of (relatives of) simple finite-dimensional vectorial Lie
superalgebras.
Kac called them “some algebras”; we now
identify them as
{\mathfrak{vect}}(0|m)
,
{\mathfrak{svect}}(0|m)
and
{\mathfrak{h}}(0|m)
.
Several years later, Kac considered
all
types of simple finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras. Kaplansky’s preprints-1975
[
Kapp
]
helped Kac to repair gaps in his
classification (
[
K1
]
,
[
K1C
]
). Various types of simple Lie
superalgebras have to be treated separately, so it is
instructive to compare different techniques of
[
SNR
]
and

[
FrK
,
Kapp
,
Kap
]
, with
[
K2
]
, where earlier partial
results, later disregarded, are cited. For a 2-page-long summary of Kac’s ideas (
[
K1.5
,
K2
]
), see
[
Ld
, §8]
.
None of these researchers considered deformations with odd
parameters classified, at last, in
[
Ld
]
.

Lie superalgebras with Cartan matrices
. Kac was the first to attribute analogs of Cartan matrices to certain Lie
superalgebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
, and to observe that one Lie superalgebra can
have several inequivalent Cartan matrices. The definition of Cartan matrices and analogs of Dynkin diagrams was later improved in
[
CCLL
]
. For the classification of finite-dimensional Lie superalgebras with indecomposable Cartan matrix in characteristic
p>0
, see
[
BGL
]
.

Over
{\mathbb{C}}
, the classification of all Cartan matrices of a given Lie superalgebra
is due to Serganova, see
[
Se2
,
HS
]
. Lebedev extended Sergnova’s method to the case of characteristic
p>0
, see
[
Leb
]
, where this place in
[
BGL
]
is corrected. For the classification of Lie superalgebras of GLAPG type with indecomposable Cartan matrix, see
[
Se2
,
vdL
,
HS
]
.
For the classification of hyperbolic Lie algebras and their superizations, see
[
CCLL
]
.

Simple loop Lie superalgebras
. For their conjectural list, see
[
LSS
]
. To prove the completeness of this list à la
[
M
]
is an
Open problem
; for a partial result, see
[
HS
]
; for Cartan matrices of loop superalgebras, see
[
BLS
]
.

Stringy Lie superalgebras
. For the classification of simple stringy Lie superalgebras (with Laurent coefficients, based on the classification of vectorial, i.e. with polynomial coefficients), see
[
GLS1
]
; for the classification of their central extensions, see
[
KvdL
]
.
Note that there is a mysterious difference between presentations of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}

generated by polynomials and those of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{L}
generated by Laurent
polynomials as the superscript indicates, see
[
GLS4
]
: we
conjecture
that there are infinitely
many relations between “natural” (from a certain point of view) generators of
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}
, whereas
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{as}}^{L}
is finitely presented.

Vectorial Lie superalgebras
. It took a while to distinguish a reasonable classification problem.
A classification of
primitive
Lie superalgebras was claimed in

[
K1.5
,
K2
]
, but was obviously wrong; Theorem 10 and even Conjecture 1 in
[
K2
]
dealing with
simple
Lie superalgebras, were also wrong. After
[
L1
]
appeared, it became clear that the
list of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras “doubles”
Cartan’s list at least twice: by superdimension and by the “odd” counterparts such as
{\mathfrak{le}}
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
, and
{\mathfrak{m}}
of

{\mathfrak{k}}
, together with
{\mathfrak{sle}}
and
{\mathfrak{sm}}
. Leites dubbed the new algebras by the next letters after
{\mathfrak{k}}
(
{\mathfrak{l}}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}
); soon
{\mathfrak{l}}
was changed to
{\mathfrak{le}}
to eliminate confusions of
{\mathfrak{sle}}
with
{\mathfrak{sl}}
.
Grozman found
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
in 1978, see
[
G
]
, but was not understood;
{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n)
was rediscovered in
[
ALSh
]
.

In
[
ALSh
]
, we claimed that
one vectorial Lie superalgebra has
finitely many distinct
non-isomorphic

realizations as a
W-filtered or W-graded
Lie
superalgebra
; for a proof, see Subsection
8.1.4
. This phenomenon was totally missed in
[
K2
]
, and in
Kac’s later papers it was stated
that to describe all filtered vectorial superalgebras is “probably impossible”. Over fields of positive characteristic, the non-standard gradings were appreciated at approximately the same time as in
[
ALSh
]
, but not as much as they deserve, see
[
KLS
]
.

Observe that, as early as in
[
ALSh
]
, we wrote that the problem “classify
primitive Lie superalgebras” is wild, cf.
[
K2
]
; for details, see Subsection

12.6
. In 1988, we had almost all examples of simple W-graded vectorial Lie
superalgebras over
{\mathbb{C}}
(see
[
L1
,
ALSh
,
Sh3
,
LSh1
]
). The adjective “exceptional”, first used in

[
Sh3
]
, implies a claim for classification of “serial”
examples. Shchepochkina discovered all the exceptional examples

[
Sh3
,
Sh5
,
Sh14
]
.

In 1996–97, we have explicitly announced the
classification of W-graded simple complex vectorial Lie
superalgebras at various conferences,
e.g.
, at a seminar of E. Ivanov, JINR, Dubna (July, 1996), Voronezh Winter
School (Jan. 12–18, 1997), and at the conference in honor of D. Buchsbaum,
Northeastern Univ., Boston (October 1997), see
[
LSh2′
]
, as an aside remark
to another,
still open
, problem “how to superize representations of quivers?”, cf.
[
LSh2
,
BZ
]
. The proof
of classification of simple W-graded Lie superalgebras, already outlined in
[
LSh1
]
, was completed by that time,
except for W-gradings compatible with parity
. Getting our confirmation that the classification is announced (November 1996),
Kac, sometimes together with Cheng, considered the case of compatible
W-gradings (
[
CK
]
) complementing our proof, and published partial results
on filtered deformations
[
CK1
,
CK1a
,
CK2
,
Klc
,
K2
,
Kcf
,
Kga
,
Kbj
]
.
Cantarini helped Cheng and Kac with corrections, cf.
[
CCK
]
with §
11
and Subsection
12.8
.

The papers
[
Sh14
]
and
[
Sh5
]
were being refereed for about two years each.
When the details of the talk
[
Klc
]
were published in
[
K3
]
(accepted 42 days after submission) we saw, to
our amazement, that Kac’s method is very similar to ours, published and
lectured about. However, Kac
considered
the case of compatible gradings
we left out. The crucial contribution of
[
K3
]
to our approach is the proof of existence and description of a certain remarkable grading of every simple vectorial Lie superalgebra. These particular gradings help to prove the completeness of our list of W-graded simple vectorial Lie superalgebras (conjectural for compatible gradings).

In this article, we classified deformations of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras.

Related results
1) The description of the irreducible continuous representations of simple
vectorial Lie superalgebras was started in
[
BL3
]
for the
general series
{\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n)
in its standard realization following
the pioneering works by A. Rudakov (
[
R1
]
,
[
R2
]
). For
a review of results on representations for other series of vectorial
Lie superalgebras obtained by 2000, see
[
GLS3
]
. Some of these
results can explain and simplify proof of the results of

[
BKL1
]
.

For a description of irreducible representations of 11 of the 15
exceptional simple vectorial Lie superalgebras, and the
related description of invariant differential operators, see

[
GLS3
]
. In
[
KR1
,
KR2
,
KR3
]
, the representations of 3 of the 15
exceptions are further studied; to explain the discrepancies between answers in
[
GLS3
]
and in
[
CaCK
,
R3
,
CaCK1
]
is an
open problem
.

2) For the classification of simple vectorial Lie superalgebras over

{\mathbb{R}}
, see the original and unexpected result
[
CaKa1
]
. Serganova classified real and quaternionic forms of finite-dimensional simple Lie superalgebras, see
[
Se2
]
.

3) For a conjectural list of simple stringy (resp., loop) Lie superalgebras over

{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
GLS1
]
(resp.,
[
LSS
]
). Serganova classified real forms of loop and stringy superalgebras, see
[
Se3
]
; to classify their quaternionic forms remains an
open problem
.

4) For the classification of simple finite-dimensional Jordan superalgebras over

{\mathbb{C}}
, see
[
Kj
,
HK
]
. For the same approach in the infinite-dimensional case, see
[
Lint
]
and references in it.

13.1.
Comparison of notation

Strangely, not all of the
results from
[
ALSh
]
or
[
Ko1
,
LSh3
]
are reproduced in
[
K3
]
.
Cheng and Kac considered the parameter
\vec{r}
only for exceptional Lie
superalgebras. In this way, they missed 34 types of W-gradings, and most of the occasional isomorphisms, cf. Subsection
1.10.2
; confusing notation obscured “drop-outs”
listed in Subsubsection
1.7.1
. Kac and his co-authors often changed, probably for non-mathematical reasons, the notation of simple Lie superalgebras previously discovered by other researchers; some of Kac’s notation confused finite-dimensional Lie (super)algebras with their root systems.

We concede that our notation for exceptional simple W-filtered vectorial
superalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}
is rather long, though it reflects the way they are constructed and the
geometry preserved (
{\mathfrak{k}}{\mathfrak{le}}

is a subalgebra of

{\mathfrak{k}}
related to
{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{le}}
, and so on). But to write

{\mathfrak{e}}(\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits({\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0})^{*})
, as we once suggested, is to create confusion:
\mathop{\mathrm{sdim}}\nolimits({\mathcal{L}}/{\mathcal{L}}_{0})^{*}
can coincide for non-isomorphic superalgebras
{\mathcal{L}}
.

The half-page-long review Zbl 0929.17026 of
[
K3
]
is succinct and (unlike MR99m:17006)
void of emotions: ‘‘The main result of the paper is a complete classification of simple infinite-dimensional linearly compact Lie superalgebras. The list of these superalgebras contains 8 series of completed
{\mathbb{Z}}
-graded Lie superalgebras, 2 series of filtered deformations, and 6 exceptional Lie superalgebras.” However, the correct answer is quite different, see statements in Subsections
1.10
and
12.8
.
In their papers, Cantarini, Cheng, and Kac considered only
a
part
of problems A to C.

(268)

\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{tabular}[]{|l|l|}\hline\cr our notation&Kac's notation\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{vect}}(m|n;r)$&$W(m|n)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{svect}}(m|n;r)$&$S(m|n)$\cr${\mathfrak{svect}}^{\prime}(1|n;r)$&$S(1|n)$\cr$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{svect}}}(0|m)$, any $m\geq 3$&$\widetilde{S}(0|n)$ for $n\geq 4$ even\cr\hline\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{k}}(2m+1|n;r)$&$K(2m+1|n)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{h}}(2m|n;r)$&$H(2m|n)$\cr${\mathfrak{h}}_{\lambda}(2|2)\simeq{\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(2;2)$¬ singled out\cr${\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime}(0|n)$&$H(0|n)$\cr\hline\cr$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}-1|2^{n-1})$&appears as a~filtered\\
for $n$ even&deform; not recognized\\
&as ${\mathbb{Z}}$-graded\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{tabular}[]{|l|l|}\hline\cr our notation&Kac's notation\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{m}}(n;r)$&$KO(n|n+1)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{sm}}(n;r)$&$SKO(n|n+1)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{b}}_{\lambda}(n;r)={\mathfrak{b}}_{a,b}(n;r)$&$SKO(n|n+1;\beta)$ for\cr for $\lambda:=\frac{2a}{n(a-b)}\in{\mathbb{C}}{\mathbb{P}}^{1}$&$\beta(=\frac{b}{a}=1-\frac{2}{n\lambda})\in{\mathbb{C}}$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{b}}_{1}^{\prime}(n;r)$&$SKO(n|n+1;\frac{n-2}{n})$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{b}}_{\infty}^{\prime}(n;r)$&$SKO(n|n+1;1)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{le}}(n;r)$&$HO(n|n)$\cr\hline\cr${\mathfrak{sle}}^{\prime}(n;r)$&$SHO(n|n)$\cr\hline\cr$\widetilde{{\mathfrak{s}}{\mathfrak{b}}}_{\mu}(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1}-1)$¬ considered\cr for $n$ odd&\\
\hline\cr singular deformations¬ considered\\
of ${\mathfrak{b}}_{\mu}(n)$&\cr\hline\cr\end{tabular}\end{array}
